# A2. Executing Entity information

## Presentation of the International Fund for Agricultural Development (Executing Entity)
The IFAD mandate is to ensure that smallholder producers become reliable partners in the context of sustainable development and poverty reduction. IFAD works with governments in their efforts to de-risk agriculture, particularly risks related to climate. In the selected countries, IFAD has been working on a number of fronts: (i) building resilience against climate change; (ii) improving agricultural markets (climate, diseases, prices, policy and political) and transaction costs in private investments in order to increase productivity and profitability for smallholder farmers; and (iii) increasing access to finance. The latter is the focus of this proposal. IFAD has been working with governments on a number of fronts: (i) building resilience against climate change; (ii) improving agricultural markets (climate, diseases, prices, policy and political) and transaction costs in private investments in order to increase productivity and profitability for smallholder farmers; and (iii) increasing access to finance. The latter is the focus of this proposal.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 4 OF 89

## Presentation of the World Food Programme (Executive Entity)

The World Food Programme (WFP) is a global leader in providing worldwide support on national, local and regional food security and nutrition plans and programmes. WFP works with governments, civil society and the private sector to enable sustainable solutions for hunger and malnutrition. WFP has a role both in helping governments and communities prepare and respond to shocks, as well as in reducing vulnerability over time.

The project is designed to reduce the risk of disaster; build resilience and help people adapt to climate change. In the last decade alone, WFP has helped 10 million people affected by climate-related disasters and shocks. Over this period, WFP has been working with its partners to scale up climate-resilient food systems that are more secure. WFP will be one of the Executing Entities of this programme to which it will bring its expertise in climate risk management, disaster preparedness and response, as well as its experience in providing global food security aid. As EEE, WFP will be responsible for the implementation of Output 3.1 (component 3).

## Presentation of the selected countries (National Executive Entities)

- The Republic of Burundi, acting through its Ministry of Economy and Finance and its Ministry of Agriculture.
- The Republic of Chad, acting through its Ministry of Finance and Budget and its Ministry of Production.
- The Republic of Comoros, acting through its Ministry of Finance and its Ministry of Agriculture.
- The Republic of Mauritania, acting through its Ministry of Finance and its Ministry of Agriculture.
- The Republic of Niger, acting through its Ministry of Economy and Planning and its Ministry of Agriculture.
- The Republic of Senegal, acting through its Ministry of Economy, Planning and International Cooperation and its Ministry of Agriculture.
- The Republic of the Gambia, acting through its Ministry of Economic Affairs and its Ministry of Agriculture.

## The National Executive Entities: The Accredited Entity will enter into the relevant Subsidiary Agreements with each National Executive Entity, as well as with the respective Ministry of Economy and Finance. Each National Executive Entity, acting through its Ministry of Agriculture, will enter into a Subsidiary Agreement with Output 2.1 and all Activities under Output 2.2 of the Programme in the respective Host Country. At the World Food Programme (WFP) level, each National Executive Entity will also enter into legal agreements (Conventions/Agreements) with the relevant Ministry of Agriculture or its relevant sectorial ministry (as described above), well as into legal agreements (Conventions/Agreements) with the following entities: (i) The Agence Nationale de l’Aviation Civile et la Météorologie Nationale de Madagascar; (ii) The Agence Nationale de l’Aviation Civile, la Météorologie Nationale et la Radio National de la Météorologie in Mauritius; (iii) The Agence Nationale de l’Aviation Civile et la Météorologie Nationale de Niger and Niger; and (iv) The Agence Nationale de l’Aviation Civile, la Météorologie Nationale et la Radio National de la Météorologie in Chad. Under such agreements, the respective National Executive Entity will be acting as a Subsidiary Agent for Output 1.1, which are to procure, install and maintain the climate information infrastructures. A designated entity (as described above) of each National Executive Entity will also enter into legal agreements with support to the recipient countries for the implementation of each of these Activities. Each National Executive Entity will also enter into Subsidiary Agreements with the respective PMAs (as defined below) of baseline activities under Outputs 1.1 and 1.2 under Output 1.1 at Activities under Output 2.1 and all Activities under Output 2.2.

# A21. Executive Summary (max: 780 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

## Over 500 million smallholder farmers produce more than 80 percent of the world's food. These farms and the 750 million extremely poor people working on agriculture (mostly as smallholder family farmers) are vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Agriculture is a sector that is highly sensitive to changes in temperature, rainfall patterns, and extreme weather events. The impacts of climate change for youth, food security, nutrition and poverty reduction, has always been a major policy concern. The COVID-19 pandemic has made this even more urgent than ever before.

## In the Sahel region, the agricultural sector plays a key role in the economy (85 percent of rural GDP) and employs approximately two-thirds of the workforce in most countries. Predominantly subsistence-based and highly reliant on rainwater, the region is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The region has experienced an increase in extreme weather events as well as the impacts of one of the main anthropogenic drivers of climate change: global warming. Temperatures across the region and across the Great Green Wall (GGW) have increased by 1°C since the mid-20th century, with some areas experiencing increases up to 3°C. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and storms are increasing in the region (e.g., groundswell) and tend to be sensitive to high temperatures. Temperatures increase higher than 2°C can be projected to lead to a significant reduction in crop yields. In addition, climate change is also affecting water availability by reducing rainfall, decreasing moisture and surface water supplies, affecting irrigation systems, harming human and animal health, and in the impacts of droughts on food security. The region is also vulnerable to desertification, coastal erosion, coastal communities (Mauritania, Senegal and the Gambia) as well as the threat of sea level rise and acidification.

## While there are many uncertainties in the projections for future precipitation levels in the Sahel, partly due to the lack of robust climate models that can be used at a sub-regional scale, it is clear that rainfall will become more erratic. The region has experienced an increase in extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves and storms, which are projected to continue to increase with climate change. Rainfall levels have been decreasing since the 1980s and this trend is expected to continue into the future. In addition, there will be a decrease in the region in the 1980s and beyond, especially Central Sahel, is currently facing its fourth major drought-related emergency in less than two decades. The region has experienced an increase in extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves and storms, which are projected to continue to increase with climate change.

## In the past 25 years, the region has experienced a decrease in crop yields of up to 30 percent due to increased temperatures and reduced rainfall. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves and storms have also increased. This is expected to continue into the future.

## Changes in land use are another primary anthropogenic driver of climate change in the region. The Sahel has been classified as one of the most degraded regions in the world due to unsustainable farming practices (slash-and-burn agriculture, deforestation, overgrazing and desertification). These practices have led to a decrease in soil fertility, reduced water availability, increased population growth and massive migration flows and continued with a general lack of access to electricity in rural areas also puts pressure on natural resources. The region has experienced an increase in extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves and storms which are projected to continue into the future.

## In addition, climate change is also affecting water availability by reducing rainfall, decreasing moisture and surface water supplies, affecting irrigation systems, harming human and animal health, and in the impacts of droughts on food security. The region is also vulnerable to desertification, coastal erosion, coastal communities (Mauritania, Senegal and the Gambia) as well as the threat of sea level rise and acidification.

## More than half of the population in these Sahelian countries (or 163 million people) depend mainly on rain-fed agriculture for power generation. The region is also vulnerable to desertification, coastal erosion, coastal communities (Mauritania, Senegal and the Gambia) as well as the threat of sea level rise and acidification.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 6 OF 89

## Climate change interacts with non-climate drivers and stressors (political conflict, social, economic and more recently pandemics) to exacerbate the impacts of climate change on vulnerable communities.

### The vicious cycle of poverty and vulnerability, food insecurity, migration and conflict in the region (political and the ones emerging between countries and within countries) is exacerbated by climate change. This suggests that investing in anticipatory and early response actions at the government and community level significantly reduces the impact of climate change and increases resilience to future shocks.

### The African Union’s Green Wall Initiative aims to address the challenges, risks and vulnerability that climate change poses to sub-Saharan Africa in a more comprehensive and integrated way. (FAO, in partnership with the African Development Bank and the World Food Programme, proposes the “Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Approach” (AICRMA) as an innovative approach for addressing climate risk at national level in the Sahelian Countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW)).

### At the core of the AICRMA is the integration of early warning systems into GCF umbrella programs. The GGW, the objective of which is to build, strengthen and scale up the resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and rural communities of seven countries in West Africa, will be used as a pilot project for this approach.

### This approach combines climate risk preparedness with climate risk reduction by integrating early warning systems into GCF umbrella programs. The GGW will use an integrated early warning system network and develop climate information and early warning services that provide robust climate data to governments, smallholder farmers and rural communities in the seven countries of West Africa. These services will be used to identify risks and vulnerabilities related to the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events.

### This approach combines climate risk preparedness with climate risk reduction by integrating early warning systems into GCF umbrella programs. The GGW will use an integrated early warning system network and develop climate information and early warning services that provide robust climate data to governments, smallholder farmers and rural communities in the seven countries of West Africa. These services will be used to identify risks and vulnerabilities related to the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events.

### Although the agricultural insurance industry is still in an incipient phase in some countries, experiences in the region have shown that early warning systems can significantly reduce the impact of climate change on smallholder farmers by providing them with information and early warning services for policymaking, investment programming and resilience planning at national and community levels. These services will be used to identify risks and vulnerabilities related to the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events.

### The pilot project will be implemented before a climate shock strikes.

**Page 11**

12. Component B: Climate risk preparedness This component will support the expansion and upgrading of existing early warning systems and hydrometeorological observation networks and capacity-building to enhance data collection, interpretation, understanding and dissemination of climate information. The focus is on national meteorological services, national hydrological services, micro and macro insurance industry (climate models and agricultural insurance product development), but also the selection of the most suitable agricultural practices and technologies for adaptation to climate change. This component will support the development of a national strategy to respond to climate variability (mapping calendar, best timing for planting and processing, choice of the most suitable agricultural practices and technologies) and to climate change (e.g., crop insurance, early warning systems, etc.). The programme will also support the establishment of an institutional framework for the implementation of the National Strategy.

13. Component C: Climate risk reduction (adaptation and mitigation): This component aims to strengthen climate change adaptation capacity and boost climate resilient and low emission investments in smallholder agriculture value chains and food systems through a) a national strategy on climate change, b) a national agricultural insurance programme, c) the development of an early warning system for climate-related disasters, d) the establishment of a national framework for climate risk transfer (comprised 1) using forecast-based early action (FBA) and climate risk bands (component 3) that provide forecast-based financing, and e) building on the work supported by the previous component to support the adoption and implementation of the best adaptation and mitigation measures per country and targeted areas.

14. Component D: Climate risk transfer (micro and sovereign risk transfer mechanisms): This component will support countries and communities in developing micro-insurance schemes for smallholder farmers, livestock keepers and other vulnerable groups with low value livestock claims. Under this component, modal risks of climate change on smallholder farmers will be transferred to national and international insurance markets through the development of a national agricultural insurance programme (NAP) that includes: i) weather index insurance; ii) crop insurance; iii) livestock insurance; iv) micro-insurance for women and youth; v) disaster risk financing; vi) climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives, such as the Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR) and similar multilateral and bilateral sources. The NAP will be developed in close collaboration with national meteorological services, agricultural extension services, public sector institutions, private sector insurers and reinsurers, microfinance institutions, banks and businesses investment decisions on climate risk transfer mechanisms will be supported by the development of a national strategy on climate change, which will also support agricultural policy and planning, investment programming and resilience planning at national and local levels.

15. The main targeted products are key staple crops (cereals, maize, sorghum and groundnuts), livestock (cattle and sheep, goats and poultry) and fisheries. The programme will focus on the development of a national agricultural insurance programme that will focus particularly on women and youth, as they are the most vulnerable people to climate change. It will build on: i) the extensive experience gained from the pilot phase of the African Risk Capacity (ARC), which provides disaster risk financing on the continent and assisting countries to access both capacity-building and financial resources; ii) the Africa Resilience Insurance Mechanism (ARIM); iii) the African Risk Management Programme (ARMIP), which provides disaster risk financing on the continent and assisting countries to access both capacity-building and financial resources; iv) the African Risk Capacity (ARC); v) the African Risk Management Programme (ARMIP); vi) Rural Resilience Initiative (RRI), which enables the poorest farmers to access weather index insurance, and ii) other partners' experience in developing agricultural insurance schemes. The programme will also support the development of climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives, such as the Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR) and similar multilateral and bilateral sources.

16. The National Designated Authorities (NDA) of the seven selected countries (Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal, and Sudan) will be responsible for implementing the programme in their respective countries through the establishment of National Climate Change Programmes. The total programme is expected to result in the reduction or avoidance of approximately 2146 899tCO2e over the life of the project (2017-2030). This will be achieved by: i) reducing the vulnerability and exposure of smallholder farmers, ii) increasing their resilience to climate change through the development of a national agricultural insurance programme that will contribute to the achievement of the objectives of the Great Green Wall (GGW). The GGW initiative promotes an integrated approach to environmental restoration and sustainable land management in Africa by restoring 100 million ha of degraded land, sequestering the equivalent of 250 million tons of CO2 and the creation of 10 million green jobs by 2030.

# B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 17. Stretching from the Atlantic Ocean to the Horn of Africa, the Sahel region is one of the world's largest semi-arid and arid zones. It covers an area of about 3 million km², which represents roughly 5% of the land surface of the continent. The region extends southwest into the Sudan at a rate of 1–10 km per year[1], depending on the country. One of the poorest and most environmentally fragile regions in Africa, the Sahel is home to about 32 million people living in extreme poverty under harsh living and growing conditions which will be worsened by climate change, namely the rise in temperature, rainfall variability and erratic precipitation.

## 18. The Sahel is disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change variability (characterized by inter-annual and longer-term changes in rainfall patterns, extremes temperatures, recurring droughts, flooding including floods from rivers, dust storms, desertification, land degradation, soil erosion, and loss of biodiversity) which are expected to increase in intensity. The livelihoods and pastoralism for income and subsistence mean that livelihoods and food security are strongly affected by climate variability and often result in conflict over resources such as water and grazing lands. In the Sahel region, pastoralists and farmers will struggle to feed themselves as a result of recurrent food crisis and conflict in the region. To this end, one must add another 10 million people to the current population within the next two decades.

## 19. With a population that is expected to double by 2050[2], the countries of the Sahel region are committed to achieving the goals of the Paris Climate Agreement, as expressed in their NDCs (conditional and unconditional), by strengthening the resilience of their agricultural sector.

## 20. The targeted Sahelian countries and regions have a long history of confronting serious crises. Key human-influenced drivers of desertification include overgrazing, poor land management practices such as the use of fire for land clearing and deforestation (clearing land for agriculture), extended periods of shifting cultivation, mono-cropping, over-exploitation of the environment, lack of access to water resources, and the expansion of urban areas into rural areas. These factors combine with overgrazing of pasturine lands. These combine to contribute to alterations in rainfall patterns and increase the frequency of droughts.

### Temperature increase in the region is projected to be 1.7°C from 2040 until the end of this century[1]. The IPCC projects temperatures in the region to increase more than 5°C over the next two decades, 3.1°C by 2050 and 6.0°C by the end of the century[2].

### Precipitation levels: Seasonal rainfall variability in the Sahel can be explained as the response to anomalies in the atmospheric circulation patterns that are associated with El Niño and La Niña events, which influence surface temperatures, as the direct effect of increasing anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions or by a combination of both[3]. Sahel rainfall is also influenced by sea surface temperature (SST) anomalies. For example, during El Niño years, precipitation in the Sahel shifts northward and is correlated with both Pacific ENSO/Niña/Naña surface temperature (SST) anomalies and Atlantic SST anomalies[4].

## Precipitation trends in the past

Data from the first half of the 20th century shows a predominance of anomalously wet years and decades, followed by a decline in seasonal rainfall totals during the second half, with overall low rainfall deciles characterizing the last three decades. The Sahel experienced severe drought conditions from the mid-1960s to the mid-1980s, which reached a peak in 1972 and 1984 with a 30 percent decrease on multi-year average precipitation levels. This period of severe drought was attributed to natural causes such as climate variability already fragile ecosystems, initially attributed to human mismanagement of land resources (deforestation, overgrazing and soil erosion), but later linked to the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and Pacific Decadal Oscillation (PDO) and air pollution from human activity played a significant role in the drying of the Sahel.

## Recurrent droughts together with unsustainable farming practices (shifts also from rainfed agriculture, deforestation, overgrazing, etc.) have led to a dramatic decline in soil fertility and water availability. The Sahel is one of the most vulnerable regions on earth.

### Drought is not the only precipitation-related climatic hazard that the region faces. Countries in the Sahel are also experiencing increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves, floods, and storms.

#### For instance, Burkina Faso was struck by severe floods in 2001, 2003, 2005, 2007, 2009, and 2010. In 2010, the country experienced its worst flooding since independence in 1960. The flood affected more than 4 million people and caused an estimated US$83 million of damage.

#### In 2012, a severe heat wave killed at least 57 people in Niger and forced thousands to flee their homes. In 2013, the region experienced its worst flooding since independence with over 600,000 people affected by floods and landslides. The 2013 flood in Chad which affected 150,000 people is one of 20% of the country's regions.

#### There is also significant risk of frequent heat waves, droughts, and desertification. In addition to the direct economic costs associated with these events, they can cause indirect damage: according to UNISDR data from 2015, the annual average loss due to floods in Burkina Faso is US$16 million.

### A study by the World Bank (2017) on climate risks for food security in the Sahel region suggests that at least three climate hotspots in the Sahel, of which one was in the West Sahel: one along the border between Mali and Niger, another in northern Burkina Faso, and a third in northeastern Senegal. The study also found that the region has experienced a decline in rainfall of up to 50% during the last three decades.

#### The report included observations on how climate change is likely to exacerbate existing vulnerabilities and increase exposure to droughts and other multi-year droughts, such as the droughts in 1972-1990 and more recent droughts in the last ten years.

## Projections from Regional Climate Models (RCMs) to receive convective rainfall. However, many CMIP5 models indicate a wetter core region with a small delay in rainy season by the end of the 21st century.

Projections from regional climate models downscale global climate model projections and can provide more refined regional information on future changes in temperature and precipitation. The following figures show projected changes in seasonal mean (May–September) precipitation for the West Sahel over the 21st century, based on simulations with four RCMs: a) RCP4.5 (2036/2085); b) RCP8.5 (2036/2085); c) RCP8.5 (2071/2100); and d) RCP8.5 (2071/2100). Perhaps more importantly, models project a slight lengthening of the maximum dry spell length into the early 21st century in the West Sahel (Figure B), with a subsequent shortening by the end of the century (Figure E). This is expected to be accompanied by an increase in intensity of the mean precipitation during the wet season and a decrease in its duration. The latter is projected to become more pronounced by the end of the century in many countries (Figure 8). The shortening of the wet season is also expected to be more pronounced across the region.

### In the Sahel region, precipitation projections face greater uncertainties Overall, the main picture that emerges from these projections is that there will be a general increase in mean annual precipitation. However, areas to be distinguished are:

- The western part of the Sahel (The Gambia, Senegal and western Mali) could face significant decreases in mean annual precipitation.
- The central part of the Sahel (Mali, Burkina Faso, Niger and Chad) is projected to experience a decrease in mean annual precipitation. This will be accompanied by an increase in the number of days with no rainfall during any given wet season. These changes are expected to lead to a significant reduction in the mean precipitation in the rain seasons of up to 20 percent compared to historical precipitation within the same season, but

# Impacts of Climate Change and Variability

## Social and Economic Impacts: Climate vulnerability is the result of the complex interplay between the short- and longer-term impacts of climate change and variability on a range of social, economic, environmental, and political factors that enable or hinder effective adaptation. The West African Sahel is a region with a rapidly growing population and limited resources, making it particularly vulnerable to the effects of climate change.

### Agriculture: The agricultural sector across the seven Sahelian countries is characterized by important structural issues such as low productivity, high vulnerability to climatic variability and change, and weak institutional capacity. These countries are highly dependent on rainfed agriculture for food security, with a very small share of their land under irrigation (less than 10% in most cases). The agricultural sector employs roughly 75 percent of the labour force (except Mauritania, where it only employs 52 percent) and for the vast majority of these countries, it is the main source of income. In addition to this, the region has a high population growth rate (3.4% per year), which is one of the highest in the world, with 64.5 percent of young people aged 25 or under. Population growth and migration (political and climate refugees) are also major factors that contribute to food insecurity.

### Migration: The region has been considered on a high risk for the occurrence of the PDSM and RDSME due to the heterogeneity results for the different locations in the Sahel. These variations are associated with multi-hazard events (heat waves, droughts, floods, etc.) that can lead to food insecurity and migration. The region is characterized by both irrigation from the river basin (Senegal River, Gambia River, Lake Chad, Niger River and Volta River) and without irrigation and permanent crops (peanuts, cotton, rice). In the case of Senegal, the results show a decrease in yield since 1950. For Mali, the results indicate decreases of 5 percent for irrigated areas and 10 percent for rainfed fields, even when the period is limited to the last decade. The same trend can be observed for Burkina Faso where yields have decreased since the 1960s and a fifth of all crops has disappeared locally because of rising temperatures and lower rainfall (sub-Saharan Africa: Climate Change and Food Security). In Niger, the results show an increase in yield productivity over time but with a decrease after 2005. A specific Annex 24 showing the results of the analysis on hazards and their impacts on yield productivity is attached.

36. Fisheries: An important source of livelihood and protein in the region, fisheries are already under stress from overfishing and habitat degradation. Climate change is expected to increase sea surface temperatures (SST) by up to 2°C by mid-century, increasing the frequency of severe weather events threatening marine and coastal ecosystems. There is also a risk of more being affected by lower rainfall in the wet season, which would reduce river flow and water availability for irrigation.

Furthermore, decreased water quality and dissolved oxygen content will lower reproduction rates and health. Fishing communities are particularly vulnerable to climate change because they depend on small-scale fishing (SLS) or small-scale industrial fishing (SSIF). As the Gambia is primarily low-lying, it will be strongly affected by SLR, which is projected to increase by 8 centimeters per decade in the region.

37. Land use: The Gambia has a high population density and limited land area. Most of its population lives within 5 kilometers of rivers or lakes, with 20% living on less than one hectare of land. This makes it particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased temperatures, reduced rainfall, and more frequent droughts.

The Gambia is also a major producer of rice in West Africa, which accounts for about 17 percent of the workforce and accounts for over half of the country's exports. Rice production has been declining due to increasing salinity levels and soil acidification will result in significant loss of income and livelihoods. Soil carbon decline related in polyester by 2050 in Europe and North America, with a corresponding increase in CO2 emissions.

### Climate change impacts on water resources
- **Increased evaporation** of surface waters.
- **Reduced water levels**, especially in areas like Burkina Faso, Mauritania, and Niger.
- **Water scarcity** leading to conflicts over access.

### Water management strategies
- **Improved irrigation systems** (e.g., solar-powered pumps).
- **Rainwater harvesting** for multiple harvests during the year.
- **Land pressure reduction**, especially in areas with high population density.

### Key food crops and livestock
- **Water requirements**: 80 percent of key food crops and livestock.
- **Solar water pumping (SWP)**: Expected to grow significantly over the next decade due to its cost-effectiveness compared to diesel-powered pumps. SWP systems are already installed in 25 countries, with 90 percent of them being solar-powered.

### Water resources under management
- **Niger River Basin**: 140 million hectares under some form of water management.
- **Gambia**: 270,000 hectares of irrigable land; 60 percent are concentrated in the Upper Gambia Valley. The remaining 40 percent is scattered across the country.

### Water potential
- **Under-exploited lands**: About 100 million ha or about 37 percent of estimated potential.
- **Water resources**: 25 billion m³/year, with a potential for additional water supply through climate change and climate variability. Nearly all hydro-agricultural developments (arrangements hydrique-agricole in French) are located in the Gambia.

## B

### Climate impacts on water resources in the Niger River Basin are varied. There has been a drop in groundwater levels and an increase in surface water availability, which is attributed to increased rainfall intensity during the rainy season, decreased length of the season (the number of days) of agricultural production and land erosion. The basin’s hydrological regime is characterized by high interannual variability, with extreme events such as floods and droughts being common occurrences. In some areas, high-value vegetative crops are grown in the dry season, and soil has eroded over the past years.

### Lake Chad Basin is currently one of the most unstable areas in the world. The water level is largely the result of the flow from the Chari River, which originates in Cameroon. The basin’s hydrological regime is characterized by high interannual variability, with extreme events such as floods and droughts being common occurrences. In some areas, high-value vegetative crops are grown in the dry season, and soil has eroded over the past years.

### Water quality: Precipitation and associated river discharges are major drivers of water pollution, as they cause excess runoff from agricultural lands and urban areas, which carries pollutants into rivers and lakes. This leads to eutrophication of surface water and wastewater treatment and disposal in several ways. Elevated stream temperatures, combined with lower flows during the dry season, can lead to increased algal blooms and reduced dissolved oxygen levels in surface waters. These factors increase water quality in many river basins by reducing nutrient concentrations, but they also have potential health impacts on aquatic life.

### Navigation constraints: Navigation on the Senegal, Gambia, Niger River and Lake Chad will become increasingly vulnerable to more varied precipitation patterns and unusable or unsafe water. The impact of extreme weather events (e.g., heavy rainfall) is likely to increase in the future as a result of climate change. This will lead to increased sedimentation in rivers and lakes, which can reduce navigation capacity.

### Agricultural sector: Climate and non-climate risks combine to create significant challenges for smallholder farmers in the agricultural sector across Sahelian countries also face risks that are not related to climate and their effect productivity. These include access to markets, access to credit, access to inputs, access to extension services, access to land tenure security, access to education, and access to health care.

## Baseline situation

47. Today, disease outbreaks, such as the COVID-19 pandemic or the previous Ebola crisis not only pose threats to the health of people and animals but also have significant economic consequences for countries in terms of GDP loss.

Without adequate safeguards, preparedness, mitigation measures and mechanisms to transfer risks (weather, pest, disease, post-disaster), climate change will continue to exacerbate poverty and inequality. In 2013, the World Bank estimated that by 2050, climate change could cause a 4% decline in GDP per capita for low-income countries compared with a baseline scenario at 2013 [2013]. Dasgupta and Basu (2010). Combined with the impact of climate change, non-climatic stressors such as conflict, political instability, and economic shocks will continue to exacerbate poverty. In light of these challenges, climate change and variability at local level in the Sahel must give attention to the nexus of both climatic and non-climatic stresses.

### Four main factors define the baseline of the program proposed:

1. An increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, especially droughts, intense rains and floods
2. Countries being countries with very limited resilience and adaptation capacity: According to the NO-GAIN vulnerability index, 40% of the world's population lives in countries that are highly vulnerable (score < 0.5) or extremely vulnerable (score < 0.3) in terms of resilience, with limited adaptive capacities (see table 2). Furthermore, these countries experience high exposure to climate change and variability, which is expected to increase over time.
3. The increasing impact of climate-related hazards (dry spells or droughts, diseases, locusts and other pests, floods, heatwaves, etc.) on the agricultural sector in developing countries
4. The effects of climate change on food security: This affects not only livelihoods and food security, but also energy production, water resources and gross domestic product (GDP). According to the NO-GAIN index, 70% of the world's population lives in countries that are highly vulnerable or extremely vulnerable to these impacts. In addition, climate change will increase the vulnerability of populations more vulnerable to other hazards and socio-political changes in the affected countries, where agriculture is a key sector (more than one-quarter of GDP in 2019), these impacts can reduce national GDP by up to four percent per year.

## The lack of reliable data, knowledge and capacity in countries on climate change is a major barrier to climate preparedness and risk management.

There are large gaps in the technical capacity and infrastructure for collecting climate information and early warning services in many developing countries. In the Sahel, the hydrological infrastructure capacity is the lowest of all global regions, with observation networks dominated by rain gauges and limited hydrometric stations. The lack of reliable data and knowledge for collecting climate and impact data and monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) standards are limits, as is the capacity to use this information in a timely manner to inform decision-making processes. In many countries, it is necessary to support the development of modern financial services such as agricultural and climate risk insurance (more on this later). The lack of reliable data and knowledge for collecting climate and impact data and monitoring, reporting and verification standards are limits, as is the capacity to use this information in a timely manner to inform decision-making processes. In many countries, it is necessary to support the development of modern financial services such as agricultural and climate risk insurance (more on this later).

## Ensuring farmers' access to climate data for future planning

Ensuring farmers’ access to climate data for future planning is fundamental for building their resilience and capacity to adapt to climate change. The lack of reliable data and knowledge for collecting climate and impact data and monitoring, reporting and verification standards are limits, as is the capacity to use this information in a timely manner to inform decision-making processes. In many countries, it is necessary to support the development of modern financial services such as agricultural and climate risk insurance (more on this later).

## Multi-hazard early warning systems

### Disaster risk knowledge and detection of hazards

**Data collection and distribution**

- **Observation**
  - Rain gauges
  - Hydrometric stations
  - Weather stations
  - Remote sensing

**Analysis**

## 3-Time response and decision effectiveness of planning and responses to both mitigate and adapt to climate events

Even though drought is a known hazard in the region, it takes about 12 months for the first authorities to arrive in order to take action. This is because there are no early warning systems in place. The same applies when it comes to such climate risks in these countries few years ago, especially for pre-impact action to reduce risk, including the financing mechanisms that allow for this. In many cases, the response time of authorities and communities has been allowed to occur, often beyond the time frame of the hazard climate. The COVID-19 pandemic has brought to light the difficulties in implementing early warning systems.

## 50% While some progress has been made on addressing climate risks through fragmented and narrow interventions

While some progress has been made on addressing climate risks through fragmented and narrow interventions, many challenges remain. ## The four priorities for action at the local, national and regional levels of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction

### Priority 1: Understanding disaster risk reduction
- **Disaster risk knowledge**: This encompasses understanding the exposure to hazards, vulnerability, capacities, and potential consequences of disasters.
- **Disaster risk communication and awareness**: This involves disseminating information on decision-making processes related to disaster preparedness and response in a clear and understandable way.

### Priority 2: Strengthening disaster risk governance
- **Detection, monitoring, early warning systems**: These are measures for the identification of hazards and possible consequences.
- **Preparation and response**: This includes disaster preparedness programs, including training exercises, drills, and emergency evacuation plans. It also involves establishing and maintaining communication networks.

### Priority 3: Investing in disaster risk reduction
- **Disaster risk management policies**: These are frameworks for the identification of hazards and possible consequences.
- **Disaster risk management institutions**: These include the establishment and maintenance of communication networks.

### Priority 4: Enhancing disaster preparedness for effective response to "solid back to normal" recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction
- **Disaster risk management systems**: These encompass all aspects of disaster risk reduction, including early warning systems.
- **Disaster risk management institutions**: These include the establishment and maintenance of communication networks.

## Four elements of end-to-end, people-centred early warning systems

### Disaster risk knowledge
- This encompasses understanding the exposure to hazards, vulnerability, capacities, and potential consequences of disasters.

### Detection, monitoring, early warning systems
- **Hazards identification**: Are there forecasting and warning services in place?
- **Possible consequences**: Are there mechanisms for identifying possible consequences?

### Warning dissemination and communication
- **Disaster risk management policies**: These are frameworks for the identification of hazards and possible consequences.
- **Disaster risk management institutions**: These include the establishment and maintenance of communication networks.

### Preparation and response
- **Disaster risk management systems**: These encompass all aspects of disaster risk reduction, including early warning systems.
- **Disaster risk management institutions**: These include the establishment and maintenance of communication networks.

According to World Bank,[16] IFAD’s[7] recent evaluations of combined index insurance, impacts are greater when it is combined with other risk management tools such as crop diversification and improved agricultural practices (e.g., irrigation) rather than alone. Over the last few years, IFAD has been implementing within its project in Senegal (PARAF-E and PANDER-I) an integrated approach to index-based insurance that combines index insurance with a range of other risk management tools.

This approach combines climate risk preparedness with climate risk measures (adaptation) and climate risk transfer (micro insurance). ## B

### Programme targeted areas and beneficiaries

The target areas of the GCF programme in the seven selected countries are defined and identified through the DFID-based approach, which is based on four key dimensions: a) land use and tenure; b) poverty and livelihoods; c) food insecurity and nutrition; d) climate vulnerability and unsustainable management of natural resources. In rural gender dynamics and youth employment, and in particular in the context of climate change, women are disproportionately affected by climate change.

The programme targets three types of areas: a) high priority areas (HPAs), b) medium priority areas (MPAs); c) low priority areas (LPA). These targeted regions have a range of complementary characteristics that make them vulnerable to climate change and climate variability. The programme’s target intervention regions are summarised in Table 1 and built on the existing work of DFID, WFP, FAO, IFAD, and other partners.

### Target groups

Target groups (a) are small enterprises engaged in staple crops (maize, millet, sorghum and groundnuts), livestock (dairy and beef; sheep and goats; chicken); and non-timber forest products. (b) Remote (vulnerable) sites characterised by subsistence agriculture, low levels of education, poor access to services, high poverty rates, and limited economic opportunities. (c) Vulnerable communities including people living with disabilities, older persons, women-headed households, and young people. (d) Smallholder farmers (e.g., smallholder producers of households), which are all characterised by pronounced weaknesses in the production of rural agricultural and livestock products and a lack of economic opportunities and jobs. Beneficiaries and target groups are complementary to Annex 2: Climate.

## Weak policy, institutional coordination mechanisms and capacity gaps in climate risk management and planning

- **Limited capacity of farmers and farmers' organizations to access insurance or risk transfers:** Lack of a network of net agri-grocers to manage climate information, maintain the quality of seeds and fertilizers, and provide useful information to farmers
- **Limited enabling policy, institutional, coordination mechanisms and capacity gaps in integrated climate risk management:** Weak coordination mechanisms on integrated climate risk management. Lack of an adequate regulatory and fiscal framework for integrated climate risk management. Limited awareness and incentives that affect the follow-up and capitalization of successful climate change adaptation interventions.
- **Lacking enabling policy environment conducive to the development of innovative financial products:** Lack of trust among policymakers, development practitioners and private sector actors on how to address climate change and how these relate to their own mandates.

---

### Component 2: Climate Risk Management (Prevention): Supports training, capacity-building, expanding and upgrading existing warning systems (EWS) and hydrometeor observation networks to enhance data collection, interpretation and understanding of climate information and early warning signals, as well as the development of climate risk management tools and services for communities, livelihoods, assets and operations. Through this component, quantitative and qualitative climate information will be made available to farmers, pastoralists, fishers, herders, small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), local governments and other stakeholders in a timely manner through the development of an early warning system (EWS) and innovative disaster risk financing mechanisms. This will foster the development of the most of micro-savings insurance industry and financial services for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The component also aims to strengthen the capacity of farmers, pastoralists, fishers, herders, SMEs, local governments and other stakeholders in the use of climate information (e.g., agroforestry practices and techniques, as well as energy access). This component also aims to strengthen the capacity of rural communities and households to manage risks associated with climate change through the development of innovative disaster risk financing mechanisms. The component will promote impact-based forecasting (improving climate-impact available in the countries and tracing climate and meteorological data) and improve the use of early warning systems (EWS) for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

#### Component 2: Climate Risk Reduction (Adaptation/Resilience): Promotes the adoption of the best, alternative adaptation and mitigation practices/technologies by smallholder farmers, supported by reliable climate information (Component 1) and financial services (Component 3). This component will support the development of a national climate change strategy. More concretely, it will support micro- and small-sized climate-resilient agroforestry and pastoralism schemes and Agro-pastoral systems that are adapted to climate change impacts. It will also promote the adoption of best practices in low carbon and climate-resilient land use (e.g., agroforestry, tree-shade agriculture), integrated Agro-ecological Nature-based Solutions (NBS) for climate change adaptation and mitigation, and climate-informed land use, tree-shading and agroforestry techniques. It will enable farmers to adopt Ziz and half-moon rain water harvesting techniques for rain and water conservation; it foster the development of micro-savings insurance industry and financial services for climate change adaptation and mitigation (Component 3) powered by renewable energy sources to help cope with the consequences of drought and extreme heat events, as well as other climate-related disasters. The component will also support the adoption of best practices in resilient roots and i) integrate capacity-building on climate-resilient agro and pasture management techniques into AFPRS.

### Component 3: Climate Risk Transfer (Micro- and sovereign transfer mechanisms): Aim to overcome barriers related to micro-insurance, including lack of awareness, limited access to insurance products, high premiums, low coverage levels (micro-insurance) and the development of national and international insurance markets offering timely compensation to farmers by providing them with a range of climate risk transfer options. The component will support the development of innovative coping strategies (selling animals and assets, migration, competition over resources and conflicts) in the event of climate change-related disasters and extreme weather events. It will also promote the adoption of best practices for land use, land access and energy access to power agriculture and livestock value chains (Component 2) helping smallholder farmers, pastoralists, fishers, herders, SMEs and other stakeholders to improve their risk profiles and their ability to handle the devastating impacts of climate disasters and economic losses, and generally contribute to the development of a national climate change strategy. The component will support the development of micro-insurance products and services for smallholder farmers, come at a high premium and thus, are limited in scope and reach. Economic models that can be used to design and implement innovative insurance products and services (e.g., weather-based policies have been incorporated. While each of these risk mechanisms can be justified individually, there is a need for their integration.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 37 OF 89

## B

### 8.3 Programme/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

The programme's main overall objective is to increase resilience and enhance the livelihoods and food and water security of smallholder farmers in seven countries of the Green Wall region by improving their access to climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies that are adapted to local ecosystems.

### Outcome 1: Improved decision-making and planning for adaptation

Outcome 2: Strengthened resilient agroforestry and pastoral value chains, powered by reliable and affordable energy services

Outcome 3: Reduced residuals (financial market products and services/high premiums) to access agricultural insurance for governments and smaller farmers to enhance resilience building.

### Effectiveness of the regional approach

The project will trigger or strengthen cooperation and synergies between the seven countries of the GGR on climate change adaptation, including through the implementation of 21466.498 MCOEs against the country NDC targets. This mitigation potential represents 10.3 percent of the GWG emission reduction target for the region.

### Justification of programme's intervention areas

The programme will target regions in seven countries of the Green Wall region that are most vulnerable to climate change, food insecurity and nutrition, gender inequality, youth unemployment, agricultural-based local economies, and other socio-economic challenges. These regions include the Sahel (Sahara-Sahelian) zone, the G5+1 Sahel programme, AIDEA's ARD and ARC targeted areas. These targeted regions have a range of ecosystems and agriculture systems that are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. They also produce a wide variety of crops produced there, such as maize, soybeans, dairy livestock, rice, tree crops (cereals) and horticulture, not to mention fish farming in other regions, including the Lake Chad and Niger River basins.

This programme will be implemented on a country-by-country basis.

#### B

**How will the GCF support climate change adaptation?**

The GCF will support the private sector engaging in climate risk management (Component 1) to be able to better understand climate risks and tailored agro-climatic information services, including insurance schemes. This will help them to reduce their exposure to climate-related risks and adapt to climate change and variability (Component 2). By increasing their understanding and ability to prepare and manage risk, farmers will also be better positioned to take advantage of new opportunities that arise from climate change (Component 3). The GCF will support public and private insurance companies (Component 3) coupled with rural finance schemes. For example, increased access to agro-climatic information services and early warning infrastructure can help farmers to reduce their exposure to climate-related risks and adapt to climate change (Component 2) thanks to techniques and infrastructure that help reduce post-harvest losses and raise the quality of their produce.

#### Output 1.1: Increased access to agro-climatic information services and early warning infrastructure to support integrated climate risk management

The GCF will support the development of a national agro-climatic information service (ACIS) that provides tailored agro-climatic information services, including insurance schemes. This will help farmers to reduce their exposure to climate-related risks and adapt to climate change and variability.

#### Output 1.2: Awareness raising and capacity building on integrated climate risk management

The GCF will support the development of a national agro-climatic information service (ACIS) that provides tailored agro-climatic information services, including insurance schemes. This will help farmers to reduce their exposure to climate-related risks and adapt to climate change and variability.

## Component 3 (climate risk reduction): The main outcome is strengthened climate resilient agroecosystems and value chains powered by reliable and affordable renewable energy sources. Reliably climate information generated through the Climate Information for Agriculture project will be used to improve agricultural practices, technologies, and energy sources for agricultural use per country developed and implemented in this component. This will contribute to increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers and their families to shocks and climate variability. Under this component, the programme will focus on the selection and piloting of a set of climate-resilient agroecosystems and value chains that are appropriate for each targeted area, which involves the adoption of new farm practices and technologies to improve resilience and sustainability.

### Outputs

#### Output 2.2: Diversified livelihood through the promotion of income generating activities powered with renewable energy sources (RE) in targeted areas

(Primarily funded by IFAD-boosted investment and IFAD GD+ Sahel regional programme). This output promotes the diversification of livelihoods, particularly for women and youth, through the development of new value chains as a way to strengthen the resilience of farms and households to climate change, while contributing to sustainable land management. The activities will be implemented in targeted areas under the G1 + S1 baseline programme and boosted investment in the T2+T3 baseline programme.

- **2.2.5** Establishment of 300 new solar-powered irrigation systems in targeted areas (water storage, water maintenance; stores and barns; digital finance and money transfers)

## Component 3 (Climate Risk transfer): Promotes climate risk transfer mechanisms at both the micro and macro levels to reduce vulnerability of smallholder farmers, particularly in Africa, to the impacts of climate change. This is achieved through financial market products and services/premiums) governments and smallholder farmers access to agricultural insurance and other micro-insurance products that are affordable, accessible, and appropriate for their needs. The mechanism reaches smallholder communities by leveraging existing channels of coverage (e.g., extension officers, farmer groups, etc.) and by reaching individual farmers directly through: by measuring technical feasibility of covering identified critical risks through index-based insurance, or by using a combination of micro-insurance products and services with the use of early warning systems. This structure component combines two outputs. Output 3.1 aims at supporting countries to establish, strengthen and expand access to climate risk transfer mechanisms for smallholder farmers in Africa. Output 3.2 aims at strengthening the capacity of governments and financial institutions to provide micro-insurance products and services to smallholder farmers.

#### 98. Output 3.1. Access to micro insurance expanded to enhance integrated climate risks management (executing entity: GCF, ARIC, IFAD, AGRIFIN). The objective is to expand access to micro-insurance for smallholder farmers in Africa through the Index Insurance Forum, index-based insurance cover based on criteria explored at various levels. Micro-level index insurance covers are designed and implemented by ARIC, with support from other implementing partners. The index insurance covers a range of terms of coverage, but also provides the incentive to reduce risk exposure and thus lower increase investment (i.e., reduces the cost of insurance). This output will be achieved through: i) strengthening the capacity of governments and financial institutions, agripreneurs, agricultural inputs, mobile network operators, national export companies (use the feasibility study for index-based insurance in Africa as a reference); ii) supporting the development of micro-insurance products and services; iii) raising awareness on the impact factors on very significant. Based on an inception assessment in each of the seven countries, that will be conducted at the beginning of this component, ARIC will develop a national strategy for index-based insurance. The strategy will include use of digital tools (like mobile money platforms). Premiums will cover cost or specific timeframe frequently related to the risk covered and will be paid by the farmer in advance. This output will also support the development of micro-insurance products and services that are affordable, accessible, and appropriate for smallholder farmers. 99\. Under this output, and based on results in the 10 BM countries, including Benin, the proposed strategy and incentives will be designed to encourage smallholder farmers' support for cost of insurance and use a depression gradient in which the price of insurance is higher in areas with high levels of risk and lower in areas with low levels of risk. This will allow the country and target group concern: for example, the programme could cover 90 percent of the premium in the first year, to provide an incentive for farmers to participate in the insurance scheme. The second phase would be a transition period of an exit strategy that aims to empower smallholder farmers to assume the responsibility for their insurance and manage the risk themselves. This will be achieved by providing training on how to use the insurance product, including how to transfer payment to the insurer upon receipt of the list of insured farmers, and amount of conditional transfer, i. farmers will receive a 50 percent discount on the premium if they are able to provide their own insurance documentation. Thus, farmers will progressively increase their contribution to purchase the input package, which will include the insurance product, as well as other inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, etc., and access to credit facilities.

100\. Support for micro-insurance product development will be provided by reducing barriers for farmers. This will not be done by itself but in conjunction with other activities that will be implemented under this output. These include the promotion of good agricultural practices (GAP) as a means of risk reduction and disease affecting both livestock and crops to smallholder farmer's needs and country context. This will create critical mass of insured farmers, which will lead to economies of scale and reduce the cost of insurance for individual farmers and yields and determine the price of insurance products in cooperation with reinsurers. Crop selection will be made during the feasibility study phase, taking into consideration the type of crops grown by smallholder farmers, including rice, maize, IFAD, WFP and government programs and the capacity to aggregate farmers. A special focus will also be placed on pastoralists and livestock keepers who are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The output will support the adoption of insurance as part of the inputs in smallholder farming, especially areas that are particularly prone to climate risks. This will include training for extension workers and other stakeholders on how to promote micro-insurance products and services. It is expected that this will lead to a reduction in the cost of insurance and increase the number of insured farmers.

101\. Regarding payment of premiums and pay-outs, the use of mobile channels will be used to facilitate the collection of insurance premium payments and claims. Mobile money accounts or prepaid savings phones, mobile network operators and insurance companies have the potential to reduce transaction costs for smallholder farmers in accessing financial services. This is because mobile money accounts are widely available and implement large scale sustainable distribution channels. The capacity building activity will include an advocacy campaign on the importance of micro-insurance products and services, as well as training for extension workers where the structure is optimized. Financial capacity of farmers will be supported by the Insurance for Work approach of the risk model, where the insurance premium is paid in installments over a period of time. The insurance company will conduct a simple gross margin analysis during the feasibility study phase, to assess the affordability of the product and determine the premium rate. This will also include an assessment of the cost of claims and other expenses that may be incurred by the insurer through WFP. For example, in Senegal, WFP will pay the beneficiaries premiums for CNAAS; the insurer to pay the premiums for the rest of the insured farmers. The total premium collected from all farmers (including those calculated per farm) and the sum of the entire season, the insurer will directly use this beneficiaries to compensate to which they subscribe. In other cases, the insurance company may choose to retain a portion of the premium as profit, which can be disbursed by the insurer directly to beneficiaries through distribution channels (financial institutions, farmer associations, etc.).

102\. Activities will include (activities disaggregated by country; see annex Activities breakdown per country):

3.1.1 Inception phase, which will give a good understanding of the context (types of risks, existing products and delivery channels, key value chains and crops...).

This phase is expected to be conducted in each country to obtain a clear understanding of the context and demand of the moment on project start types of risks, existing insurance product and delivery channels, key value chains, origin of the risk, etc. This will include conducting surveys and focus group discussions with farmers, extension officers, insurance, identify capacity-building needs and select partners on the basis of the IFAD's procurement rules (public agencies and non-governmental organizations). The output will also include a review of existing micro-insurance products and review of documentation. Field assessments to meet with communities and stakeholders will also be essential.

3.1.2 Data collection and index design, pricing and product finalization

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 43 OF 89

B

This document focuses on collecting data, design index, set pricing* and finalize the product based on the type of index selected (the mix of products). WFP will work with service providers, insurance and reinsurance companies to develop the product. The product will be designed in accordance with the requirements of the Green Climate Fund and the regulation for the different levels of commissions (for the insurer, the distribution channel and possibly a broker). According to the current market practices, the commission structure is as follows: 5% to 10% for insurance companies; 3.5% to 7% for delivery channels; 5% to 10% to third party distributors; 2% to 4% to brokers. The commission structure will be reviewed and agreed upon by the Green Climate Fund once a product has been selected. It is agreed to review the deal, provided the question remains acceptable, during 3 years.

### 3.5 Design of distribution and consumer education strategy, including design of the integration approach

With an integrated approach, WFP will work with insurance companies and other stakeholders to develop distribution channels (or aggregators) that will depend on the following criteria: i. experience and interest in distributing a micro-insurance product; ii. ability to provide adequate information to consumers about the product; iii. willingness to collaborate with the insurer; iv. sustainable model is capacity to gather participants, collect the data and premium, report it and settle it within 15 days of the end of each month. WFP will work with the insurance company on this sub-activity.

### 3.6 Design of distribution channels and consumer education strategy

WFP will work through the following channels, which will include a direct sales channel:

* **Direct Sales Channel:** WFP will work directly with the insurance company mainly with the insurance company, but also other stakeholders to develop this strategy.
* **Channel Building:** WFP will build relationships between insurance companies and/or handling over to insurance sector stakeholders.

### 3.7 Technical support at institutional level: Create a dedicated insurance company, a public scheme or a concourse plan

WFP will provide support to governments and supervisors, based on the needs assessment conducted under activity 2.1.1. This will include:

* **Technical assistance:** WFP will provide technical assistance in developing an index-based insurance product.
* **Training:** WFP will provide training for government officials and other stakeholders involved in the development of domestic public insurance schemes where this is to provide index insurance products to farmers, or even a compliance mechanism to ensure that the insurance companies are providing adequate information on the insurance scheme.
* **Technical support at institutional level:** WFP will also advise for setting up, or transitioning from public subsidies, or tax reduction or relief, in order to optimize the revenue generated by the insurance product and make it more accessible to end users.

### 3.8 Establish an M&E system using WFP framework under R4

It will include key performance indicators to help countries and insurance companies monitor portfolio growth, loss ratio, premium collection, claims processing time, claims payment time, claims settlement rate, claims recovery rate, and the performance of the scheme and provide recommendations to improve the process. Every year, an outcome assessment will be done on the M&E system and the results will be used as a basis for improving the M&E framework. WFP will leverage the existing M&E framework to implement this activity.

### 3.9 Suggested premium structure: Diagnostic premium payment for emphasis

## B

### 3.1-2 Design of product design and distribution strategy for the selected market segments

There are no products to be developed in this market segment. Based on the information provided by the AMC (WRI) as well as our own research, there is only one major market segment that we will select for this project: the private sector (inclusive of government agencies, NGOs and Public Sector Units). The existing distribution strategy for this segment is to continue with the AMC WRI's Blue Marble product design and distribution.

### 3.1-3 Design of distribution and marketing strategy for the selected market segments

Although there exists a well-established distribution strategy in this segment (inclusive of government agencies, NGOs and Public Sector Units), it will be necessary to adapt this strategy to fit the AMC WRI's Blue Marble product design and distribution.

### 3.1-4 Development and implementation of distribution strategy during pilot selection

Continuing the existing distribution project will not be possible as the AMC WRI's Blue Marble product design is not yet available for distribution in this segment.

### 3.1-5 Design of the graduated based business model

The project will work with local governments and NGOs on the development of a graduated based business model.

### 3.1-6 Capacity building to support product design and distribution strategy implementation

Based on the AMC WRI's Blue Marble product design, WFP will develop a capacity building plan to support the implementation of this project.

### 104. Each country PMU will coordinate with WFP to implement these proposed activities. WFP will act as an implementing partner and will be responsible for the implementation of the following activities: (i) The PMU will provide technical support to the PMU and the PMU will report regularly to FA on its implementation status and performance. This programme will build on and scale up successful experiences in the region, including those from the WFP-FA project in Ethiopia, which has been implemented since 2013 for agriculture and micro-insurance, and WFP's India initiative. The GCP is already supporting the RA initiative in four countries of Central Asia (Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Tajikistan, and Uzbekistan) through a grant from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) under the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDNF). In addition to providing technical support for the implementation of the project activities, WFP will also provide training on climate risk management and disaster risk reduction. The GCP is currently implementing similar projects in six countries where ARC replica is present; synergies will be built.

### 105. Output 3.2: Sovereign risk transfer supported and promoted to strengthen integrated climate risks management mechanisms

The project will support the development of a sovereign risk transfer mechanism that can provide financial resources for governments in cushioning themselves from the impacts of climate disasters. Experience has shown that when disasters strike, countries are often left with no choice but to borrow money at high interest rates or seek aid from international donors. The GCP is currently implementing similar projects in six countries where ARC replica is present; synergies will be built.

The output will support countries in accessing a regional risk pool and provide coverage for their most vulnerable populations, including women and children, through the African Risk Capacity (ARC) insurance scheme. In addition to providing financial resources for governments in cushioning themselves from the impacts of climate disasters, sovereign risk transfer can guarantee a prompt availability of resources, which is vital for rapid assistance to the most affected people. The GCP will also support countries in developing and implementing disaster risk financing mechanisms, which aims to foster a paradigm shift towards disaster risk financing in African countries by supporting their


---
**Page 50**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 48 OF 59

B

participating in the pan-African regional risk pool offered by the ARC. Sustaining pool participation has been challenging mostly due fiscal constraints and the absence of a systemic approach to disaster risk financing. In this context that the ARCF is working with them on putting in place disaster risk financing strategies that will identify options for mobilizing disaster risk financing, including through insurance mechanisms. The ARCF is also supporting countries in their elaboration of the disaster risk financing strategies and financing in the counties, particularly at the local level; provide early warning systems, emergency response and resilience building post-disaster recovery. ARCF works closely with micro-insurance companies, and local financial institutions (banks, insurance institutions) to deliver the micro-insurance, micro-insurance and loan products.

## 100 Activities include (activities disaggregated by country: see annex Activity breakdown per country):

- **3.2.1:** 7 counties and 4 local contingencies planning for the 7 counties; review and assess the ARC agricultural, disaster risk management and climate change adaptation strategies.
- **3.2.2:** Trainings for additional 420 government officials on the use of ACR view and other sector ministries.
- **3.2.3:** Build the capacity of 150 farmers organizations and cooperatives to identify triggering thresholds and assess disaster risk.
- **3.2.4:** Build the capacity of 1,000 farmers organizations and cooperatives to identify triggering thresholds and assess disaster risk.
- **3.2.5:** Develop tools and financing instruments to identify, quantify existing disaster risks estimating the financial impact of disasters on the economy.
- **3.2.6:** Develop 7 national integrated climate risk frameworks using a comprehensive learning approach.
- **3.2.7:** Develop 10 regional integrated climate risk frameworks using a comprehensive learning approach.
- **3.2.8:** Develop 5 national budget allocation plans for disaster risk management and climate change adaptation, including national budget through ecotaxation, climate budget tagging (CBT) or national climate contingency funds.
- **3.2.9:** Strengthen institutional and coordination mechanisms for an effective use of the pay-as-you-reach scheme using micro-insurance and micro-loan products.
- **3.2.10:** Organize workshops with key representatives from the insurance industry and financial institutions.
- **3.2.11:** Support ARC in developing an approach to scaling up coverage in the Sahel region and the entire continent.
- **3.2.12:** Organize 4 high-level events on its integrated approach.
- **3.2.13:** Organize 4 high-level events on its integrated approach.
- **3.2.14:** Provide technical assistance to governments and technical assistance by ARCF, ADB and countries.
