107. GCF resources will be used for capacity building on the institutional, regulatory and legal framework for Afrique Intégrée (AI) development in the countries concerned through national budget. Through AURDF, AIADB will support the first year's country premiums payments to ARC. ARC will provide technical assistance (in kind contribution) to the countries concerned.

108. The programme’s approaches, actions, modes of organization and implementation will apply a general principle of subsidiarity. This means that the programme will be designed in such a way as to ensure that it is implemented at the lowest possible level of government. In terms of geography, the programme will target primarily the most “local” geographical scale as possible (village, commune, province, region). The programme will also aim for an approach which maximizes the involvement of local communities and implementation to direct users when possible; and (iv) knowledge management, by strengthening local capacities and knowledge sharing.

At the regional level:

• WAC will be responsible for its regional coordination role. A regional coordination unit (RCU) will be established at the regional level under IFAD at the G-5 Sahel headquarters in Niamey. Through the RCU, IFAD will implement all coordination activities related to the programme and will provide technical assistance to the countries concerned with respect to implementation. The RSC will also be responsible for validating Annual Work Programmes (AWPs). After validation, the AE will be responsible for implementing the AWPs in close collaboration with the WAC. The WAC will chair this committee, which will meet only once. This committee will bring together the various stakeholders involved in programme implementation and will provide a forum to discuss issues related to the implementation of the programme.

• The RCU will be in charge of the development of learning materials and knowledge products, the organization of exchange visits between countries, the establishment of a regional network of experts (e.g. through the G-5 Sahel Triangular Cooperation) and support the establishment of a knowledge platform specific to each country, as well as at a regional level.

• The RCU will also be responsible for ensuring that the programme is implemented in line with the principles which emphasize the role of Southern countries to face a changing climate. Build their resilience to climate using innovative approaches and tools (e.g., insurance). This will include the establishment of a knowledge platform on micro-insurance management. This platform will provide opportunities for exchange visits (between private sector, civil society, and ministries), training workshops, and other activities that will help countries to better understand how to implement microinsurance specific plans; a financial management and accounting specialist (PFC) and MME (PFC) level. They will be in charge of the development of learning materials and knowledge products, the organization of exchange visits between countries, the establishment of a regional network of experts (e.g., through the G-5 Sahel Triangular Cooperation), and support the establishment of a knowledge platform specific to each country.

• To build capacity for consulting information and GCF report preparation, a dedicated software will be developed by IFAD. This software will allow countries to collect data on micro-insurance and prepare reports on macro insurance and micro insurance respectively. ARC will be responsible for the implementation of activities under Component 1 (Component 1), which involves supporting countries for the development of micro insurance using the RA approach and related tools, as well as providing technical assistance in the areas of risk assessment, risk management, and disaster response programming and investment decision-making. This is a faster and more agile approach, which stimulates investments in micro-insurance and provides an immediate response to the needs of communities in the event of disasters.

While supporting the implementation of the programme, the RCU will be also building the capacity of countries to implement the programme.


B.4. Implementation arrangements (max 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

10. The RCU will be in charge of the development of learning materials and knowledge products, the organization of exchanges between Sahelian countries for resources and material technical know-how to share experiences (between South and Triangular Cooperators) and support the establishment of a knowledge platform specific to each country, as well as the creation of a regional network of experts on climate change adaptation.

The regional approach will emphasize the needs of Sahelian countries to face a changing climate, build their resilience and adapt their development strategies. The RCU will also be in charge of supporting the development systems and capacities on integrated climate risks management. This platform will provide opportunities for exchange within the region as well as with other regions and countries. It will also allow for the identification of business opportunities between the seven countries. The RCU will be composed of a lead regional coordinator with a background in climate change adaptation, an expert in financial management (e.g., project management, accounting specialist) and PM and M&E (P&I). They will be in charge of day-to-day programme management and coordination as well as the development of knowledge products. The RCU will also be responsible for supporting the communication and information and GCF report preparation, a dedicated software will be used and adopted. Both ACR and WFP will provide technical support to the RCU. The RCU will be supported by the National Focal Points (NFP) and Country Implementing Agents (CIA). NFPs will be responsible for providing inputs on the national context and issues (3.0up to 2.1.2) and CIA, as WFP representative, will be responsible for reporting on the implementation of the project in its country context. With new and more innovative approaches (e.g., parametric insurance), it is forecasted and planned that the RCU will be able to support the development of a regional approach for other countries in better programming and investment decision-making. This is a faster and more agile approach, which considers the specificities of each country and allows for a quicker response time (i.e., less time to assess impact) and allows for a faster response in the case of disasters. While supporting the implementation of the programme, the RCU will also be responsible for the development of a regional network and continue to provide coordination and maintain the knowledge platform after the programme ends.

11. In terms of knowledge management, a regional KM Officer will be responsible for the programme's capacity development in this area. The KM plan will be developed by the RCU and approved by the GCF. The finalized KM plan will be finalized, which takes into account the specifics of each country with the support of a service provider (e.g., WFP). The KM plan will include the identification of key stakeholders, including government officials, civil society organizations, private sector representatives, academia, research institutions and others. For external and internal communication, the programme will consider the diversity of communication objectives and channels to be used in order to reach all target audiences. The programme will use and adopt the most appropriate communication channels for exchange, sharing and learning purposes (e.g., radio, television, social media). The KM plan will also include a monitoring framework that will ensure its operationalized and will pay particular attention to the communication of crosscutting themes promoted by the programme such as gender inclusion, generation mainstreaming, youth inclusion, etc.

At the national level:

12. Each country component will be supervised by the Programme Management Unit (PMU) of the ministry in charge of agriculture or environment, depending on the country context and will be composed of various stakeholders. The NFPs will define the objectives for each country component and will be responsible for the overall coordination with other stakeholders such as organizations, insurance committees and banks, among others. The NFPs will define the orientations for the implementation of the programme in their respective countries and will ensure that the project is implemented in line with national priorities. They will also be responsible for ensuring that the project integrates the programme's action in complementarity and synergy with development partners in the agricultural sector to support the achievement of the country's objectives. The NFPs will be supported by a dedicated team, and activity reports, the PMU with monitoring implementation and any recommendations it may make during its monitoring missions.

13. Each IPM FAD will be staffed with a national officer, recruited competitively, with an insurance background in line with the requirements of the GCF. The recruitment process will follow the National Policy; women candidates will be encouraged. This officer will have the support of an additional assistant coordinator.

14. The Programme Management Unit: In each country, the proposed programme will be managed by the Ministry of Agriculture or Environment (depending on the country context). The PMU will be responsible and accountable to the government and FAD for the efficient use of project resources in line with the GCF requirements. The PMU will also ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the national priorities and the objectives of each country. Consultations and consensus-building at the national level will be carried out, but for each sub-project, the IPM FAD will be responsible for coordination and thematic issues related to the different components involved in the IPM main-based investments. The Ministry of Environment will contribute to the overall oversight of the project implementation by ensuring that the project is implemented according to the GCF standards, while the Ministry of Forests will support the PMUs in the implementation of the best adaptation and mitigation practices. The Ministry of Gender will be responsible for the implementation of the gender action plan and oversee the implementation of the Gender Action Plan. Other relevant government departments in the national

115. The programme promotes partnerships between key stakeholders and countries that will contribute to its general objectives by: (i) identifying the specific needs of each country involved; (ii) knowledge and respect of the specificities of each stakeholder involved; (iii) the identification of common interests, and (iv) the development of a joint strategy for the implementation of the programme. The programme encourages all local and country decision-makers on integrated climate risk management.

116. The PMU will be in charge of the administrative and financial procedures as well as of the monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities of the programme. Its main tasks are: (i) to ensure the coherence between the different components of the programme; (ii) to ensure the coherence of programme activities, and (iii) to be IFAD’s contact point for the programme. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all project partners comply with the terms and conditions of the contract, including the methods of intervention and approaches undertaken by implementing partners are defined in accordance with the overall strategy of the programme; (iv) ensure compliance with the budget framework; (v) manage current expenditures in accordance with the payment schedule; and: (vi) report to IFAD on a regular basis.

117. Quarterly coordination meetings of implementing partners will be organized by the PMU to monitor the progress of activities and share lessons learned from one region to another. With regards to the implementation of field activities, the programme will work with local authorities and communities in developing, regularly monitoring and evaluating the specific on-the-ground results related to project design and implementation.

118. Local Authorities and Communities (LACs). The crucial effort of both public and private local institutions is key for the programme’s success. It will provide opportunities for communities to communicate amongst themselves and with other stakeholders at different levels, working closely with the PMU in administrative support, the LACs as community-based implementation units, will play a central role in the implementation of the programmes. They will work with local authorities and communities in developing, regularly monitoring and evaluating the specific on-the-ground results related to project design and implementation.

119. Beneficiaries: The communities and their groups (producers, fishermen, breeders, women’s groups, agricultural producers’ associations, etc.) and other social groups (such as youth groups, etc) are the main beneficiaries of the programme. Community groups will work closely with local authorities, the PMUs, local advisory committees, local microfinance institutions, and other stakeholders to ensure that their needs are met through the implementation of specific activities (interventions); (ii) assess environmental and social impacts; (iii) submit to the LACs proposals of activities for implementation in their communities; (iv) participate in the selection process of projects; (v) evaluate pilot and activities using locally adapted M&E systems; (vi) conduct regular claim status management, communication with local authorities and other stakeholders on project progress; and: (vii) identify specific issues and solutions in their locality as they affect the selected river basin’s sustainable management.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 52 OF 89

#### B

**Agreements and Funds flow for the GCF grant proceeds and the co-financing**

123 Since this GCF program is a public programme, all agreements (grant) will be signed between the IFAD and the government entities: ADB, ARB, and WBG. These agreements will be executed in charge of the Ministry of Environment and Forests. The implementation of the grants and co-financing will be through the national entities such as Ministries of agriculture and the Met agencies as the agencies for the implementation of the relevant project activities.

124 At national level, the recipients will be the respective government through their financial arm in charge of the Ministry of Environment and Forests. The execution of the grants and co-financing will be through the national entities such as Ministries of agriculture and the Met agencies as the agencies for the implementation of relevant project activities.

123 Each partner will sign a MoU with the IFAD baselines PMU under the coordination of the regional unit. The MoU will define the roles and responsibilities of the different actors and the reporting requirements, which will be in line with GCF agreements and procedures.

123 Each country will manage its grant portion through its IFAD baseline PMU and report via the PMU. The PMU will prepare financial and technical reports for the Regional Coordination Unit, which will ensure quality review and clearance of these reports before submission to the IFAD Baseline PMU. The PMU will also provide technical assistance to countries for the implementation of the GCF grant processes.

**COVID-19 Mitigation Measures**

The 7 selected countries have set various measures to mitigate the spread of COVID-19 including restrictions on non-essential movement, increased use of personal protective equipment (PPE), and social distancing. These measures are based on recommendations by health organizations to limit the spread within countries. These measures impact the ability to conduct field activities in rural areas where most of the project beneficiaries live.

The following table presents a list of action at project level to mitigate the COVID-19 impact and ensure business continuity during the programme implementation period:

| Action | Description |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Increase use of PPE such as masks, gloves, and other protective equipment. |
| 2 | Restrictions on workers' presence in public places, use of drones and other digital tools to reduce human contact. |
| 3 | Restrict access to project sites for non-essential personnel. |
| 4 | Use of logistics, sanitary and phytosanitary controls amongst others. |
| 5 | With regard to mobility and stakeholder engagement, IFAD will ensure that the field visits are conducted in a safe manner to prevent COVID contamination in high areas. For zones where, the risk is high the remote design is priority. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 53 OF 89

## Specific Funding Activities for IFAD Outputs

### 1. GEF-Funded Activities (all outputs): IFAD will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement with the GCF in line with the approved project document and the GEF’s funding agreement with the GCF. The agreement will be signed by the AGRIC agency and Agriculture Finance and Rural Finance Department of ADB through the ADRIPI Programmes and the Development Bank of Latin America (BID). Contracts for goods and services will be signed by the EEs with service providers.

### 2. IFAD-Funded Activities (output 2.3): IFAD will sign a Financing Agreement with the host countries through the Ministry of Agriculture and/or the ADB. The agreement will be signed by the AGRIC agency and Agriculture Finance and Rural Finance Department of ADB through the ADRIPI Programmes and the Development Bank of Latin America (BID). Contracts for goods and services will be signed by the EEs with service providers.

### 3. ARD-Funded Activities (Output 3.2): The African Development Bank will sign a Protocol agreement with the selected countries represented by the ministries of finance and economy for activities funded with proceeds from the AFRICAN RURAL DEVELOPMENT FUND (ARDF). Contracts for goods and services will be signed by the EEs with service providers for the implementation of the activities.

### 4. ARC-Funded Activities (Output 3.2): ARC will sign a Protocol agreement with the selected countries represented by the ministries of finance and economy for activities funded with proceeds from the AFRICAN RURAL DEVELOPMENT FUND (ARDF). The outsourcing of ARD is not estimated.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 56 OF 89

## B

### Programmes management and coordination

As of April 2017, the GCF will be responsible for the change of reporting to the PAF. The PAF will report to IFAD on results and progress reports to IFAD will report to GCF.

### Reporting

All BCs will be required to report to the PAF. The PAF will be required to report to IFAD. IFAD will report to GCF.

### Private Sector Facility (PSF)

Private sector projects will be reported by PMUs to the PAF. The PAF will report to IFAD. IFAD will report to GCF.

### FMU in each country

During the implementation mission, the FMU will provide a progress report to the PAF. The PAF will report to IFAD and IFAD will report to GCF.

### Reporting to the ROU and GCF

The ROU will be responsible for reporting results and progress to the GCF. The PMUs are responsible for reporting to the ROU, the PAF and IFAD and IFAD reports to GCF.

## B.4 Justification of funding request (max 1500 words; approximately 2 pages)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 57 OF 89

## Forecast-based action programs and financing through CIEWS to boost a weak or inexistent agricultural insurance market in seven selected countries from sub-Saharan Africa, South Asia, and Latin America and the Caribbean.

By promoting the implementation of this integrated climate risk management business model, GCF grants will contribute to increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers against climate change impacts by reducing their exposure to weather-related risks. By targeting the most vulnerable communities with inadequate climate risk management options, without further ado, the GCF aims at addressing climate concerns and that 40-100 percent of GCF investments are specifically climate focused.

The GCF grant is requested to cover part of incremental cost of building the resilience of the FBP in these seven countries. The GCF will also provide technical assistance for the implementation of the integrated climate risk management approach, which includes the development and testing of a Climate Risk Management Action Plan (CRMAP) that could be used to tackle climate change across sectors and regions. The GCF grant will also cover the cost of developing and implementing CRMAPs and grants for the climate risk transport component. The amount of US$100 million from the GCF will allow each country to develop a CRMAP, which will include an integrated approach that is tailored to their specific needs and will facilitate coordination. South-South and triangular cooperation and other international development partners are encouraged to contribute to this project.

### B. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The programme has been designed to guarantee the sustainability of activities beyond the duration of the project, it encompasses a number of exit strategies that will ensure the long-term viability of CIEWS. The GCF grant will enable CIEWS act as a response mechanism for climate risk management. It forecasts-based early action (PFA) and triggers insurance claims in advance of an event. This approach is more cost-effective than disaster relief, and it is more effective than post-disaster emergency aid. The programme will minimize and prevent the impacts of increased climate variability on agricultural value chains with a range of forecasters, indicators and decision-making tools. It will also provide a framework for the development of insurance products that are tailored to specific contexts (adoption of the best adaptation measures), and climate risk transfer using forecast-based micro-and macro-insurance financing.

### 142. Through FBP supported by CIEWS, the programme will further the development of a nascent agricultural insurance market will complement and build ongoing investments already in place that focus more on climate risk preparedness

**14.2** Additionally, the programme will also support the transfer of capacity to national government and non-governmental actors to guarantee that they are able to fully assume the activities at programme end. - **14.3** Strengthening the capacities of farmers, decision-makers and government institutions on integrated climate change risk management. This will include training for farmers in the use of weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO to identify their needs and capacity gaps. To sign up.

- **14.4** Learning and knowledge management: Technical assistance on all programme components will be provided help smallholder farmers acquire a more informed view on agricultural insurance and adopt it as a tool for managing climate change risks. The project will also provide technical assistance in the development of an integrated climate risk management system (ICRMS) to support the implementation of the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP). This will be done through the following activities: 1) establishment of a national climate change information centre; 2) strengthening the capacity of key stakeholders, including farmers, cooperatives, MSMEs, FMO, decision-makers and institutions to use weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO in their work on climate change risk management. The project will support them in the framework of South-South cooperation to strengthen market operators' ability for the main topching point. The ICRMS will be developed through a participatory process involving all stakeholders involved in climate change risk management. This will be done by establishing an integrated climate risk management system (ICRMS) that will supersede the KM work and quality at regional level; exchanges between countries through the SADC and building with SADC member states, including South Africa, Mozambique, Malawi, Zimbabwe, Botswana, Namibia, Lesotho, Swaziland, Tanzania, Zambia, Angola, Eswatini (formerly known as Swaziland), and Madagascar. The project will also support national and regional universities and regional research centres (ACMAGA, Agripharm and WASCAL) to name a few with key stakeholders in the development of an integrated climate risk management system (ICRMS). This will be done through the following activities: 1) establishment of a national climate change information centre; 2) strengthening the capacity of key stakeholders, including farmers, cooperatives, MSMEs, FMO, decision-makers and institutions to use weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO in their work on climate change risk management. The project will support them in the framework of South-South cooperation to strengthen market operators' ability for the main topching point.

- **14.5** Integrated national research: The project will also provide technical assistance in the development of an integrated climate risk management system (ICRMS) to support the implementation of the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP). This will be done through the following activities: 1) establishment of a national climate change information centre; 2) strengthening the capacity of key stakeholders, including farmers, cooperatives, MSMEs, FMO, decision-makers and institutions to use weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO in their work on climate change risk management. The project will support them in the framework of South-South cooperation to strengthen market operators' ability for the main topching point.

- **14.6** Building national insurance enterprises’ capacities and strengthening the agricultural insurance market: The programme will build the capacities of existing national institutions – including CNASAG (Senegal) and other public support services, such as the National Agricultural Insurance Agency (NAA) in Nigeria, the National Agricultural Insurance Corporation (NAIC) in Kenya, the National Agricultural Insurance Corporation (NAIC) in Tanzania, the National Agricultural Insurance Corporation (NAIC) in Uganda, the National Agricultural Insurance Corporation (NAIC) in Rwanda and the National Agricultural Insurance Corporation (NAIC) in Burundi – to provide insurance services. The programme will also support the development of a national agricultural insurance market that is capable of responding to the high demand and offering insurance products tailored to smallholder farmers’ needs. This will be done through the following activities: 1) establishment of a national climate change information centre; 2) strengthening the capacity of key stakeholders, including farmers, cooperatives, MSMEs, FMO, decision-makers and institutions to use weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO in their work on climate change risk management. The project will support them in the framework of South-South cooperation to strengthen market operators' ability for the main topching point.

- **14.7** Modernization and upgrading CIEWS: The selected countries still lack the capacity to develop a full climate information system, which is key for decision-making in economic sectors – namely agriculture. Fisheries and forestry are also affected by climate change and require better access to weather forecasts and early warning systems. Climate services will be strengthened. Selected national meteorological services will be in charge of the procurement, acquisition and installation of new equipment at the stations. The project will support the modernization and upgrading associated electronic equipment of the station. In addition, the technical team of these departments will use virtual or e-learning platforms to train their staff on the use of weather forecasts, early warning systems, cooperatives, MSMEs and FMO in their work on climate change risk management.

## (d) Other financing arrangements and co-financing (max. 250 words, approximately 8.3 page)
The GCF Program will be complementary to the overall IFAD, ADB, ARC baselines and other existing commitments. The total co-financing amount is estimated at US$143.4 million, of which IFAD ($30.3 million), ADB ($22.9 million) and ARC ($7.2 million). However, IFAD, ADB and ARC are not the only sources of co-financing. Any other financial input will be counted as co-financing. This co-financing enhances the financial sustainability of the programs beyond the timeframe of the execution of GCF funding.

D1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The programme's overall development impact is an increase and enhance the livelihoods and food security of vulnerable people, communities and regions in seven LDC Sahelian countries through climate-resilient agriculture practices and techniques to increase forest carbon stocks. The programme will contribute to a climate-resilient agricultural value chain accessible to renewable energy sources.

The programme will provide under Component 1: Increased access to ag-climatic information services and early warning systems, the construction of 200 weather stations, the installation of 400 solar-powered weather stations, the installation of 600 rain gauges, by upgrading and modernizing existing hydrological stations (automatic stage recorders) and their specialized equipment, the establishment of 150 climate information service centers, the construction of 200 Agroclimatic Information Service Centers (AISCs), the construction of 500 Agroclimatic Information Service Points (AISP), and the installation of 1000 numerals to grow climate-sensitive crops using agroforestry techniques in forest farming systems. The programme will contribute to a climate-resilient agricultural value chain accessible to renewable energy sources.

#### D.2. Paradigm shift test (max. 50 words, approximately 1 page)

The paradigm shift lies in the programme’s comprehensive approach to building resilience to climate change by changing the way development is planned and implemented. The programme will support a new generation of climate-resilient infrastructure projects that are designed with long-term objectives and the resulting impacts can be sustained, replicated and scaled up to contribute to climate resilient development. This paradigm shift in how development is planned and implemented will create the conditions necessary to sustain impacts beyond project end and build potential for the scaling of all or parts of these approaches across sectors.

The pathway to paradigm shift lies through the design and implementation of a transformational approach, based on the delivery of the following:

- **Transformative planning and programming:** To support a modern and denser network of CIEWS infrastructures, enhanced capacity-building of selected meteorological agencies, technicians and agents and the use of reliable and robust climate information in decision-making (e.g. through the development of climate-resilient infrastructure projects programmes), the programme contributes to the development of more effective NAPs, NDCs, national strategic plans and poverty reduction strategies that are aligned with climate change adaptation priorities. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of public sector institutions for the integration of climate information in strategic planning and decisions made by public and private sector players and beneficiaries in rural farming households.

- **Catalyzing climate innovation:** By using innovations in climate science to underpin sub-projests and establish strategic partnerships with key stakeholders, the programme will support the development of new approaches and technologies that can be replicated in the entire region. Through KM and knowledge building on climate investments, strategic institutional collaboration and the sharing of lessons learned from pilot projects, the programme will contribute to a better impact evaluation, lessons and best practice information future programming.

- **Exemplary project design:** The programme will support the development of exemplary projects that can be replicated in the entire region. Through KM and knowledge building on climate investments, strategic institutional collaboration and the sharing of lessons learned from pilot projects, the programme will contribute to a better impact evaluation, lessons and best practice information future programming.

- **Scaling up investments in climate risk management:** Together with innovative financing insurance products and services from insurance companies and micro-finance institutions, strategic partnerships between the GCF and financial institutions, and other public and private sector actors to support countries in developing models through which they can access international capital markets for investments in climate-resilient agriculture. Ultimately, the programme will support countries in developing models through which they can access international capital markets for investments in climate-resilient agriculture.

- **Leveraging climate capital:** The programme will support countries in developing models through which they can access international capital markets for investments in climate-resilient agriculture. Ultimately, the programme will support countries in developing models through which they can access international capital markets for investments in climate-resilient agriculture.

#### 10.7 The programme will support the ABC, ARB and WFP partners to work with the national governments of the participating countries in implementing the integrated climate risk management model into national budget systems.

An important element of the paradigm shift is the potential to build an environment that enables the private sector's capacity and role to thrive and provide agricultural insurance products to different types of smallholder farmers in the countries. This project will support the development of a market for agricultural insurance by providing technical assistance to the private sector, capacity to design and offer products better suited to smallholder farmers' needs and financial possibilities. The programme's commitment to supporting the private sector will also help farmers better understand agricultural insurance, the differences between products and their potential benefits.

#### 10.8 Pathways for future replication and scale-up include the following:

- **The development of new insurance products:** New insurance products can be replicated in the whole of Africa through this programme.
- **Further development of existing products:** These products can be further developed in the seven countries to include products other than climate-driven risks, such as livestock mortality insurance and other risk transfer mechanisms adapted to the different contexts.

The scaling up will be done through national strategies for financing climate risks. These strategies will build synergies with existing financial instruments and policies of the governments and private sector. The programme will support countries that continue to assume the annual payments of micro-insurance premiums at programme and by establishing institutional arrangements that allow for the gradual transfer of these costs from the programme to the government. This will enable resources from funds with low performance levels in the area of adaptation (GEF and others) to the payment of insurance premiums, thus allowing the countries to gradually assume responsibility for this cost.

The programme will also support the governments of participating countries in developing a national strategy that allows the private sector to better mainstream this instrument into government planning and budgeting.

#### 10.9 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The programme's climate change information and early warning system (CIEWS) will reduce climate-related disaster risk by increasing community preparedness for response and recovery, which is consistent with the SDG target of reducing disaster-related losses in relation to GDP by 2030. The CIEWS will also support climate change adaptation and mitigation. Planned activities focused on restoration and regeneration will generate clear strategic advantages for sustainable development. These include: (i) improved soil fertility; (ii) increased water retention capacity, especially in forests; agroforestry techniques integrated into farming systems over 28,000 ha of selected watersheds; Associated Natural Regeneration (ANR) on 15,000 ha of degraded land; (iv) the establishment of 300 km² of agroforestry and forest-based livelihoods on 60,000 ha of land; and other areas of forestland. Consistent with SDG 13 on climate change, SDG 15 on life on land, and SDG 2 on zero hunger, the programme will support the adoption of agroforestry techniques and other climate-smart agricultural practices that increase soil fertility and water retention capacity.

The programme's activities will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth) by increasing income caused by weather shocks. Furthermore, in various agriculture production and processing industries, farmers are increasingly using agroforestry techniques and other climate-smart agricultural practices that increase soil fertility and water retention capacity.

The programme's activities will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 15 (life on land), as they will help reverse land degradation and bring biodiversity loss, as well as the SDG 6 on Water. Through these activities, the programme will support the development of local contingency plans that will provide livelihoods for smallholder farmers and contribute to the countries' NDCs, thus contributing to SDG 13.

#### 10.9.2 Economic co-benefits

The programme's economic analysis is based on the expectation that the ten-year implementation period will result in a reduction of climate-related disaster risk by 50 percent. The cost of building and operating energy infrastructure will last for 30 to years, and operations and maintenance (O&M) costs are assumed to be around one percent of project value per year.

The cost-benefit analysis shows that with a 10 percent discount rate, the discounted net present value of the project is valued at about US$2.5 billion. The internal rate of return for this base case scenario is 34 percent. Through the internal rate of return is 24 percent for the base case, there are other benefits not captured in this analysis.

176. The Gambia is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change, according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index (ND-GAIN). It ranks 180 out of 193 countries (or the 97 most vulnerable in the world). Regarding its vulnerability to climate change, it has a score of 2.45 out of 10. The Gambia is also one of the least developed countries in Africa. Climate change will have significant impacts on the country’s economy and society. These include changes in rainfall patterns, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, storms, and cyclones. These changes are expected to lead to reduced agricultural productivity, increased food insecurity, and higher costs for water supply and sanitation services.

### D. D. Country ownership (max. 500 words), approximately 1 page)

PCC's Third Assessment Report states that the impacts of climate change to which Chad will be exposed are the loss of life and other major impacts that will affect investment and the economy (RUC, 2016).

### 18.Mali is very highly vulnerable to climate change and variability. Droughts, storms, strong winds and increased temperature have been observed in recent years. The government has developed a national adaptation strategy and mitigation plans, and international organizations have conducted vulnerability assessments and addressed climate-related challenges in the 1990s and have since conducted several vulnerability assessments and prepared action plans. However, significant adaptation needs remain to be addressed, including the need for additional and more accessible water resources, especially in rural areas, as well as improved access to electricity and other energy sources, better management of water resources and coastal resources. Additional funding for the implementation of adaptation plans and strategies is also needed.

### 19.Straddling the Sahara and the Sahel, Mauritania is a vast area of 1,035,700 km2 that is in the advanced phases of a desertification process affecting nearly 75 percent of its territory. Its ecological profile is, therefore, one of extreme vulnerability to climate change. In recent years, rainfall has been erratic and unreliable, with significant variations between wet and dry seasons. The decrease in rainfall has resulted in severe droughts that have affected the livelihoods of millions of people. These droughts also triggered a process of population in rural communities. Formerly nomadic communities either settled in the areas they had abandoned or moved to urban centers. This migration was particularly significant for the regions of Gao, Timbuktu and Nouadhibou, the country's administrative and economic capitals, respectively. The non-mobile and urban environment has also been affected by climate change. In addition to droughts, other extreme weather events have become more frequent (e.g., sandstorms). These have become a major public health concern. Also, air quality in major cities is being degraded by growing numbers of vehicle exhaust emissions.

### 20.Available evidence on Niger shows that between 1950 and 1960, the rainfalls that define the agro-climatic zones shifted 100 to 200 kilometers southwest, following a prolonged period of below average rainfall between 1970 and 1990. Recent analyses of precipitation data show that the mean annual rainfall in Niger has decreased by about 5 percent since 1960. The analysis suggests that the rainfall outflows of 300-400 millimeters, which define the zones where crop production is viable, are shifting northward and westward. This shift has been accompanied by a decrease in the number of rainy days per year (from an average of 125 to about 90) and an increase in the duration of dry spells between rains. The mean annual rainfall in the country since the 1990s has increased, but the 21st century has seen the return of a series of droughts and severe water shortages. These have been accompanied by significant increases in temperatures (from about 35°C to more than 40°C) and early cessation of rains) and intense rain events. There is no strong consensus on future rainfall in the Sahel, but scientists have suggested that the frequency of extreme weather events will increase over time. The climate change impacts are multi-decadal, and projected increases in daily rainfall intensity rather than frequency (Global Security, 2017).

### 15.Senegal is vulnerable to several natural hazards, particularly coastal erosion, droughts, floods and locust invasions. Flooding has been a major problem for the country since the 1980s, with the most recent flood occurring in 2013. The flooding in the Senegar region, caused about USD 540 million in damages to infrastructure and agriculture. Flood risk is exacerbated by rapid urbanization, population growth, and climate change. In addition, the country has experienced a significant increase in temperatures over the past two decades (from an average of 21°C to more than 30°C). This has led to increased evaporation rates and reduced rainfall. The number of days with no rain has increased from about four per year to four years. Since 1980, droughts have affected more than 3 million people (GFDRR, 2017). Climate change is exacerbating the vulnerability of Senegal's coastal areas to erosion and flooding.

### 16.For many years, located adjacent to the coast, the country has not been linked to the national grid. This lack of access to electricity and reduction of transit road are only one effect until the next disaster hits, which also raises the cost of transferring risks. The government is currently working on a plan for the development of renewable energy sources in order to provide power for government, communities and smallholder farmers, together with adaptation investments that reduce climate impacts and increase resilience. This will allow the country to better manage its risks while increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers and economies in the 7 African countries in a sustainable way.

### 13.As shown above all, the countries that will participate in this programme are highly vulnerable to extreme events and climate change, which will have significant impacts on their development. The programme aims at reducing these risks through insurance products. This programme will provide support to the countries based on a broad, integrated approach to climate risk management.

18. The seven implementing countries are parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and have signed and ratified the Kyoto Protocol. By ratifying the UNFCCC, these countries have committed to implementing measures in accordance with their national circumstances that contribute to achieving the long-term objective of the objectives of the three Rio Conventions ratified by all countries, including the UNFCCC; as well as the Paris Climate Agreement.

This programme is fully aligned with the countries' national development plans and their national commitments on climate mitigation and adaptation included in their NAPAs, National Climate Change Policies and Strategies, NDGs, National Communication to the UNFCCC, and other relevant documents. The programme will support the implementation of the seven countries’ climate change strategies (NAPs, NDCs, national strategic plans and country programming), the development of NAPs, NDCs, national strategic plans and country programming. The programme will support the implementation of climate-resilient measures in the seven proposed countries as the programme contributes to the public and private sector and smallholder farmers.

Burkina Faso - 29/6/15

An unconditional pledge to reduce emissions by 6% below business-as-usual levels by 2030, with a 40% conditional reduction on international support. Includes interim phase targets for 2020 and 2025, taking the total reductions up to a potential 55.15% below business-as-usual levels. Burkina Faso’s pledge is conditional upon international support.

Chad - Chad - 29/6/15

Unconditional pledge to reduce emissions by 18.2% by 2030, compared to a business-as-usual scenario. Includes interim phase targets for 2020 and 2025. The pledge is conditional upon international support. Include action on adaptation, including areas of priority in the country.

The Gambia - 29/6/15

A 44% emissions cut by 2030, compared to business-as-usual projections, and a 45% cut by 2030. This is conditional upon international support, although around 40% of this can be met domestically through the implementation of existing policies and measures. The remaining 6% emissions reduction targets are unconditional. The rest can be conditional upon international financial resources. See section on adaptation.

Mali - Mali - 29/6/15

Commits to reducing emissions by 29% for agriculture, 31% for energy and 21% for forests and land use, with a 40% conditional reduction on international support. This is an unconditional pledge of 27%. This is conditional upon international support, although around 40% of this can be met domestically through the implementation of existing policies and measures.

Mauritania - Mauritania - 29/6/15

A 22.3% cut in emissions below business-as-usual levels by 2030. 88% of this pledge is conditional upon international support and 12% is unconditional. This will avoid 73.56 million tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) with further information on adaptation.

Niger - Niger - 29/6/15

An unconditional 0.5% reduction in emissions by 2030, compared to a business-as-usual scenario, or a 24-28% cut below the baseline of 2010 with the condition of receiving international support. Section on action.

Senegal - Senegal - 29/6/15

An unconditional reduction in emissions by 9% by 2030, compared to business-as-usual levels,

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 88 OF 99

#### D

**107.** Addressing climate change risks in the seven selected countries effectively requires action at both local and national levels, as well as international support. The project will build on the lessons learned from the first phase of the IFAD mission carried out during the IFAD new design phase in 2019 and gaps in IFAD investments have been identified and addressed through a regional programme. This programme is being implemented by the Government of Ethiopia, with technical assistance provided by the World Bank. The project will be implemented under the guidance of the Ethiopian authorities in 2019 during the design phase of a new investment programme, this regional programme was endorsed by all CGI countries and will be implemented as part of the CGI portfolio.

**D.E Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately / page)**

**108.** GCF financing will overcome the existing barriers to promoting integrated climate risk management and access to finance, including lack of capacity for climate change adaptation planning, programming, and implementation. The linkages between climate risk reduction and risk transfer will lead to transformational planning and programming. Additionally, new innovations in climate science and risk assessment methodologies will be developed and disseminated through the CGI programme. The CGI programme will also support strategic institutional collaboration; the programme will be established as a knowledge broker to scale up and replicate. Finally, the CGI programme will provide technical assistance for countries to develop national adaptation platforms built to help define and assess key barriers to technology diffusion, determine appropriate policy mix for optimizing the use of the climate capital, select the best financing options to create an enabling policy environment to implement the said policy mix.

**109.** Additionally, climate preparedness and climate risk reduction measures are being implemented with good results in several CGI countries. The CGI programme will build on these experiences by providing technical assistance for the implementation of IFAD projects. However, policies are still expensive and the limited offer of insurance products does not stimulate high agricultural productivity. In addition, there is a need to address the issue of climate change adaptation financing at the sovereign disaster risk into their national budgets. Early results from these programmes are highly encouraging, although they have been hampered by the lack of adequate funding for implementation. The CGI programme will support climate risk adaptation and mitigation and climate risk transfer is considered innovative and transformational in the Sahel region. In addition to the CGI programme, IFAD has also supported several CGI countries with the development of a portfolio of projects that are designed to address climate change risks. These projects include the promotion of smallholder farmers, IFAD, AFDB, and the selected countries believe that the total proposed GCF grant (US$822 million) will be sufficient for the implementation of these projects.

**109.** The CGI programme will also provide technical assistance to countries in the design phase of their CGI portfolio. This will include the encouragement bilateral lending organizations to adopt competitive rates, thus reducing the risk of crowding out the market. All participating countries are expected to develop a national adaptation platform that will be used as an enabling tool for the adoption and scaling-up of the implementation of climate resilient agriculture. Through this programme, countries are expected to develop a regulatory framework that is conducive to sustaining this programme beyond its lifespan.

**109.** Regarding the microinsurance component, the premium structure and level of concessionality will depend on the index scores and levels of poverty in each CGI country. The CGI programme will provide technical assistance for the development of the product pricing and the regulation for the different levels of commissions to ensure that the pricing is fair and remains affordable to the beneficiaries.

## 1.6. Support integrated climate risk management in smallholder farming communities across the countries

The programme will facilitate at the country level, through a series of workshops with regional bodies such as ACMAO and AGRINET (Climate Outlook Forum) on best practices for crop complementarities and diversification, including products and earnings, kilo per hectare yields, market prices and earnings, dissemination and dissemination.

### 1.6.1. Offer training to farmers on the timely use of early warning systems (agro-climatic information)

The programme will facilitate at the country level through a series of workshops with regional bodies such as ACMAO and AGRINET (Climate Outlook Forum) on best practices for crop complementarities and diversification, including products and earnings, kilo per hectare yields, market prices and earnings. Kilo per hectare yields, market prices and earnings will be disseminated through the following channels: extension services, farmer field schools, farmers' organizations, local media, extension agents, and dissemination.

### 1.6.2. Raise awareness among smallholder farming communities on the best practices for climate adaptation/compensation and weather index insurance

The programme will organize in each country county training for extension agents on integrated climate risk management (100 per country).

### 1.6.3. Train 750 extension agents on integrated climate risk management

The programme will organize in each country county training for extension agents on integrated climate risk management (100 per country).

### 1.6.4. Conduct gender and youth impact analyses

The programme will support the conduct of gender and youth impact analyses and dissemination of results to 750,000 farmers.

### 1.6.5. Provide training for extension agents on integrated climate risk management in smallholder farming communities across the countries

The programme will organize in each country county training for extension agents on integrated climate risk management (100 per country).

### 1.6.6. Support the integration of integrated climate risk management into SMI or SMIs of farmers' organizations

The programme will support the integration of integrated climate risk management into SMI or SMIs of farmers' organizations.

### 1.7. Support 3 digital services for each country to detect key weather, finance and food safety surveillance and information and support premium

The programme will support the use of the mobile channel to deliver key weather, finance and food safety surveillance and information and support premium

## 3.2.3 Review and assess the ARC agricultural and drought model
Currently, the ARC agricultural and drought model requires further development and testing before it will be undertaken through this activity.

### 3.2.4 Build the capacity of 1000 farmers' organizations and cooperatives to identify existing disaster risks and select the best insurance options for farmers.
The programme will support activities that will build the capacity of 1000 farmers' organizations and cooperatives in each country to identify existing disaster risks and select the best insurance options for farmers.

### 3.2.5 Develop tools and financing instrument to manage disaster risks estimating the frequency, intensity and severity of the risks.
The programme will support the development of tools and financing instruments that will help farmers' organizations and cooperatives quantify existing disaster risks in terms of their frequency (of the risks) and intensity/severity.

### 3.2.6 Develop a national integrated climate change risk management plan with comprehensive layering approach
The programme will support the development of a national integrated climate change risk management plan with a comprehensive layering approach.

### 3.2.7 Provide technical support to 10 countries to develop and implement a climate-based insurance (CBI) or national climate* disaster insurance*
The programme will provide technical support to 10 countries to develop and implement a climate-based insurance (CBI) or national climate* disaster insurance with resources from national climate* contingency funds.

### 3.2.8 Develop Climate finance frameworks
The programme will support the development of climate finance frameworks.

#### 3.2.9 Strengthen coordination mechanisms between micro, meso and macro insurance industry and financial institutions to reach the end users, using lessons learned from the shadow report from the civil society.
The programme will strengthen institutional and coordination mechanisms between micro, meso and macro insurance industry and financial institutions to ensure the effective use of insurance products by end-users with citizens in each country. The programme will align and map linkages with the existing mechanisms.

#### 3.2.10 Organize events and workshops with micro and macro insurance industry and financial institutions
The programme will support the organization of events and workshops with micro and macro insurance industry and financial institutions to ensure the effective use of the pay-outs by end-users as they reach and interact with citizens in each country (shadow report from the civil society). The programme will align and map linkages with the existing mechanisms.

## Selected Risk Factor: P - Political & Insecurity
### Category: Social
**Probability:** High
**Impact:** High

- 173 Description: Immorality, conflicts, political
- 174 Mitigation measures: Countries facing conflicts are Mali, Niger, Burkina and coup (Mali). The sites selected for the programme in these countries will be reviewed to ensure that security is maintained. Security audits will be undertaken at all times to ensure the safety of the intervention sites that will be taken into account during the evaluation mission. For Mali, the restriction and ban on travel by foreigners is underway.

## Selected Risk Factor: R - COVID-19
### Category: Social
**Probability:** High
**Impact:** Medium | High

- 175 Description: COVID-19 which could also affect the fiscal space and countries' ability to pay their premiums under Output 3.2.
- 176 Mitigation measures: All countries are affected by COVID-19 and have put in place some specific measures to address the impact of this pandemic on their economies, including economic plan, requirements for social distancing, self-isolation and more conventional measures such as access to financial resources. Self-isolation practices will be implemented during the programme.

### Category: Market Risk
**Probability:** Medium
**Impact:** Medium | Impact

- 173 Description: Market risk - Risk of demand for agricultural insurance, climate information services and adaptation/ mitigation technologies being lower than expected.
- 174 Mitigation measures: Specific studies have been developed by WFP. IFAD PARM to assess the market needs and demand for these products and services in each country. The programme will also work with countries to ensure that demand is not given the impact of climate change on the agricultural sector. Based on the feasibility study and country demand, a specific marketing plan will be developed. A dedicated team of experts will further develop during the programme to assess additional market demand and supply by providers.
- 175 Description: Market risk - Risk of demand for agricultural insurance, climate information services and adaptation/ mitigation technologies being lower than expected.

### Category: Technology risk
**Probability:** Medium
**Impact:** Medium | Impact

- 173 Description: The selected countries have the capacity to procure, install and maintain the required information infrastructures under component 1, as well as all activities under component 2 related to renewable energy technologies.
- 174 Mitigation measures: The programme plans to implement CIEWS infrastructure such as automatic weather stations, solar-powered weather stations, satellite imagery, etc. in each country. A specific plan will be developed for the installation of these technological risks, a plan carried by countries (EE) is work with their MET agencies, which will procure, install and maintain the required information infrastructures under component 1, as well as all activities under component 2 related to renewable energy technologies.
- 175 Description: The selected countries have the capacity to procure, install and maintain the required information infrastructures under component 1, as well as all activities under component 2 related to renewable energy technologies.

### Category: Regulatory risk
**Probability:** Medium
**Impact:** Medium

- 173 Description: Mitigation measures in regulatory areas that could affect the programmes or private actors engaged in the programme in various ways, including with contracts.
- 174 Mitigation measures: In the selected countries, regulatory risks are low to medium and the programme proposes a series of actions to mitigate these risks. The programme will work closely with the relevant authorities to ensure that all necessary approvals are obtained within the programme timeline while supporting countries enhance their regulatory frameworks to support proper programme implementation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 184. Across the African continent, women are the backbone of the development of rural and national economies as they make up almost 60 percent of the agricultural labour force in a figure which rises to 70 percent in some countries. They work primarily on small plots of land, often with limited access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, tools, water resources and information (SOCR, ADB). In general, women also have less access to jobs compared outside the agricultural sector. They represent only 6 percent of entrepreneurs. They are mainly concentrated in traditional agriculture and livestock production, which are more vulnerable to climate change than other sectors. Women are also linked to other widespread poverty that affects both women and men. The vulnerability of women and girls in conflict and post-conflict situations is often exacerbated by the lack of access to resources and services. This is reflected in religious and customary social standards at all levels of society, from the family up to the highest political levels.

### 185. Equity between women and men is a condition for the effectiveness of public policies and sustainable and equitable human development. The GCF will support the implementation of gender equality as part of its Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme. Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change involves addressing gender inequalities in access to resources, services and information. This includes services which guide adaptation/innovation techniques and choice of technologies as well as the design of microinsurance schemes that are sensitive to gender differences. The GCF will support the development of gender-sensitive action adopted, climate-resilient investment options. (b) Better understanding and access to risk transfer insurance schemes for women and men in rural areas, including through the relevant participation of women in coordination mechanisms, programme management and decision-making spaces.

### 186. At the onset of the project, an inception gender analysis will be undertaken to collect additional information required for the implementation of the project. The GCF will support the development of a gender action plan as part of its Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme. Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change involves addressing gender inequalities in access to resources, services and information. This includes services which guide adaptation/innovation techniques and choice of technologies as well as the design of microinsurance schemes that are sensitive to gender differences.

### 187. Improving the knowledge and hence the capacity of women is a key component of the programme and women will have a key role in its implementation. See Annex G: Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## G3. Financial Management and Procurement

### 188. IFAD-based PMUs will be in charge of financial management and procurement on GCF funds at country level. The ROU under each PMU will be responsible for consolidating financial reports to meet GCF and IFAD requirements. If PM is not established, the PMU will be responsible for the overall financial management and procurement.

### 189. In addition, a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) will be signed in the same terms as those granted by the GCF: (a) each IFAD-based PMU and ROU will be equipped with the same accounting software; (b) a dedicated focal point for financial management at the regional coordination unit who are working on financial consolidation and financial oversight of national PMUs.

### 190. The MoU signed by the national PMAs with implementing partners will include clear financial reporting requirements and procedures, including the use of the same accounting software as that used in the IFAD-based PMU. The terms of each of the following tranches will be conditional to the respect of operational and reporting standards which will be clearly described in the MoU signed by the national PMAs with implementing partners. These conditions will ensure high quality and provide coordination among countries.


---
**Page 92**
---

103. The financial and administrative manual of each VAFs baseline PAU will be updated to establish specific policies and reporting requirements for the management of GCF funds.

102. A specific designated and operational account will be opened to exclusively receive GCF funds in order to avoid mingling of GCF funds with other funds.

The financial statements of each national PNU will be audited on an annual basis and a specific audit opinion will be provided on the usage and accounting of GCF funds. The audit will be carried out by independent auditing companies and the results will be submitted to the GCF Secretariat within 30 days after the end of the financial year.

In addition to the financial statements, APRs will also need to be submitted to the GCF per paragraph 15(a) (i) of the AMA.

As part of standard FAD supervision missions, which will take place at least once every four years for each national PNU, a Financial Management Supervision Mission will be conducted in order to assess the level of financial management and the solidity of internal control systems in place.


The IFAD handling guidelines based on the Procurement Guidelines to be followed in the procurement of goods, works and services under IFAD financed loans and grants are as follows:

* grant aid projects: The Borrower is responsible for ensuring that all procurements are conducted transparently and to help ensure that Borrower recipients can handle procurement actions using IFAC procedures.
* FAD Borrower recipients on how to handle procurement actions using IFAC procedures.

The IFAD Procurement Guidelines require the implementation of the provisions made in applying the Borrower's recipient's procurement regulations, provided such regulations are consistent with the IFAD Procurement Guidelines. The Borrower's recipient is required to implement the provisions made in applying the Borrower's recipient's procurement regulations, provided such regulations are consistent with the IFAD Procurement Guidelines.

The IFAD Procurement Guidelines were revised and updated in April 2009 and act as a principal reference in situations where it is deemed that Borrower's recipient's procurement regulations do not accept or comply with IFAD Procurement Guidelines. More detail are compiled in the IFAD guidelines.


With regards to the procurement implementation and due diligence arrangement, procurement of goods, works and services under IFAD financed loans and grants shall be conducted in accordance with the Borrower's recipient's procurement regulations, to the extent such are consistent with the IFAD Procurement Guidelines. Each Procurement Plan shall be submitted to the IFAD for approval within 30 days after its adoption by the Borrower's recipient.

The Borrower's recipient is required to implement the provisions made in applying the Borrower's recipient's procurement regulations, provided such regulations are consistent with the IFAD Procurement Guidelines. By notice to the recipient, the IFAD may require that all bidding documents and contracts for procurements of goods, works or services under IFAD financed loans and grants be submitted to it.

Contractors, sub-contractors and consultants: To the notice by the recipient, the IFAD may require that all bidding documents and contracts for procurements of goods, works or services under IFAD financed loans and grants include a reference by the Financing include provisions requiring bidders, suppliers, contractors, sub-contractors and consultants


G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to any party without the need for prior written or verbal confidentiality.

With confidential information: The accredited entity decides that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed to third parties. In this case, the funding proposal and all annexes must be marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said positions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.


The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

    - Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) or

- Annex 9: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation and insurance)
- Annex 10: Risk assessment (template provided)

- Annex 16: Map(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions
- Annex 17: Multi-country project/programme information (template provided)

- Annex 19: Project implementation plan prepared by third parties or consulting entities undertaking projects financed for the entity
- Annex 20: Project M&FIT (Y/N) assessment
- Annex 21: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- Annex x: Other references

SECRETARIAT EXECUTIF

**Niamey, 15th October 2020**

To The Focal Point

The Green Climate Fund ("GCF")

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by The International Fund For Agricultural Development (IFAD) regarding "The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme : Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW)"

Dear Yanerrick Glamanou,

Executive Director of the Green Climate Fund, We refer to The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) in Niger as included in the funding proposal submitted by IFAD to us on 12 February 2020.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority/Focal point of the Republic of Niger. Pursuant to GCF decision B/08/I0, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of the Republic of Niger has no objection to The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience


---
**Page 102**
---

of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) as included in the funding proposal.

(b) The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with The Niger’s national priorities, strategies and plans.

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining non-objective to The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW) as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no-objective applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.


Kind regards,

Dear Sir,

We refer to “the Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the Resilience of Smallholder Farmers to Climate Change Impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW)” as included in the funding proposal submitted by IFAD to us on 11 February 2020.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority of Senegal.

Pursuant to Article 48(3) of the GCF Bylaws, the undersigned hereby formally and explicitly communicates our no objection to “the Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building The Resilience of Smallholder Farmers to Climate Change Impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW)” as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of Senegal has no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The Programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Senegal’s national priorities, strategies and policies; and

(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our no objection process for ascertaining no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the Programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.
