# Secretariat’s assessment of FP162

## Proposal name:
The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW)

## Accredited entity:
International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

## Country(ies):
Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and the Gambia

## Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| The programme implements an integrated approach (e.g., climate information and early warning systems), risk reduction measures, and disaster insurance (mouane) across a highly climate-vulnerable region (western Sahel countries and populations of pastoralists). | While accredited entities (AE) and executing entities (EE) are involved in collaborating in joint programmes, the range of programme activities strong over implementation period. |
| The AE leverages the expertise of a panel of EE and implementing partners to develop a complex programme at sizable scale using the financial and technical resources of a range of donors and stakeholders across region. | Lessons learned from programme implementation will need to be accessible to and well integrated in future projects. |

The programme makes a significant contribution to building resilience against climate risk, domestic re/insurance markets in the Sahel, bringing innovative instruments (index-based insurance, micro-insurance, agro-pastoralists and governments).

The programme invests in institutions and builds capacity for planning, designing and implementing ex-ante risk financing interventions.

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations," respectively.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment


---
**Page 113**
---

1. **Project background**

The objective of this programme is to build, strengthen and scale up the resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and rural communities in Sahelian least developed countries (LDCs) – Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and the Gambia – to climate change using an integrated climate risk management approach. The project will support governments in developing national climate risk management strategies that include climate risk transfer by means of insurance mechanisms, at the micro- and sovereign levels. In relation to preparedness for extreme weather events, the project will support governments in developing climate information and early warning systems (CIWS) that provide robust climate data to government decision-makers and other stakeholders. The project will also support farmers with more informed decisions and adopt effective preventive and adaptive measures to reduce the risks and impacts of climate change and extreme weather events.

The project will support countries in dealing with climate risk reduction measures aimed at enabling farmers to adopt the best climate adaptation and mitigation practices, while supporting governments in developing national strategies for promoting effective and sustainable agricultural and resource management practices in their respective area’s climate context.

The project will also support the development of climate insurance programmes, which are a cost-efficient and effective way of offering a faster response to disaster and losses of crops and livestock. The project will support governments in developing national strategies for reducing and distributing climate risk among stakeholders in the insurance market. Under its third component, the project will support governments in increasing access to micro-insurance schemes in the seven Sahel countries and provides support to governments to utilize sovereign risk insurance to bolster their ability to finance recovery following climate disasters, particularly droughts. The project will also support governments in developing national strategies for climate risk insurance on the African continent and bring insurance products and instruments to countries that are not yet able to access micro-insurance or sovereign risk insurance or other financing instruments available following climate events.

As a cross-cutting mitigation and adaptation programme, this project will reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting sustainable agricultural value chains through the adoption of renewable energy technologies (RETs) for water mobilization and power production. The project will support governments in developing national strategies to diminish deforestation, desertification and smoke from the use of firewood, while improving health, food security and livelihoods. By supporting governments in implementing improved land management and land use, the programme will support the efforts of the countries of the Sahel region towards meeting their commitments under the Paris Agreement on climate change development pathways in agriculture, as expressed in their nationally determined contributions (NDCs) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The project will reduce 27.572 kilotons of carbon dioxide equivalent (KtCO2e) Mauritania: QGtCO2e and Niger and Senegal: 1.5 GtCO2e per year, respectively. The Gambia pledged to achieve a conditional reduction of 17504 GtCO2e.

The accredited entity (AE) for the programme is IFAD. Executing entities (EEs) are the African Union Commission (AUC), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the World Food Programme (WFP) and hydro-meteorological agencies in each of the seven Sahel countries. The environment and social safeguard category is B.

2.2 **Component-by-component analysis**


---
**Page 114**
---

## The programme seeks GCF funding of USD 82.05 million for a programme of activities with a total cost of USD 143.33 million implemented across six years in seven Sahel countries. The programme reaches 817,922 direct beneficiaries and 5.33 million indirect beneficiaries. The estimated impact (20-year) mitigation impact is of 21.45 million tonnes of CO₂ equivalent (tCO₂eq).

### The programme has three components as further described below:

#### Component 1: Climate risk preparedness
- Total cost: USD 79.83 million; GCF cost: USD 19.28 million

The main outcomes of this component are strengthened climate weather information services, improved access to early warning systems for agriculture, livestock farming, agricultural insurance products and services, and capacity development for farmers, government and the private sector. The component has two outputs:

- Output 1.1: Strengthening climate weather information services and early warning infrastructure to support integrated climate risk management (EE: ARDB, ABR, and hydro-meteorological agencies)
- Output 1.2: Improving access to insurance products and services and capacity building and institutional development on integrated climate risk management (EE: ARDB)

#### Component 2: Climate risk reduction
- Total cost: USD 40.22 million; GCF cost: USD 23.15 million

The component will strengthen climate-resilient agroforestry and pastoral value chains powered by reliable and affordable renewable energy sources. Reliable climate information generated through the early warning system will be used to support the adoption of adaptation/mitigation techniques, including forestry and land-use practices and technologies; and (ii) improved access to insurance products and services for farmers in targeted areas.

Under this component, the programme will focus on the selection and implementation of adaptation measures under Output 2.1, while Output 2.2 supports the shift towards climate resilient agroforestry and pastoral value chains powered by renewable energy sources in the targeted areas, which involves the adoption of on-farm practices and technologies to improve resilience.

- Output 2.1: Best available technologies for adaptation/mitigation (forest and land use; renewable energy) adopted and implemented with agricultural insurance schemes (EEs: Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger)
- Output 2.2: Diversified livelihoods through the promotion of income-generating activities powered with renewable energy (EEs: Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger; Senegal; and Togo). The programme will leverage all existing GCF investments and the IFAD CS+1 Sahel regional programme.

#### Component 3: Climate risk transfer
- Total cost: USD 60.15 million; GCF cost: USD 38.08 million; ARDB contribution: USD 24.97 million; ABR financing: USD 7.24 million

Component 3 promotes climate risk transfer mechanisms at both the micro and macro levels to reduce vulnerability of smallholder farmers in targeted areas. The objective is to reduce obstacles (ill-adapted financial market products and services/high premiums) to governments and improved access for smallholder farmers to agricultural insurance to enhance their resilience against climate risks. This component will leverage the insurance premium generated through the climate networks and early warning systems expanded and upgraded under Component 1 to support the agricultural insurance industry. ### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

The project scores well against GCF investment criteria, as further described below.

#### 3.1 Impact potential

**Scale: High**

The proposal has provided an extensive assessment of historical climate variability and change on agricultural impacts. This evidence points to recurrent droughts, desiccation, declining rainfall, locusts and famine, with limited capacity and appropriate approaches to adapt. Interactions of climate hazards with vulnerable social conditions has resulted in famine.


---
**Page 116**
---

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

The proposal has articulated how low capacity of smallholder farmers to adapt to climate variability and change is undermining the unsustainable and negative practices that render them highly vulnerable to climate variability and change.

The proposal also clearly identifies the key barriers and root causes that must be addressed (see table 9 in the funding proposal) and alternative solutions through GCF investments.

### 3.2.1 Innovation

The proposal seeks to address these challenges by strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for climate responsive planning and development (performance measurement framework [PMF] v5.0 and related indicator[s]) through capacity-building activities that will increase country-level knowledge on (i) early warning systems; (ii) contingency plans; and (iii) disaster risk management. The proposal has detailed the potential for expanding the scale and impact of the proposed interventions and explicitly identified how they can be scaled up to national level, including climate change adaptation and management and gender-sensitive climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies; and increased access to finance for smallholder farmers through improved value chain development in production and processing. It will also promote the generation and use of input-based information systems (IBIS) at the regional/national scale. The proposal seeks to strengthen PMF v5.0 and related indicator[s] through capacity-building and technology transfer. The blending of the ADAPT, ADB, ABC, IFAD and WFP 84 Bill Harms Initiative at the regional/national scale with local micro-insurance schemes constitutes a unique multi-scale innovation.

---

29 If successful, this proposal will enable the scaling up of the approach at the continental scale and foster climate resilience and adaptation at scale.

16 The proposal contains significant capacity development and technology transfer interventions that seek to effect long-term behavioural change to ensure the improved utilization of natural resources and the preservation of the environment through climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices. It also seeks to build on existing CSA initiatives in the region, as an approach that is also innovative for the country/regions.

17 The proposal will support the creation of a regional international knowledge sharing hub between organizations partnering in the project. However, this will benefit from a broader coalition of partners in the region undertaking knowledge brokering in early warning and early action. This will broaden and strengthen mechanisms to enhance coherence between climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) through cooperation between partner organizations responsible for implementing the activities.

Interactions of these climate hazards with vulnerable social conditions has resulted in famine, food insecurity, and poverty, so the predominantly rain-fed agriculture has been compromised.

This is expected to reduce poverty and vulnerability.

### Expected positive social and health impacts

#### 3.3.2. Expected positive social and health impacts

**a:** Historically, pastoralists maintained productivity by migrating herds south during the dry season (October to June) and north during the wet season, thereby exploiting grazing and water resources in areas outside traditional migration corridors.

**b:** While farmers and pastoralists historically worked together (with pastoralists benefiting from grazing of crop residue and farmers benefiting from manure droppings), growing competition for land and water resources has led to conflicts between these groups. Land degradation and competition over water and land resources (Grain, Livestock, Water) are also contributing significantly to competition. Malian refugees have three times as many livestock than locals, increasing the likelihood of competition over land and water resources.


---
**Page 119**
---

### Expected positive economic impacts

The proposal is based on a cost-benefit analysis, showing a discounted net present value of USD90 million over a 15-year period at discount rate of 10 per cent. The economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 24 per cent exceeds the discount rate of 10 per cent, demonstrating the added value of the intervention. The analysis shows that: [i] total benefits increase by 38 per cent; [ii] total costs decrease by 20 per cent and; [iii] total cost increase of 20 per cent out of which total benefits increase of 15 per cent. The proposal has the potential for green job creation and can unlock opportunities for women and youth livelihoods, particularly in rural areas where they are more likely to be employed.

By creating and enabling access to robust climate services, forecast-based financing and insurance schemes, the beneficiary community livelihoods will be made more resilient with protection against climate-related shocks and stresses.

The proposal implementation period is six years, but it is expected to have impacts over 20 years. The hydromet systems and water, land and energy infrastructures are also expected to last beyond this time frame. The economic benefits of the proposed interventions can be expected to have further impacts on economies and the evolution of financial systems in the target countries.

The operations and maintenance costs are estimated to be 1 per cent of programme investments and will be covered by governments'/hydromet agencies' budgets after the programme.

The proposal has indicated that there are additional potential benefits that have not been quantified. Given the benefits the proposal presents, it will benefit from a detailed analysis of the financial impacts on the national economy and the wider region. This will be undertaken together with the recommended input evaluation exercise during project implementation.

It should also explore potential long-term sources of funding and technical assistance for the hydromet systems and water, land and energy infrastructures through the World Meteorological Organization-led Global Basic Observing Network, the Systematic Observation Financing Facility and the World Bank's Climate Resilience Fund.

### Potential to reduce gender inequalities in climate change impacts and/or equal participation by gender groups in contributing to expected outcomes

Women constitute about 70 per cent of the workforce in the agricultural sector and face barriers to accessing resources. The proposal will contribute to reducing these barriers through a gender focus as 50% beneficiaries of interventions are women, and thereby targets align with SDG 5 (gender equality). The proposal also strengthen the technical and managerial capacities of women in hydromet services and climate change adaptation and mitigation activities so that ensure women are active participants in the governance and decision-making process by giving them equal representation on the National Steering Committee.

**Page 120**

3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

The seven participant countries are LDCs that are highly exposed to climate risks. They also have a significant debt burden and limited access to grant finance for climate action.

Increasing evidence of climate-related hazards, namely prolonged and intense droughts, rainstorms, and heatwaves, has led to more frequent and severe disasters in these countries over the past decades. If these were not problematic enough, climate change is expected to exacerbate these hazards further, creating unprecedented disasters over the next few decades. There is no consensus among climate models on future rainfall projections for these countries, which adds another layer of uncertainty to this region, creating uncertainty in understanding how global warming will impact rainfall and drought.

Based on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) Country Index, the participant countries are ranked as having a high risk of food insecurity and fragile socio-economic conditions. With the projected high demographic growth, competition for the climate-induced natural resource scarcity will lead to food insecurity and fragile socio-economic conditions. Climate-induced migration is expected to increase in these countries, putting urban and rural farming communities at envisage to increase as climate change gathers pace.

The proposed interventions are aligned with the national strategies to address climate change and human security challenges. The interventions are aligned with the local, national regional and global development strategies, in particular the NDCs, NAPs, SIDS, regional integration and trade strategies, as well as the African Union Agenda 2063.

**Scale: High**

3.5 Country ownership

In this programme, the interventions are aligned with the countries' national strategic and development plans, NAPs, NQGs and related commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. It is also aligned with the 2015 global agendas (SDG), Africa Agenda 2063, the Ecowas Development Strategy, the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) regional framework for climate services and the Great Green Wall Initiative.

The interventions are designed to ensure that there will be no duplication of efforts or overlaps with other instruments. The project revenue would not be able to cover the cost. The co-financing ratio of 1:37 is intended to leverage insurance capital via IIGC, the private sector and government funding.


**Scale: Medium to high**

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposal has requested a grant of US$280.45 million from the GCF. Given the level of poverty in these countries and their limited fiscal bandwidth for the proposed co-borrowing on loans, the grant instrument is appropriate for the interventions. The GCF investment is envisaged to unlock barriers to the adoption of innovative policies and technologies.

The project revenue would not be able to cover the cost by other instruments (e.g., debt). The project revenue would not be able to cover the cost. The co-financing ratio of 1:37 is intended to leverage insurance capital via IIGC, the private sector and government funding.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The programme intends to scale up the resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers in the project area through improved agricultural practices. It aims to improve risk preparedness by building the capacity of governments and communities, reduce risks by improving agricultural productivity, and promote climate risk insurance schemes.

The AE has assessed the programme against the GCF environmental and social safeguards. The Secretariat confirms the category B classification given that the programme is likely to have significant adverse impacts on people and/or social risks and impacts that, individually or cumulatively, are few, generally site-specific, and can be addressed through implementation measures.

The programme will be implemented over a large geographic area for which specific locations have not yet been identified; therefore an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) has been developed. The ESMF is included in the GCF Information Disclosure Document. It provides guidance on how to assess potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the programme, identify measures that can be taken to avoid or minimize adverse impacts, and mitigate any adverse impacts that the programme may have on people and the environment.

The ESMF includes a screening form for assessing the environmental and social risk screening form, which is included in the ESMF. Additionally, subprojects will be analyzed to ensure they do not contain elements on the exclusion list. For subprojects categorized as category B, an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will be prepared and disclosed in line with the GCF Information Disclosure Policy. An outline of a typical environmental and social management plan is provided.

The ESMF lists potential positive and negative impacts associated with the programme. The AE has assessed the programme as having an overall positive impact if risks are mitigated and monitored, including: improved food security; increased income from livestock diversification; improved access to finance and markets; improved access to land and resources for smallholder farmers; improved agricultural productivity through investment that creates opportunities for entrepreneurship and income generation; and food security to alleviate poverty. The AE has also assessed the programme as having a positive impact overall, for example due to agro-forestry, reforestation and the assisted natural regeneration of trees.

Potential minimal negative impacts are expected from land clearing owing to the rehabilitation

Benefits expected from the programme’s implementation include access to land for agricultural production, improved water supply and sanitation, rehabilitation of water points, and access to renewable energy by households from mini-grids and off-grid systems.
The AE has provided a programme-level gender action plan that contains activities, programme components, performance indicators, sex-disaggregated targets with baselines, timelines, and responsible parties. The gender action plan will be prepared prior to implementation. Benefits expected from the programme’s implementation include access to land for agricultural production, improved water supply and sanitation, rehabilitation of water points, and access to renewable energy by households from mini-grids and off-grid systems.
For example, women lack land tenure security for agricultural activities, which limits their ability to secure credit or loans and financing to invest in farms. This is despite the fact that women perform most of the labour for traditional agriculture. Women also have limited access to training services and information on climate information that would allow them to make better decisions on adapting to the effects of climate change.
In addition, many women in the beneficiary countries are still burdened with tasks such as gathering wood for fuel. Wood is still the main source of fuel for cooking purposes for many households, with low access to rural areas having access to electricity. This leads to deforestation and soil degradation, which increases the distances that women have to walk to find wood and therefore contribute to time poverty. In addition, women are responsible for collecting water from distant sources.
The AE has provided a programme-level gender action plan that contains activities, programme components, performance indicators, sex-disaggregated targets with baselines, timelines, and responsible parties. # 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The programme aims to contribute to a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient and low-emission agriculture through the mobilization of climate finance to strengthen adaptation measures in the agricultural sector, with a focus on smallholder farmers. The project will also reduce risks to the insurance industry. The programme consists of three components: (i) climate risk preparedness; (ii) climate risk reduction; and (iii) climate risk transfer. Total project costs are USD 143 million, of which USD 90 million is being provided by ADB. The lead countries are LDCs in Africa. Co-financing in the form of grants of USD 30 million and USD 23 million will be provided by DFAT and AIDB, respectively. In-kind co-financing of USD 7.2 million will be provided by ADB.

## 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)


---
**Page 125**
---

### Program-specific execution risks (medium risk)

The AE has determined that upgrading for climate information services and adaptation/mitigation solutions would result in lower impacts for the programme. Comfort is derived from market studies conducted by AE, IPAF and WFP.

- **Technical:** The AE will develop, deploying, expanding, and upgrading existing early warning systems and infrastructure such as mini-grids. Project success will depend on the AE's ability to ensure that the new systems are fully operational in order to ensure its continued use throughout the lifespan of the project. Comfort can be derived from capacity development provided to the countries' meteorological agencies and agreements signed with these agencies.

- **Political and security risk:** The AE has identified risks relating to insecurity, conflict, local government instability, and corruption. These risks are mitigated by the track record of the AE and EEs operating in the region. COVID-19 could affect the fiscal space of countries and their ability to pay premiums under outcome 3.2.

- **Market risk:** The AE has identified that it will need to undertake to develop and support the microinsurance market, including premium support for beneficiaries. Success under this component will be driven by continued availability of affordable insurance products


---

---

and beneficiaries' willingness to pay during and after premium support is provided. Comfort is derived from the feasibility studies conducted, product and market development activities to be undertaken under this component, experience of the EEs in the sector and the involvement of the EEs in the project implementation. The insurance products are expected to be affordable.

The activities under component 2 supported by the microinsurance under component 3.1 are expected to have revenue generation potential as a result of increases in crop yields. All contracts will be subject to the terms and conditions set out in the policy documents with narrow focal space. Furthermore, the use of grants is supported by the target beneficiaries: small producers, subsistence farmers, marginalized communities and young people. The term sheet contains provisions to enter unconsciously for microinsurance shall be paid on to the end beneficiaries.

### Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 24 September 2018, and it became effective on 9 November 2018.

The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme. It is recommended, prior to the submission of the funding proposal to the Board, that (a) the AE has obtained all necessary approvals; and (b) the Secretariat sends a revised draft agreement with the AE in form and substance satisfactory to GCF confirming that all final internal approvals by the AE have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement the programme.

The proposed programme will be implemented in seven countries in which GCF is not provided for under national law. The Secretariat has confirmed that these countries are protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, and the associated risks need to be handled by the AE.

Barkhisa Faino: The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with a background note on privileges and immunities, in English and French, to the national designated authority (NDA) in October 2019. The NDA forwarded this document to the Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Foreign Affairs for review. The NDA sent a revised draft agreement with comments from the Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Foreign Affairs to the Secretariat. ### The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the Independent Integrity Unit
Both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigate any claims for compensation relating to alleged breaches of privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after a thorough review of the relevant documentation to ensure that no illegal expropriation in the country(ies), or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### The Programme
envisages the implementation of three Components, which consist of relevant Outputs and Activities thereunder. Each Executive Entity for an individual Activity, the respective roles and responsibilities of each of those Executive Entities have not yet been clearly determined; however, while the Accession Entry will enter into the terms and conditions agreed with each Executive Entity in accordance with the AMA, further details on other contractual arrangements to be entered into by

Accredited Entity, Executing Entities and the final beneficiaries of the Programme are still required.

Moreover, the Accredited Entity requested from the Secretariat a deviation from the terms of the JMA to exclude the application of GCF's step-in rights for this programme, indicating that in order to allow the execution of the funding proposal with other international organizations as counterparties, it is not feasible for the Accredited Entity to allow GCF or any other party to have step-in rights over the assets and liabilities under the subsidiary agreements. According, and subject to, as a condition for execution of the funds according to the terms of the JMA, the Secretariat has requested from the Board a deviation from the Accredited Entity that the National Designated Authorities (NDAs) of each Host Country have been informed about this deviation and that the relevant information disclosure provisions has been included in the term sheet to allow for such deviations from the AMA. This means that, subject to Board approval, in case the Accredited Entity decides to discontinue programme implementation or terminate the funding proposal, the GCF will not be able to assume any role or appoint another party to replace the Accredited Entity, and the only alternative would be cancellation of the funding proposal according to the terms of the JMA.

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(i) Submission of a copy of GCF's certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the AE has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and has the capacity and authority to enter into the funding agreement. The submission of the funding proposal is conditional on substantial modification of the funding proposal, in accordance with the terms of the JMA, which must be submitted within 180 days to obtain its final internal approvals and to provide to the Fund such certificate or opinion.

(ii) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or after the AE has provided the Fund with a confirmation confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(iii) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP162

## Proposal name: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW)

### Accredited entity:
International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

### Country/ies:
Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and the Gambia

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Project overview
The project's target Sahelian countries are Burkina Faso, Chad, The Gambia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger and Senegal. The project seeks to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers. Based on its theory of change (the project’s ultimate goal is to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers), the following impact indicators should be achieved:
- Strengthened climate and weather information services to support decision-making and planning in agroforestry and livestock, agricultural insurance products and services, and capacity development for farmers, government and the private sector.
- Diversification of livelihoods through improved agricultural value chains, powered by reliable and affordable renewable energy sources; and
- Reduced obstacles (financial market products and services / high premiums) to access agrarian insurance for governments and smallholder farmers to enhance resilience-building.

#### 1.2 In order to accomplish these outcomes, the project proposes three strategic interventions:
(1) Strengthening climate information systems;
(2) Improving agricultural value chains; and
(3) Enhancing risk transfer.

The project will run for 20 years, including a 5-year implementation period. It will directly target smallholder farmers, numbering 817,922 direct beneficiaries, and also over 5.3 million indirect beneficiaries, representing 6.19 per cent of the total population of the seven countries.

#### 1.3 Greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation
A total of 21,446,699 tCO₂e in mitigation is estimated from the project: 4.9 per cent will come from the use of solar energy for agriculture, irrigation and water supply; 50 per cent from conservation agriculture (CSA); land-use management (LUM); and sustainable management of forests (SFM).

#### 1.4 The project will also contribute to the following:
- The installation and use of 492 solar panels, and then the creation of a mini-grid for farmers not connected to any grid.

* The Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (EX-ACT) is an appraisal system based on F&D to provide estimates of the impact of agriculture and forestry development projects, programs and policies on the carbon balance. The carbon balance is defined as the difference between the amount of greenhouse gases emitted or sequestered due to project implementation as compared to a business-as-usual scenario.

Currently, degraded forests will remain degraded land and extremely degraded forests without the project. The baseline assumption for agricultural land is that all land under agriculture will be cropland. ### Adaptation Impact

#### 1.3.1 Adaptation to Vulnerability
The funding proposal targets vulnerable poor farmers: it out of 10 farmers in Sub-Saharan Africa rely on rain-fed agriculture and agropastoralism; and most households earn at least 55 per cent of their income from agricultural sources, reaching about 80 per cent for those living below the poverty line. These households are exposed to water stress (poor irrigation, limited water sources, weathered roads, and limited post-production facilities) exacerbated by climate change. The proposal aims to improve the capacity to monitor in a near real-time state of operations of the national hydrometeorological agencies in these countries to provide reliable weather and climate advisories. As per the funding proposal: "hydro meteorological infrastructure is underfunded, with a low number of stations (defined as the number of station per 10,000 km²) as low as 0.4 in Niger, 0.5 in Mali and 1.6 in Senegal. The average annual precipitation has been observed to have increased from about 780 mm since 1960 to over 1200 mm by the early 2000s (from an average of 34°C to 37°C). Moreover, precipitation has been observed to have declined, and has not recovered since the late 1960s. The number of days with temperatures above 35°C has increased from about 8 in the 1960s has been increasing since 1998. These observed changes in climate have adversely affected crop yields and livestock production. In addition, the increase in temperature also forces the migration of farmers to resource-rich areas, thus threatening the degradation of land and water resources in other territories, which in turn triggers conflict between farmers and pastoralists."

#### 3.2 Historical Observations and Projections on Climate
##### Extreme Events (1900–2015)
In recent decades, each of the seven African least developed countries (LDCs) included in the funding proposal have been experiencing declining rainfall and increasing temperatures. These changes have led to increased frequency of droughts, floods, food security crises, and other disasters. Many of these disasters recorded in these countries have been hydro-meteorological in nature. For example, between 1900 and 2015, there were at least 34 major droughts that affected millions of people and impacted the food security of more than 24 million people in the Sahel region[1]. For the Sahel region in general, most disasters are hydro-meteorological in nature. Droughts have been recorded as a result of low rainfall and high temperatures, which are currently along major river systems and in many urban areas. As presented in figure 1, the incidence of droughts has increased over time.

Figure 1: Total drought and flood frequency in 18 African countries

# Incidents

## Past observations: Increasing temperature (1981–2018): Over West Africa and the Sahel, 2018 was the 7th warmest year on record. The rate of increase in temp was 2.27°C (from 1950-2018) and 3.80°C (1990-2018) per century (ACMAB, 2020). As may be expected, the largest temperature increases have occurred over land areas. This increase in temperature translates to a change in the average temperature of 0.85°C between 1970 and 2006 or, using the trend since 1901, an increase of 3.15°C and led to a 4 percent increase in global total runoff. Overall, the warming has been accompanied by more frequent heatwaves (above 38°C) and droughts (below 7°C) (Padgham et al., 2013), with an increase of 0.2–0.5°C per decade in some countries (Sylla et al., 2016). In addition, the frequency of extreme rainfall events has increased over West Africa and northern Sahel (e.g., southern Mauritania, Mali and Niger and northern Burkina Faso) show little seasonal variation in precipitation, with a tendency for more frequent heavy rainfalls and hot extremes, making the region a hotspot region for climate change (Turco et al., 2015). This warming has been accompanied by an increase in evaporation rates, which is linked to high evapotranspiration of Surface Water supply of the main transboundary river basins.**

## Past observations: declining precipitation (1981–2017): "The first part of the 20th century was characterized by a general decline in rainfall over West Africa, with an increase in seasonal rainfall totals during the latter half of the record, with overall low rainfall decades punctuated by occasional wet years. The second part of the 20th century has been dominated by the prolonged drought conditions since the mid-1960s to the mid-1980s (Hulme, 1992; Lüttje et al., 2002; Terribile and Lamb, 2005). The Sahelian climate over the past decades has been characterized by a general decline in rainfall. Since the 1980s rainfall has not returned to pre-1960s levels. Precipitation has still not recovered from the drought of 1973–1974 and the subsequent dry years (e.g., 1975, 1976). For example, the droughts of 2011–12 hit many across countries on the region (Marsh et al., 2016). Despite this partial recovery, rainfall has not returned to pre-1960 levels. The Sahel is characterized by a high degree of rainfall variability (Bassinti, 2013). There has been an increased number of rain days with writing concentrated in the north and east of the region. This has led to a reduction in the interannual and annual variability, and a delayed onset and an early retreat of the monsoon season (Bassinti and Sodé 2009; Baidu et al., 2013; Seth et al., 2013; Bassinti et al., 2015; Ibrahim et al., 2014; Samoy et al. 2016). This has led to a reduction in the interannual and annual variability, and an early retreat of the rainy seasons. These have likely had impacts on agriculture and livelihoods in terms of changes

### Projections
The Sahel is a large region in the continent of Africa, and its future vulnerability to climate change has been well studied. Because of the large area covered by the region, it is difficult to make generalizations about the entire region without considering local climatology. The dynamic scaling down under CORDEX regional climate models (RCMs) applied on GCMs allows for a more detailed representation of the Sahel and its variability in precipitation. The funding proposal used the following validated models to project future changes in temperature and precipitation: RCP 4.5, RCP 6.0, and RCP 8.5. For the emission scenarios, and GCM CMIP5 was used for the representative climate pathway (RCP) 4.5 and R.S scenario. The results of the projections show that the northern Sahara will experience an increase in temperature to the order of 3°C by mid-century, whereas the southern Sahara will experience a decrease in surface temperature of about 1°C.

The projections of the two GCMs for precipitation indicate rather large intermodel variability and are not conclusive. The RCP4.5 scenario shows a general increase in rainfall, as observed by Working Group I of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change in its Fifth Assessment Report. However, different variations in the algorithm for the core CMIP5 GCM suggest that there is no consensus among models about the timing and magnitude of any small delay in the onset of the rainy season by the end of the twenty-first century with respect to the baseline 1971–2000 period. The RCP8.5 scenario shows a general decrease in rainfall, as a prominent feature for the projection during the 2020-2050 time frame, representing a projected drying trend.

The RCMs provide for higher resolution projections, indicating a -5 to per cent decrease in annual rainfall by the 2030s with respect to the baseline of 1971–2000 under the RCP4.5.

scenarios Such a decline in total annual rainfall will be accompanied by a slight lengthening of the maximum dry spell length within the rainy season (see figure 6). The combination of increasing temperature and decline in total annual rainfall (see figure 6), coupled with a delay in onset of the rainy season, may lead to an increase in the number of days with no rain. This may result in an increase in drought intensity. The model-driven scenario under RCP4.5 for the 2030s indicates that the Sahel region will experience more frequent and longer dry spells, leading to increased drought conditions. Such escalated drought conditions would tend to adversely affect many aspects of human well-being, including declining crop productivity as well as affecting the availability of water for livestock - both having a detrimental effect on agriculture- and agropastoral-based livelihoods in the Sahel region.

Projection of extreme events. "Climate projections based both on models and observations indicate that one of the most profound consequences of global warming is the likelihood of more frequent and severe droughts."

The climate rationale for this project is satisfactory. The climate analysis established the historical climatology of the Sahel region using the available 100-year historical data sets for temperature, precipitation, and evapotranspiration (e.g., Karamouz et al., 2017). It then projected the future climatology using three regionally validated models that show consistent patterns. The results indicate a general trend of increasing temperatures with decreasing precipitation, and occurrence of more extreme events, especially droughts and floods. Under the RCP4.5 scenario, the most likely periods for the period 2036–2065 and 2071–2100 for the RCP8.5 and RCP8.5 scenarios to project temperature and precipitation trends.

Impacts of climate change and variability and extreme events. Given the reliance of the agricultural sector on rainfall, climate change will have a significant impact on people (farmers and communities whose population is projected to double by the end of 2040). Critical resources (the agriculture sector and ecosystems [e.g., water, land, and forests]), built infrastructure (roads, bridges, and other public works), and social services (e.g., for farmers, crop and livestock, farming tools and equipment); for businesses, farm inputs, loans and credit, insurance, and financial services; for households, food security, public finance and development programmes). Extreme events such as prolonged droughts and flash floods will have a significant impact on the livelihood of people by depleting their assets and tools of the trade. This will impact farmers' ability to put food on the table and affect other basic needs (e.g., health care, education, housing). The need to survive will force farmers and pastoralists to migrate or compete for scarce resources such as water, grazing and

### Actions to address the vulnerabilities

#### 3.1.2. Actions to address the vulnerabilities

**20.** The funding equitably addresses the vulnerabilities of the target farmers and communities, the government, and other stakeholders (e.g., businesses), and the barriers exacerbating these vulnerabilities. It seeks to strengthen the resilience of vulnerable populations through preparedness, risk reduction and risk transfer; it seeks to strengthen the absorptive, adaptive and transformative capacities of the target 817,922 farmers and the indirect 53.3 million beneficiaries in the project area, as well as the government and other stakeholders in rural communities and relying on ecosystems for livelihoods and food.

**21.** Risk preparedness: This preparation begins with the updating of national meteorological agencies (NMAs) to standards of the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) in terms of facilities and personnel. The NMAs will be established as a key component of the climate information system, which includes present (e.g., automatic weather stations, rain gauges, super computers, and communication systems) and staffing capacity for climate monitoring, climate data management, climate-based forecasting, and other climate and weather services. Once established, access to NMAs improved climate information will be provided through a range of channels. Improved actionable climate information will improve understanding or appreciation of the need for preparedness, risk reduction, and risk transfer in the face of climate change impacts, changes in precipitation, changes in planting and harvesting seasons, and the slow onset of prolonged drought or fast onset of flash floods due to intense precipitations. The set-up of EWS for climate change will be a key component of this activity. This will ensure that dissemination of risk advisories to both farmers and communities in general via available modern communication channels (e.g., mobile phones, radio, television) once the rehabilitated NMAs will also enhance the climate and disaster risk readiness of farmers and communities. The project will support the development of a national strategy for awareness campaigns and capacity-building workshops with farmers, communities, and decision-makers on how to use climate information in their livelihoods, business or climate services and information in their activities or business operations. This activity is under component 1 Climate risk preparedness (i.e., output 1.1). Increased access to agrometrical information will be provided through the development of a national strategy for risks management, and output 2.2 Awareness-raising, capacity-building and institutional development.

#### Risk reduction

**23.** Through a combination of awareness campaigns and capacity-building (e.g., workshops and seminars) and hands-on approaches/farmer field schools, risk reduction will be achieved through the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices. The training and skills development on CSA, LURB and SFM will be conducted on site or virtually, depending on COVID-19 protocols and restrictions. In addition to upskilling and education


---
**Page 142**
---

## Risk reduction

Complementing this set of activities on farmer behavior, another component of risk reduction will be the strengthening of agriculture and irrigation facilities used by farmers and agricultural producers. This will include the construction of water storage and irrigation facilities. Other activities will focus on knowledge management and capacity building for policy or planning inputs to each of the governments. These activities fall under component 2 Development of Climate Change Adaptation/ Mitigation Practices.

### Risk transfer

The introduction of risk management instruments at the level of commercial farmers, smallholder farmers, and extreme climate-risk pools, and the Extreme Facility Fund will further contribute to the increased absorptive capacity of national governments and local governments. The management of residual risks through climate-linked microinsurance products will incentivize farmers to participate in the massive capacity-building, and eventually lead to the integration of these risk management instruments into the broader CCAFS, LUMI and SFM. Macroeconomics for national governments will reduce their fiscal burden in financially supporting agricultural insurance schemes.

### Scale: High

The potential for paradigm shift emanates from all of the interventions in high. These interventions are not new to African countries; thus, it can be said that they are not innovative. However, from the perspective of African LAGs that have not experienced these interventions before and will benefit from their integrated climate risk management approaches for the first time, this shift is likely to be transformative.

### Transformation of NIMAs and/or government institutions' way of doing business due to climate services

Backed with modern tools such as quality equipment, evidence-based data and information systems (e.g. trained meteorologists), the seven countries will be able to create access to reliable climate information (e.g. agrometeorological) and services (e.g. impact-based forecasting) that can be used for decision-making in agriculture, water resources, and culture. However, these NIMA climate services will also enable the operationalization of a climate- and project-specific government development plan. These plans, programs, projects and programs of governments and private sector stakeholders. Decision-makers across sectors, for example transport (e.g. aviation, maritime and land), urban planning (e.g. housing, energy management, waste management, etc.), health (e.g. disease surveillance, immunization and training, and public health), public finance, budget, trade, finance and investment, among others, will be able to use impact-based forecasting to climate-proof their critical

# Transformation of farmers and agriculture-based livelihoods

## Scaling up of sustainable and innovative finance

The de-risking of farmers and the agriculture sector will lead to a new era of innovative financial instruments that are financially sound, environmentally friendly, and socially acceptable. The development of parametric microinsurance (e.g., weather index-based products) and climate-linked financing tools is expected to be supported by public institutions, international development agencies, and private financial institutions. The institution of hedging of insurance with credit facilities (i.e., the combination of a loan or other credit facility with an insurance product that covers short-term, and reduced collateral requirements). Due to the de-risking of farmers and the agri-based BVCs, and the important role of the agriculture sector in any COVID-19 recovery scenario, the diversification of financing sources is expected. This will be achieved through local development banks, microfinance institutions (with access to concessional grants from multilateral development banks) or private financial institutions. Financially inclusive digital platforms for agricultural credit can also be developed, considering that they form the majority of this population in rural areas.

During the design, some microfinance institutions stated that they could lower the interest rate on their loans by 1 to 3% if such measures were in place. All responses to second batch of questions from the independent TAP

each of these countries, New Islamic financial products (e.g., climate takaful, musharaka and mudarabah) will be aligned with sharia principles.

31. The scaling up of finance will be built on the previous and ongoing initiatives of IFAD, the accredited entity (AE) in this region and in the agriculture sector (component 2). 3.2.4 Scaling up of risk transfer mechanisms

32. Currently, insurance penetration and understanding are very low in the region. Thus, the potential replication or scaling up of the risk transfer mechanism is also high, especially after the successful pilot of the World Food Programme (WFP) microinsurance project (cash grant program) (i.e., cash transfers) could be used to target and enroll farmers.31 The proposed microinsurance builds on the Rural Resilience Initiative of the World Food Programme (WFP) R4V WFP/IFAD pilot project, which has been implemented in 2019 with a total of 500,000 beneficiaries insured in this year. This will further pave the way for more entitlements and coverage under target 3 – such as the development of studies (e.g., national disaster risk financing strategies) and tools (e.g., climate risk assessment, vulnerability analysis, etc.). The Climate fiscal framework, tools and financing instruments to identify and quantify existing disaster risks, estimate potential losses, and develop adaptation measures. The Climate Risk Atlas, review and assessment of the African Risk Capacity (ARC) agricultural, rangeland and drought models - will provide an enabling mechanism for the roll-out of a national microinsurance (e.g., surveying and mapping of risk areas, etc.).31 According to the AE, the question is related to the risk of "widespread catastrophic drought in sub-Saharan Africa today could cost upwards of US $5 billion annually. This would be equivalent to 20 per cent of the total amount allocated by African country donors' climate aid budgets." These risk management tools will also further the development of Islamic financial products and services, which are currently only the approach on "bundling of service," which essentially links microinsurance with other activities (e.g., savings accounts). The AE noted that, in addition to the existing microfinance banking and insurance products already exist to serve farmers, the improved credit rating and the needs of farmers (especially Muslim farmers) can potentially trigger the development of more Islamic financial products (e.g. Islamic finance, and digital finance), especially in the face of a pandemic scenario.

### Indigenous Peoples
The Sahel region is home to several ethnic groups. As per the stakeholder engagement plan, the funding proposal will deal with indigenous peoples in a “sensitive and culturally appropriate manner” by involving them through their communities to enable targeted meaningful consultation, including identification and involvement of representatives from all relevant indigenous groups, respecting their languages and cultures; culturally appropriate engagement processes; providing sufficient time for IPs decision-making processes; ensuring that IPs are informed about the project activities or measures that could affect these either positively or negatively.**173** GCF’s ESS pertains to Indigenous Peoples/Sub-Saharan Africa Historically Underserved Traditional Local Communities (STLCs). The involvement of indigenous peoples in the project design fosters full respect for the human rights, dignity, aspirations, identity, culture, and natural resources of these groups. It also ensures that their views are taken into account. Underserved Traditional Local Communities. ESS173 is also meant to avoid adverse impacts of projects on IPs and other historically underserved traditional local communities or local communities, or when avoidance is not possible, to minimize, mitigate and/or compensate for such impacts.

### Decent work and economic growth (Sustainable Development Goal 8)
Under component 2.1 (see output 2.2) Diversified livelihood through the promotion of income-generating activities in rural areas will be achieved by providing training and support to IPs on other interventions (risk preparation, risk reduction and risk transfer). This will improve the livelihood opportunities for IPs and reduce their vulnerability to climate change impacts on these groups.**174** The increased awareness and access to climate information, better understanding of basic financial products and microinsurance applications on finance, improved access to markets and value chains, and access to renewable energy (e.g., solar-powered roads, and cheap renewable energy sources) will greatly enhance access to diversified jobs for IPs in rural areas. This will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 13 (climate action).

Under output 2.5.2, 200 cooperatives, SMEs or farmers organizations and 3 sets of capacity-building training programs will be established in each of the seven African LDCs. COVID-19 protocols will be followed (see note 3).

### 3.3.2 Partnerships for goals (SDG 17)

#### e. Partnerships on climate services

The funding proposal will also strengthen the regional capacity of the commercial, institutional, country, regional and international levels. At the international and regional levels, the upgrading to WMO standards of the new hydro-meteorological centre in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia (e.g., African Centre for Climate Change Technology) and centre of excellence (e.g., African Centre of Meteorological Application for Development), and AGRHYMET Regional Research Institute will be supported. The project will also support the development of climate data and information, and new services such as impact-based forecasting and EWS. The IFAD strategy on climate change and disaster risk management will be strengthened at all levels and will also strengthen relationships in climate risk management among the African LDCs. The South-South and Triangular Cooperation will facilitate exchange visits between farmers and high-level government officials, including ministers of agriculture, to share experiences with each other.

The private sector, insurance companies, representatives of regional organizations of agriculture producers, agricultural extension service providers, research institutions, development partners, and traditional authorities.

#### f. Partnerships on climate risk management

The development of weather-based insurance products in the mainstream will also foster collaboration among financial institutions at the local, regional and international levels. This will be triggered by the need to support the development of a market for agricultural insurance products that can cater to the needs of farmers, agri-bas VICE entrepreneurs as clients, and the public and private sector institutions such as business associations, cooperatives, credit unions, financial intermediaries, agricultural extension services, and insurance institutions, and ABC WFP African Development Bank (AfDB) and IFAD. The AfDB Africa Risk Management Facility (ARMF) will play a key role in promoting disaster risk financing on the continent and in assisting countries build capacity building and disaster risk transfer solutions as part of their long-term resilience-building efforts (African Resilience Strategy).

#### g. Community partnerships

The commonality of interventions across the seven African LDCs will help reduce the potential conflicts arising from access and use of critical resources such as water, land, and forests. The project will support the development of collaborative partnerships at the government and community levels, especially at the borders where transboundary corridors exist.

### Social co-benefits

#### 3.3.4 Higher levels of production will have significant social benefits in the form of reduced food insecurity and less out-migration of male community members in search of alternative employment opportunities.

- **Impact on households:** The project is expected to generate employment opportunities, especially for women and youth, representing 50 per cent and 40 per cent, respectively, of all beneficiaries of the project. It is anticipated that agricultural and agroforestry-based productivities will be enhanced through improved access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and pesticides; weather forecast-based financing and programmes; access to insurance schemes, etc. Improved water infrastructure will also help farmers offset rising moisture stress during critical crop growing periods, thereby enhancing crop yields.

#### 4 Needs of the recipients

**Score: High**

- **Primary recipients:** The primary recipients under this funding proposal are the 817,622 farmers and 3.5 million rural households that depend on them for their rural consumption. Other recipients are the hydrometeorological agencies. Next are the insurance (financial institutions or insurance companies), the government as a whole, and the needs of the ecosystems.

#### 4.1 Needs for climate services

- **Need:** The needs of agricultural meteorological and hydrological services are to be met through upgraded weather stations and other equipment fitted with modern automatic weather stations, rain gauges, computers, and communications equipment that are on a par with WMO standards.
- **Meteorological services:** These services will provide farmers with climate-based forecasts and early warnings, and disseminate these (climate and weather) services information to the various stakeholders, including the farmers, communities, various public and private decision-makers. The latter include those working in micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) of the agri-food value chain, such as input suppliers, traders, processors, and other service providers; financial institutions such as insurance, bank and non-bank financial institutions and microfinance entities. These needs are covered under component 1.

#### 4.2 Needs of farmers

- **Farmers need food, improved incomes, and sustainable livelihoods:** These needs are to be met through the combined interventions on behaviour changes, financial literacy, sustainable use of resources, availability of rural infrastructure (e.g., road, renewable energy power), value addition in agri-food BVC facilitation, access to markets, and access to risk transfer mechanisms. Farmers and rural communities as a whole will improve their capacity for adaptation and resilience through the combined interventions on behaviour changes.


---
**Page 149**
---

### Needs of government and the private sector (i.e., insurance companies and users of insurance products)

The needs of insurance companies are to be better beginning with reliable data from climate risk information systems. This will enable them to price weather-indexed crop and livestock and triggering thresholds, and from financial institutions on priority coverages in support of their lending activities, as well as NHMAs in the agent-based BVs on critical supply chains. These data and information enable insurance companies to design and develop risk transfer instruments in support of farmers and in close collaboration with WFP (with its microinsurance pilot project) and other partners. The government will be enabled to combine its climate change adaptation efforts with poverty reduction, as the vulnerability of vulnerable poor farmers and communities will be reduced, and government will be enabled to focus on other pressing sustainable development issues. These needs are addressed under component 3.

### Needs of ecosystems

The combined intervention on teaching farmers how to do CSA, LIM and SFM and on building resilient infrastructures (e.g., water wells, and solar-powered energy sources) will directly contribute to the reduction in land degradation and desertification on ecosystems. These needs are addressed under components 1 and 2.

### Absence of alternative financing

Sub-Saharan countries have been experiencing absolute poverty, often due to major crop losses incurred because of climate change. Many of these countries belong to the Nhara-LILAC group, where there is a dearth of financing for the economic and social agendas of the participating countries. However, climate-related services are central addressing the root cause of their high rates of poverty. The development of sustainable agricultural production systems with value chains are critically important steps. However, the participating countries have no institutional capacity to support the development of these systems. The root-cause barriers need to be addressed with public financing; there is hardly any private sector interest in supporting such efforts.

It appears that GCF is the only viable alternative financing window that could extend its support towards this objective.

The institutions, especially NHMAs, need immediate support to acquire hydrometeorological equipment, modelling capacities and other support so that they can provide reliable climate risk information. The development of agent-based BVs need support to operate for the promotion of value chains, so that farmers can have better returns from their agricultural production. This project has elements that will offer much-enhanced institutional capacity building.

### Country ownership

The funding proposal was formulated through a series of consultations during the design phase in 2019. IFAD consulted many stakeholders, especially rural and farming communities* including civil society organizations and non-governmental organizations


---
**Page 150**
---

several areas. Among the stakeholders consulted were government authorities (e.g., African LDC nationally designated authorities, finance, agriculture, environment, and energy), NGOs, cooperatives, and women and youth representatives.

The seven participating countries are parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) that signed and ratified the Kyoto Protocol. By ratifying the UNFCCC, these countries have committed to implementing measures to adapt to climate change and to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions in accordance with this protocol. This programme will contribute to the implementation of objectives of the three Rio Conventions (the Convention on Biological Diversity, the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Agreement, and the SDGs) and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction.

This programme is fully aligned with the countries' national development plans, and their national climate change strategies. It will contribute to the implementation of national adaptation programmes of action, national climate change policies and strategies, NDCs, national disaster risk reduction strategies, national strategies for disaster risk reduction, and intended nationally determined contributions.

The programme is also aligned with the ongoing investment programmes in the Sahel region (GIZ-GGGI-IFAD project, GEF 6 project, and the present executing entity) via ADB/IFC, ARB (EE), and WFP (EE).

### The Great Green Wall (GGW) is a GEF 7 flagship project. The GGW is a GEF umbrella programme covering approximately 100 million hectares of degraded land across 15 km - 8,000 km in the African continent, and helping to sequester 250 MEGtCOeq and create 10 million jobs over its lifetime. It provides a framework for planning and programming investment in climate resilience in the Sahel region.

### IFAD is linking its baseline investment programmes covering the entire Sahelian corridor with the GGW project, through the use of IFAD programmes for farmers and the agriculture sector focusing on "risk mitigation strategies, which promote sustainable land management practices that increase productivity and production profitability, and smallholder farmers' coping strategies towards more competitive livelihoods, while enhancing resilience to climate shocks." IFAD is also to become the lead agency for the GGW.

### The ADB is the premier pan-African development institution promoting economic growth in Africa. The ADB's GGGI-IFAD focus on disaster risk management programme development; access to finance and insurance; and capacity building. ARB will work closely with microinsurance companies, and local financial institutions to develop innovative insurance products that address macroinsurance, microinsurance, and loss bonds. ADB/IFC will run from 2019 to 2023 in partnership with ABC.

### ABC is the African Risk Capacity (which hosts the secretariat and conducts ABC advisory capacity and advocacy-building activities), and the ABC Insurance Company Limited (which is tasked with delivering risk transfer services). ABC has a track record of insuring over 5 million people against weather-related polycrop losses USD 600 million. With the Strategic Programme 2020-24, ABC will scale up its operations, innovate new products and expand coverage to other countries in Africa.

### WFP is a leader in innovative microinsurance solutions (e.g., index-based insurance, and WFP RIS) that can complement measures strengthening access to natural and financial capital for smallholder farmers. The GGW project will provide an opportunity for WFP to scale up its innovative microinsurance products.


---
**Page 151**

shocks and stresses. WFP has very strong experience in working with various international organizations, non-governmental organizations, civil society, and the private sector to enable people, communities and countries.

All seven countries have issued a no-objection letter, and one of these programmes has been included in the GCF project proposal. The content of the no-objection letters were all the same, very general in their statements, and without reference to any specific climate-related policy, objective or activity. As such, there are no new commitments, obligations, rights or privileges between GCF and African LGDs have remained pending since 2015.

### Financial viability and other financial indicators
#### 1.3.3. Financial internal rate of return, sensitivity analysis

The projected economic internal rate of return (EIRR) shows a positive net present value (NPV) at the base case scenario with an internal rate of return of 18% or 29 per cent. The positive NPV and IRR represent the value of avoided "losses and damages" due to the implementation of CSA, LUM and SFM projects in terms of reduced GHG emissions and impacts of drought. A 16-year period was used for the discounting of cash flow to match the project's life cycle (implementation and maintenance cost), which was estimated at 3 per cent per annum (see table 14).

### Table 14: Economic internal rate of return, sensitivity analysis

| Dominated avoided (NPV) | Damages avoided (IRR) | hurdle rate |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Base case | -USD 20.8 million | 3% |
| Costs +20% | USD 109.83 million | 24% |
| Benefits +20% | USD 76.24 million | 15% |
| Costs and benefits +20% | USD 186.07 million | 17% |

The projected positive NPV and high IRR (with 10 per cent as the hurdle rate) demonstrate that CSA, LUM and SFM projects are expected to improve farmer productivity and increase compensation for crop losses during drought. Conservative assumptions were used in computing the EIRR.

#### Notes:
- For "the avoided loss and damages" - In lieu of crop losses – owing to the absence of data availability – agricultural productivity for each LDC (average production value added per capita for the period 2008–2010) was used. Per the GCF, "losses and damages" are defined as "the costs associated with climate change impacts that cannot be captured from the project; a private sector entity is able to avoid that cost and invest that money and get credit for the carbon savings generated by the project, but the same practices can't be carried out."
- The avoided losses and damages cover only the use of renewable energy and not the carbon sequestration and emission avoidance from the Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) Land and Management (LUM) and Sustainable Forest Management (SFM). These are considered as co-benefits. The avoided losses and damages for the CSA, LUM and SFM were excluded because "these measures are based on changes in behavior of farmers and the value may range significantly depending upon the specific context and implementation." In addition, the avoided losses and damages from the use of renewable energy can be captured from the project; a private sector entity is able to avoid that cost and invest that money and get credit for the carbon savings generated by the project, but the same practices cannot be carried out."
- The costs used in this model are USD 150/ton CO2 and USD 4 per ton LULUCF. This price range is at the lower end of the international carbon market prices, ranging from USD 1,740/ton to USD 119/tonCO2, with half of the covered emission groups in the USD 350/tonCO2 range. South Africa's carbon price is about USD 750/ton.
