# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW)

Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and The Gambia

December 2020


---
**Page 165**
---

# Introduction

1. Across sub-Saharan Africa, the agricultural sector remains critical to local and regional economies. It is the basis for food security and an important source of employment, particularly for women. Rural women are the backbone of rural development and national economic growth in sub-Saharan Africa (UN Women, 2018). For example, in Burkina Faso and Mali, for example, an estimated 93 percent and 78 percent respectively of active women work in agriculture (World Bank, 2020). High levels of rural poverty coupled with a high proportion of women-headed households in sub-Saharan Africa (UN Women, 2018) make the contribution of women's productive activities to the household and community a crucial factor for food security. Women's contributions are critical to increasing agricultural production and receive a significantly lower share of income in comparison to men. They bear the brunt of climate change impacts on agriculture because they have limited access and control over all land, productive resources and information (SCPZ, ADB, 2015).

2. In addition, natural disasters do not affect all people equally. Marginalized populations, including women, tend to be disproportionately negatively impacted. For instance, women and girls are affected more than men due to a "disaster." This can be explained by the fact that "natural disasters have been found to exacerbate existing vulnerabilities that render females more vulnerable to their fatal impact," According to the World Bank report on gender equality in disaster risk management (2015). Women often take greater responsibility for household chores, increasing their hardships during floods and droughts. In addition, women are more likely than men to be displaced and are more likely to experience loss of housing, lack access to services and relief and are at far greater risk of sexual violence.

3. Women in sub-Saharan Africa rely heavily on agriculture for their livelihood. Due to the high climate-sensitive nature of the agricultural sector, women's livelihoods are much more fragile than men's (UNDP, 2015). They become increasingly vulnerable to impacts of extreme weather events (UNDP, 2015), and they are particularly sensitive to changes in geographical location, and unmitigated climate change is regarded as potentially one of the biggest stressors for women in sub-Saharan Africa (FAO, 2016; IPCC, 2007a, 2007b).

4. Therefore, effective support for women farmers is critical for the optimization of agricultural productivity, food security and sustainable improvement of rural livelihoods. Women farmers receive only a fraction of the inputs and extension support that men farmers do (UN Women, 2018). They are often excluded from credit access in commercial farming; they often do not receive a cash income for their work, which translates into lower levels of investment in agricultural production and less investment. This negatively affects their productivity levels, the gender gap in agricultural productivity – measured by the value of agricultural produce per unit of land – and a significant reduction in women's empowerment (UN Women, 2018). Not only is equalizing women’s access to agricultural inputs (including labour, time-saving equipment, seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, credit, extension services, training and mitigation for low emission and climate resilient agriculture) critical to close gender gaps in agricultural productivity, but it also has a positive impact on food security and social gains. Across many sub-Saharan countries, it could raise crop production by up to 19 percent (FAO, 2016). Women's empowerment is also critical for the reduction of people out of poverty (UN Women, Policy brief no. 11). Furthermore, a changing climate means that women need to be supported in adopting and implementing these climate-smart approaches to agriculture help close the gender gap and promote women’s empowerment, economic development and societal resilience to shocks.


---
**Page 166**
---

5. Social and cultural norms in the seven-targeted countries (Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and The Gambia) in this proposal impose several constraints on women. The gendered division of labour in the households places the burden of securing water for drinking and cooking on women while men are free to engage in income-generating activities or to further invest their earnings into the household. This leaves women with less time available than men, making them less able than men to invest their time in agricultural work, particularly in polygamous households. Even in countries where women’s right to own land is legally recognized, the major constraints are that women have to share this land with other family members and are often unable to secure tenure on the land they cultivate.

These norms reduce the amount of time that women have available for their own productive activities and limit their ability to invest in agricultural production. Women also often lack security of tenure in the land that they cultivate and are therefore unable to benefit from investments made by them, such as planting high-value crops or installing early warning systems (EWSs) which are for preparedness, to better adapt and mitigate the effects of climate change and transfer part of the climate risk in the market through agricultural insurance schemes.

6. Women in the region also having less access to skilled jobs compared to men (8 percent in paid employment against 12 percent of men) and they tend to be marginalized in the labour force, despite their high levels of education and entrepreneurship. They are mainly concentrated in traditional agriculture (70 percent) and in the informal sector (35 percent). These women are more likely than men to be linked to widespread poverty that affects both women and men; the vulnerability of women and girls is further exacerbated by social norms and practices, which are deeply rooted in religious and customary social standards at all levels of society, from the family unit to the highest political level.

Since the mid-1980s, almost every country has created a national gender policy or strategy. However, legislation that discriminates against women still exists. In practice, gender is usually considered as an afterthought and gender policies are often not implemented in practice. The countries have different legal systems: a civil law system of civil, customary and Sharia laws, coupled with scarce resources impedes the effective implementation of gender equality strategies. For example, in some countries, attempts by the state to introduce laws promoting equality and gender equity have been met with resistance from religious leaders and communities as is the case in Niger, Mali and Chad in relation to the Family Code. However, through community-based organizations (CBOs), women’s groups, non-governmental organizations and programmes implement activities that take into account gender equality and equity.

The programme will work closely with CBOs, women’s groups and other local legal systems and patriarchal systems deeply rooted in religious and customary social standards and use the same channels (community and customary) to implement the activities. The programme will also ensure that all its partners are aware of the programme's principles of intervention and specifically address the needs and priorities of women and girls.

8. The 2016 Human Development Report shows that the situation is worsening in the seven countries targeted by the programme. They figured among the bottom of the global list in the Human Development Index (HDI). Mauritania occupies the 173rd position; Senegal, 182nd; The Gambia, 173rd; Mali, 175th; Burkina Faso, 169th; Niger, 180th; and Nigeria, 187th.


---
**Page 167**
---

## A more in-depth gender analysis for each of the seven countries of this programme is presented below.

### Burkina Faso

#### Demographics and social norms
- The population of Burkina Faso is estimated at 17.9 million people in 2014, of which around 51 percent are women.
- Nearly half (46.2 percent) of all households are headed by women.

#### Social norms and gender equality
- This situation is the result of inequalities based on gender that limit women's access to their economic rights and reduce their access to economic and social opportunities in relation to men, such as education and political commitment.
- The project aims at increasing women’s access to land and sustainable energy sources in agriculture. In addition to their housework, women also manage family businesses, leaving very little time for the pursuit of income-generating activities.

#### Gender equality index
- The GGI score is 56.6 out of 100.


---
**Page 168**
---

12. This inequality can also be seen in their access to services, information particularly climate information for decision-making and technology to select the right solutions and equipment's/ cropping and harvesting periods. Two-thirds of those with an account at a bank or microfinance institution have access to credit, compared to only 30 percent who use weather index insurance, a certain sector of the population believes that men and women should share equally in household decision-making power and responsibility. Ownership of a mobile phone is more common among men (78 percent) than among women (52 percent) (Yeleen Rural Electrification Project in Burkina Faso, ADB, 2019).

13. Another consequence of discriminatory social norms is that in half of the households, the father or husband makes all decisions about how to use household income and spends it on himself rather than on his family. This has a negative impact on the education or health of children. In terms of inheritance: 25 percent of the population considers that women should not inherit property from their husbands. In terms of widowhood, 40 percent of widows have inherited the majority of the property of their deceased husbands (with their children).

Development and poverty

14. According to the 2014 Multisectoral Continuous Survey, 40 percent of the population lives below the income poverty line in Burkina Faso. The proportion of people living on less than $3.10 per day is higher rural in Burkina Faso (more than 92 percent of the poor live in rural areas). Individuals living in households headed by women under 45 years of age and with children under five are more likely to be poor, with a poverty rate for female-headed households at 37.1 percent compared to 28.6 percent for male-headed households. The poverty rate for female-headed households is 11 percent higher than for the ones headed by men.

15. In 2018, Burkina Faso ranked 182 out of 189 countries and territories. Its Gini value for that year was 47.3 (the average for sub-Saharan Africa is 46). Women hold only 18 percent of parliamentary seats and 6.0 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level education compared to 25.5 percent of men. The maternal mortality rate is 9 per every 100,000 live births, 37.10 women die from pregnancy related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 104.6 births per 1,000 women ages 15-19. Female participation in the labor force was 68.5 percent compared to 75.1 for men (Human Development Report, 2019).

Women and agriculture

15. The agricultural-sectorial sector employs nearly 90 percent of the population and accounts for 33.7 percent of GDP in 2016 (ADB, OECD, UNDP, 2017). Women account for 84.5 percent of all agricultural workers in Burkina Faso. They are responsible for attending to their own fields, whose crops are for home consumption or sale. The General Agricultural Census shows that women represent only 39.6 percent of the total workforce and 42.5 percent of women. As for fishing activities, women are present in the processing and marketing of fish (Yeleen Rural Electrification Project in Burkina Faso, ADB, 2019).

Access to land ownership is a major constraint for women in Burkina Faso. Even though they represent more than half of the agricultural labor force, they own less than 40 percent of landowners. When they are landowners, their decision-making power remains limited compared with men (58 percent). Women have access to only 12 percent of credit, compared to 32 percent of men, because of customary law and community land management practices. Only 67 percent of women own livestock or poultry. Access to agricultural equipment: 96 percent of such household use traditional tools (hoes and shovels) for plowing and 80 percent for harvesting. Women have access to emergency situation use ploughs. Moreover, women's access to agriculture extension services remains low (Yeleen Rural Electrification Project in Burkina Faso, ADB, 2019).

Education


---
**Page 169**
---

18. While the enrollment rates for boys and girls are almost equal at the primary education level, this is not the case at the secondary and university levels: in the latter, the average enrolment rate for boys is 35 percent and 32 percent for girls. Literacy rates for children aged 15 to 24 are higher for boys (56.8 percent) than for girls (43.8 percent).

Employment
19. Unequal access to education limits women's opportunities in the area of skilled employment while they are highly integrated in the informal sector. Women are responsible for all or most of household chores and childcare, which leaves them with a limited number of hours per week in rural Burkina Faso is 56 hours for women than that of men at 60 hours.

Energy
20. Because of their role in families and communities in energy supply, women are the first to be deprived when these essential resources are absent, difficult to access or dangerous to use. Data statistical from 2013 revealed that male-headed households have a wider access to electricity than female-headed households (75 percent vs. 48 percent). More than 90 percent of Burkina Faso's population do not have access to modern cooking fuels. The rate of household access to clean gas is 4.9 percent nationally and about 12.2 percent in the urban areas, while only 3.7 percent of households use electricity for domestic purposes. The national coverage of grid electricity is 28.6 percent and shows strong disparities between urban and rural areas. As a result, the women are exposed to high levels of indoor air pollution, which is toxic, resulting in a health crisis with high levels of deaths resulting from pneumonia, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, lung cancer, bronchitis, emphysema, tuberculosis, and land degradation.

Policy framework
21. The government-related policies in the country are:
- Constitution of Burkina Faso of 1991: establishes that all individuals are equal, regardless of sex.
- Law No. 043/96 / ADP of November 1996 on the Prevention and Punishment of FGM
- National Gender Policy (2009)
- Constitutional amendment states that 30 percent of candidates on electoral lists must be women. However, as a result of the 2015 elections, only 11 percent of parliamentary seats were occupied by women.
- Law No. 034/2009 / AN of 16 June 2009 on rural land tenure: establishes that managed land is not subject to inheritance.
- Law n° 034-2012 / AM of 02 July 2012 on agrarian and land reorganization.

The government's action revolves primarily around the implementation of the National Gender Policy (NGP) and the National Plan for Economic and Social Development (PNDES). The PNDES aims to reduce gender inequalities, promote decent employment and social protection for all, including women. It also aims to increase female representation in decision-making positions, female business owners from 21 percent in 2015 to 50 percent in 2030, in promoting decent jobs opportunities, improving access to education and training, increasing access to vocational training, etc. (Yeelen Rural Electrification Project in Burkina Faso, ADRS, 2019).

23. The Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Gender is responsible for the implementation of the National Gender Policy and the National Plan for Economic and Social Development of Women. At the institutional level, the gender mainstreaming initiatives undertaken by the ministry are not always consistent with its overall objectives. The Ministry is underfunded. There is a lack of mechanisms for consultation and coordination between the key players, which are: 1) the national, regional and municipal councils for the promotion of gender; 2) the gender groups established within this ministry to promote


---
**Page 170**
---

gender mainstreaming: 3) the consultation framework of the PTFs (including Swiss Cooperation, World Bank, UNDP and UNFPA, among others) and the TFP Common Gender Fund. The main reasons are the low level of ownership of gender issues by policymakers and the lack of a strong political will to drive the implementation process. Women's civil society organizations suffer from their weak structure and the absence of a national coordinating umbrella structure.

24. The government has put in place reforms to combat violence against women. For instance, since 1998, the law prohibits forced marriage. More recently, in May 2015 and began to be applied in 2016. However, in general, sectoral policies do integrate the gender dimension. The lack of a national strategy on gender equality is also a problem as there is a scarcity of up-to-date disaggregated statistics by gender in their different development sectors. This makes it difficult for policymakers to take informed decisions.

25. The law allows girls to be married at 17 years of age, against a legal minimum of 20 years for men. The widespread practice of early marriage discriminates against girls and largely explains the large demographic growth of the country.


In Burkina Faso, this law prohibits all forms of violence against women and girls, however domestic violence is still very common. More than three women (37 percent) have been victims of domestic violence in their lifetime, commonly with six in five men (16 percent) having beaten a woman at least once. Only 20 percent believe that a wife can be the best friend or another, while only 4 percent believe that a woman can beat her husband. Despite its prohibition, female genital mutilation is also common and three (35 percent) of women aged 15 to 45 are circumcised.


Chad

Demographics:

27. In 2009, the population of Chad was estimated at 11.04 million inhabitants, of which 50.6 percent were women. 75.1 percent live in rural areas and 50.4 percent are youth under 15 years of age. There are still large gender disparities in the country: it is estimated that 158 out of 169 countries have a higher female literacy rate than Chad. Gender stereotypes are pervasive and are aggravated by conflict and displacement. Chad is also affected by violent conflicts, which have displaced more than one million people within the region.

The country hosts 450,000 refugees, of whom 55.5 percent are female and 24.3 percent are of school age (UNF. Chad country strategic plan 2019-2023).


Development and poverty:

28. Chad is the world's second most food-insecure country (118th out 119 countries on the Global Hunger Index). The regions most affected by food insecurity and with the greatest vulnerability are those in the Sahel, where the situation has worsened since 2016. The worst during the lean year season (from June to September). From 2016 to 2017, national food security indicators deteriorated significantly, especially for women and children under five years old. In 2017, food insecurity prevalence was particularly high in the Sahel region (45 percent), with a significant increase of 38 percentage points during the lean season (40 percent). Over 3 million people are food-insecure or at risk of food insecurity, and more than half of them live in the Sahel. The situation is worsened by the lean season every year (WFP. Chad country strategic plan 2019-2023).


29. Chad ranked 187th out of 189 countries and territories on the 2015 Human Development Index. Women and 34 percent of these head Twenty-three percent of Chadian households are headed by women, a figure that is even higher in rural areas (36 percent) and among younger women (41 percent).

# Women and agriculture

## Food processing activities, the sale of firewood, and informal sector jobs (Landrinks, USAID)

### Chad

30. In Chad, the percentage of the female labor force that works in agriculture is 59 percent, serving as farmers or working men. Women have restricted access to productive assets, credit, land ownership and the opportunity to rent land. Land inheritance customs often discriminate against women, and men decide on the use of harvested crops and income. Only 10 percent of women have access to credit for agricultural activities; only 26 percent engage in livestock activities; 22.6 percent have access to credit and 28 percent have a bank account.

31. The division of household responsibility is still highly gender-biased: women are expected to take care of the children, prepare meals, clean the house, collect firewood and water (and sometimes their family's water supply). Furthermore, in terms of access to land and property, customary practices often prevent women from owning or renting land. Women have fewer opportunities to participate in income-generating activities. For instance, while they participate actively in livestock production, this activity is not considered a source of wealth; it is usually used for subsistence purposes rather than for sale. This situation is particularly visible in rural areas, and as rural women constitute up to 40 percent of the country's population, this has significant implications.

### Education

32. On average, girls receive less education than boys and are more likely to leave school early, as they are often forced into early marriage. Chad has the third highest rate of child marriage in the world: 68 percent of girls are married as children. Education enrollment is higher for boys (75 percent) compared with girls (49 percent). The dropout rate is high through secondary (11 percent to 3.5 percent) and tertiary (1.4 percent to 0.3 percent). Literacy rates among women are less than half that of men (23.2 percent versus 55.7 percent). This prevents women from accessing better climate information, when available and generated by existing CIEWS.

### Employment

33. Unemployment is higher among women – 24.7 percent compared to 18.7 percent for men – and among people with no university diploma or higher qualification. The female labor force participation rate was at 65.9 percent in 2015, compared to a 50.3 percent decrease during the economic crisis, while the male rate steadily decreased from 80.5 percent in 2002 to 77.4 percent in 2015. Women are more burdened with household chores, childcare and – in rural areas - agricultural activities.

### Energy

34. The 2009 EVST report demonstrated that wood is still the main source of fuel for cooking purposes for over 90 percent of households. This proportion is slightly higher for rural areas (93 percent) compared to 75 percent for urban areas. In rural areas, the burden of resource degradation falls on women and girls who are responsible for collecting firewood and water. Natural resource degradation increases the distance that women have to walk to find wood and water, which can be a significant time commitment. Women and girls are also at risk to injuries and respiratory and other health problems (République de Tchad, Document de Politique Nationale Genre de Tchad).

### Policy framework

35. The Ministry of Social Affairs, Gender Equality and Family oversees the implementation of gender policies on the national level. The institutions in this area are:

- Constitution of 1996, revised in 2005: recognizes gender equality
- 1996 Labour Code


---
**Page 172**
---

* National Population Policy and the 1995 Policy for the Integration of Women in Development.
* Law n°38/98 recognizes that men and women should be granted equal employment opportunities.
* Law N°16/PRG2006 promotes the education of girls.
* National Gender Policy (PNG) 2007-2011.

The government's strategy for 2017–2021 emphasizes social protection, the prevention of gender-based violence, economic empowerment and livelihoods, and capacity building to ensure the full implementation of the gender equality (GEEF - Chad country plan).

36. The 2007 National Gender Policy outlines the government's strategy to ensure the gender inequality gap is reduced by half by 2030, and that women are included in decision-making processes and natural resource management. The strategic orientations of this policy are:

* systematically integrate the gender dimension at all levels: planning, budgeting, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of development strategies, policies and programs.
* develop a communication strategy for changing mentalities and behaviours.
* promote equal and equitable access to basic social services and decision-making spheres.

37. The Chad Constitution and laws clearly outlaw gender discrimination. However, traditional and religious practices continue to influence women's lives. A civil code that coexists with the civil code and the French code of 1958. This prevents women from enjoying all their rights. The problem lies primarily in the lack of enforcement of the laws and regulations. Women are not aware of these laws or do not have sufficient knowledge of them, and high illiteracy rates and the persistence of traditional practices also contribute to this situation. Women need access to information about the information they need to defend their rights; that is when they are aware that they exist (République du Tchad).

Mali

Demographics

38. The country of Mali is an agro-sylvio-pastoral land with about 15.6 million inhabitants, of which 51 percent are women. The vast majority of the population (90 percent) lives in rural areas and only 24 percent live in urban areas. Women represent 38% of the national, 86 percent in urban areas. Statistics on total energy consumption in the country is 20.4 kWh per capita.

Development and poverty

39. Mali's HDI value for 2018 is 0.427, positioning it at 154 out of 189 countries and territories. The gender gap index (GGI) is -0.36, significantly lower than the GGI for males, resulting in a Gender Development Index (GDI) value of 0.867. In terms of gender inequality, it is one of the lowest in Africa. Mali ranks last among countries with a GDI below 0.51 out of 158 out of 162 countries in the 2018 index. In Mali, 8.8 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women and 37.4 percent of ministerial positions are occupied by women. The causes and the adolescent birth rate is 169 births per 1,000 girls aged 15-19. (Human Development Report, 2019). The practice of early marriage is still highly prevalent in Mali.

40. Mali ranks 90th of 119 countries on the Global Hunger Index. Over the past five years an average of 3.6 million people (18 percent of the total population) were food-insecure, of


---
**Page 173**
---

which 600,000 were severely food insecure. Food insecurity is more than 50 percent higher among households headed by women than those headed by men. Diversification of food consumption is low. Access to nutritious food is constrained by low incomes and lack of knowledge. Women spend up to three times as much time on tasks as expensive as one that covers energy needs only (WFP, Mali country strategic plan).

### Women and agriculture
41. Malian women play a major role in agricultural production and are responsible for subsistence farming while men participate both in subsistence and commercial agriculture. In terms of the provision of labour, women and men work side-by-side in almost all the stages of crop production. However, women have less access to inputs such as seeds or agricultural products. Men (either fathers or husbands) mediate and control Malian women’s access to land, credit, fertilizers, pesticides, and other inputs. Women’s contributions are often unrecognized. Even when women have access to assets rarely have control over them.

42. Women, 78 percent of whom live in rural areas, are at a disadvantage regarding access to land. They own only about one-third of the total land area used for food production but hold only 10 percent of land use rights and 8 percent of land ownership titles. Women are also heavily involved in unpaid household work. According to the Ministry of Agriculture, women represent more than 75 percent of agricultural workers and 92 percent of total credit allocated.

43. A major determinant of gender disparity is lack of access to land. Most of the land is owned by the government and in the traditional system of land use, men are more likely to have access to land than women. Traditionally, women cannot own land in Mali. They can cultivate only a small plot of land (about 1 ha) that they inherit from their mothers or wives. This discourages women from investing in land improvements. The President of Mali had recently announced the creation of a national land tenure commission for women and youth (ARD Inc., Land Tenure Assessment Report, 2011) and the Profil Genre de la République du Mali (PGRM) report on gender equality in agriculture in 2008-2009 about 20 percent of rural women had access to land, an increase of 3.1 percentage points from 2007 (AEDB, Profil Genre de la République du Mali). Women often form associations and request the government to allocate them land for their use or to provide them with land for their collective use. Although this is a good strategy, it does not assure them that the land will remain theirs.

44. Mali is currently facing significant environmental problems such as desertification, floods and rapid deforestation. Women are more victims of the adverse effects of climate change, but they also play an important role in adapting to these changes. The use of firewood products, the sale of wood and coal have negative impacts on the environment. So, some women’s associations have been created to promote alternative energy sources such as the development of collection pits for processing drying wastewater, development of soap workshops, etc. The national priority is to strengthen efforts to promote the adoption of practices that are more productive, less costly and less energy-consuming for all men and women in rural areas (Mali, BOAD).

### Education
45. Similar to other countries in the region, women and girls in Mali have less access to education than men and boys. The adult literacy rate for women is 25.7 percent, whereas the national average is 38.1 percent. In rural areas, only about one-third of women attend a secondary level of education (7.3 percent) is less than half the percentage for their male counterparts (16.4 percent). The situation in primary education is even worse: only 20 percent of girls are enrolled in primary schools, only 48.3 percent complete the primary school cycle. In some areas of the country less than 50 percent of enrolled children are girls, one of the widest gender gaps in the world. This is unlikely to improve in the short term, as


---
**Page 174**
---

### Employment

46. Women make up 38.4 percent of the economic active population (EAP) in Mali, and of these, three per cent are in the agriculture sector (FAG). However, these figures are misleading because the definition of EAP includes paid labour, and a study by the African Development Bank (AfDB) and the World Bank (WB) in 2013 found that only one out of ten women declared that they have never received any remuneration (AfDB, Profit Genre).

### Energy

47. The national rate of access to electricity in Mali is 41% (17% in rural areas) and the electrification rate remains low with around 19% of households connected to the electricity network. This situation is due to a lack of investment in the sector, which has been poorly exploited, which accentuates the energy deficit as the country is totally dependent on imported petroleum products and wood combustibles from its natural forests. This situation is exacerbated by the fact that Mali's population growth rate (3.5%) is higher than its economic growth rate (2%). Mali’s energy sector remains very vulnerable to climate change due to its high dependence on non-renewable energy sources, which are imported or produced in a country where energy supply falls disproportionately on women.

### Policy framework

48. In 1997 the Ministry for the Promotion of Women, Children and the Family (MPFWE) was created as the main government institution responsible for women's empowerment and gender equality. The National Action Plan for the Promotion of Women and the Equality for Gender Equality, adopted in 2010. This national policy sets the country’s vision, strategy and priorities for strengthening gender equality and equity. It promotes equal rights between men and women by ensuring that women have access to resources. One important component is to improve women's access to appropriate technology and information through the establishment of a Gender Technology and Development Thematic Group (GTiDEG) was established to facilitate dialogue between the government, development agencies and civil society to support the policy’s implementation.

49. In 2009, the government attempted to adopt a new Family Code which was to introduce improvements in women's rights and gender equality. The code was widely supported. However, opposition from conservative groups forced the government to withdraw the code. Two years later, a new code was introduced, resulting in several setbacks in terms of women’s empowerment.

### The Agricultural Orientation Law of 2008

50. The Agricultural Orientation Law of 2008 (Lo d’Orientation Agricole) takes a strong approach to gender equity, food sovereignty and support for small-scale farming. An enforcement study conducted by the Ministry of Agriculture in 2013 found that women farmers surveyed were not even aware of its existence (Minimant and Ta., 2011). The National Plan of Priority Investment in the Sector of Agriculture of Mali (PNPI-SA) 2011-2015, 2011.

51\. In November 2015, the Malian National Assembly adopted a gender quota bill that requires that at least 30 percent of elected or appointed officials be women.

### Mauritania

#### Demographics
- Mauritania is a lower-middle-income and largely arid country with an area of 1.03 million km² and a rapidly growing population of 4.4 million, of which 50.4 per cent are male, and 49.6 per cent female.
- The majority (72%) of the population is under age 25 years old.
- Despite significant progress in reducing child mortality rates, 18% of children still lead nomadic lives and 32 percent of households are headed by women.
- Despite significant progress in reducing child mortality rates, 18% of children still lead nomadic lives and 32 percent of households are headed by women.

#### Development and poverty
- Mauritania is a food-deficit country: cereal production meets only 30 per cent of national demand. Availability is generally not an issue, but markets are vulnerable to global food price fluctuations.
- The country has poor infrastructure with limited access to electricity, water supply, and roads are occasionally cut off in rainy seasons. As a result, food security is a concern.
- On average, 30 per cent of the population lives below the poverty line (28.5 per cent men; 24.3 percent girls, and 27.4 percent boys) are recurrently food insecure.
- In August 2017, 29 percent of households were food insecure in rural areas compared to only 26 percent of male-headed households were food insecure.
- Sixty percent of food-insecure people lived in rural areas. Rural women are disproportionately affected because they have low levels of education and limited access to credit, land tenure rights, and other livelihood capabilities (WFP; 2018).

#### Poverty
- Levels fell from 42 percent in 2006 to 35 per cent in 2014. But there are major discrepancies between region in terms of gender, age and occupational status.
- 74 percent of the poor – largely farmers, pastoralists and landless or unpaid women and youth – live in rural areas.

#### Gender-based violence
- Data on gender-based violence is scarce, but an estimated 55 percent of girls have been subjected to female genital mutilation and the rate is much higher among girls without education. The practice is most prevalent in the poorest quintile (86 percent) and girls in rural areas (99 percent).
- Force-feeding is also still practiced upon 24 per cent of women and girls in rural areas, but the practice is declining.

### Women and agriculture
- Most of Mauritania’s population relies on agriculture: 60 percent are smallholder agropastoralists and 30 percent are landless seasonal workers. Smallholder farmers, agropastoralists and landless seasonal workers are among the most vulnerable to food insecurity.
- Farming is the main activity carried out by rural women, but productivity is still low. Women have limited access to credit, land tenure rights, and other livelihood capabilities; inadequate agricultural practices; and limited access to financing.


---
**Page 176**
---

57. Women’s activity in rural areas is notably concentrated in the low-productivity agricultural sub-sector. They are particularly involved in soil post-preparation (sowing, weeding, harvesting, transportation, storage, processing and harvest conservation) and other tasks (feeding livestock, animal husbandry, etc.). In some countries, women are increasingly responsible for market gardening, which has intensified over the past decades following long periods of drought. Women’s participation is also increasing in the framework of cooperatives. This has helped to significantly improve feeding and the living conditions of the population. Livestock farming accounts for 14 percent of GDP and women are involved in all stages of production: breeding, rearing, milking, etc., as well as providing them with veterinary care, and process and market dairy products. They still practice subsistence agriculture to a large extent, which is their most important source of income that they can personally control (ADEB, Country Strategy Paper 2016-2020).

58. Access to land is a major obstacle to women’s participation in agriculture: 56 percent need the guarantee of a third party to conduct a land ownership transaction; 31 percent are families with no sons and only 18.7 percent of them own land (ADEB, Country Strategy Paper 2016-2020).

59. Although the Constitution and the Land Reform Act guarantee equal rights to own property and land (Constitution, Art. 15; Land Reform Act, 1983), under Sharia law, women and girls are not considered heirs of their father or grandfather. In the case where there is only daughters and no sons, part of the inheritance of the deceased father is first distributed equally among his daughters. The remaining portion goes to the widow of the deceased and his wife. Women face additional challenges in retaining property (both land and non-land assets) after marriage. This is because women are often not considered as full owners; their right to ownership of assets is often limited to objects related to women’s work and daily life, such as livestock or household items. In some cases, it may also be the case that the family of the deceased father is granted full custody of the child (rather than to the living mother), allowing his female sole rights over the child's inheritance. Furthermore, in some countries, women need a male guardian’s authorization for the authorization of a third party, generally her guardian or a male family member (CEDAW, 2014). Women are also often excluded from land ownership and management, which is key source of income. In some places, women are excluded from land ownership altogether because they do not have legal rights to inherit or own land (GI-ESCR, 2014). Social status and ethnicity also play a role in land ownership, making it more difficult for certain groups of women to access land. Women may be treated as either slaves or descendants of slaves, to access lands (Minority Rights Group International, 2016) (CEDAW, 2014).

60. Women have the same financial rights as men equal rights as men to open a bank account, access a line of credit, or register a business (World Bank, 2015). The government developed a National Plan of Action for Rural Women 2009 - 2012, in which it laid out specific measures to improve women’s access to financial services. These include improving infrastructure structures and establish financial tools and financial management training programs. However, women still face significant barriers when accessing formal or informal financial services, including the traditional roles of men as the head of the household, which can prevent women from accessing credit for their own business or other assets that they could use as collateral and their low levels of literacy and education (World Bank, 2015). The percentage of rural women aged between 15 and older with an account at a financial institution or mobile-money-service provider; the percentage is significantly higher for men, at 26.3 percent (UN Women).

Education


---
**Page 177**
---

61. Net enrolment in primary education – 73 percent for girls and 69 percent for boys – has not changed in the past decade. Of the children enrolled, only 35 percent of boys and 30 percent of girls complete the primary cycle. Attendance at school averages 8.3 years among women compared to 12.4 years among men. The literacy rate is estimated at 69.7 percent among women and 55 percent among men. Additionally, women have limited access to adequate information services to make decisions regarding the best adaptation/mitigation technologies and solutions or options for effective risk transfer (climate insurance).

### Employment

Women are particularly disadvantaged as a result of discrimination in access to land, financial services and markets, an uneven burden of unpaid labour, household work and higher levels of poverty. Women face significant barriers when it comes to obtaining remunerative work, particularly in rural areas. An estimated 12.5 percent of children aged 5–6 years – primarily in agriculture – are hence at risk of all the forms of child labour.

### Female participation in the labour market is 29.2 percent compared to 63.2 for men (Human Development Report 2019). Even though the labor code prohibits gender discrimination, women face significant barriers when it comes to obtaining remunerative work, particularly in rural areas. An estimated 12.5 percent of children aged 5–6 years – primarily in agriculture – are hence at risk of all the forms of child labour.

### Energy

40. Very few households in rural areas has access to electricity and therefore, women and girls must still bear the burden of gathering wood for fuel. This in turn impacts negatively on their health and well-being.

### Policy framework

The country is signatory to many conventions on respect for human and women’s rights. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) which was ratified, except for some articles that run counter to the present legal system. The Code on Personal Status adopted in 1980 and amended on 20 July 1991 considers women as citizens in their own right and recognizes gender equality in all areas. The Code on Personal Status adopted in 2001 is the centerpiece of the country’s efforts to ensure respect for human rights, particularly through a series of legislation in Mauritania. It governs all issues relating to the private sphere (family, marriage, divorce, etc.). It also provides for the protection and promotion of women’s progressive character and some advances (marriage age set at 18 years for boys and girls, right to work, right to education, right to own property, right to access professional training for women to continue studies or carry on professional activities, etc.), it is quite revelatory of the country’s efforts in this regard. However, the Code still reflects a certain idea of women being perpetual “minors”. For instance, the Personal Status Code establishes that women are not allowed to make decisions about their own health and decisions on behalf of the family without the consent of the wife (CEDC, Gender Index).


---
**Page 178**
---

### Demographics

As of 2018, Niger’s population was estimated at 21.4 million inhabitants; it is expected to reach 34.9 million in 2030 and more than triple in 2050 to 69 million. Women account for more than half of the population (52-4 percent), and this share has actually increased in the last decade. In 2018, women accounted for 57% of Niger’s total population. Single-headed households made up 16 percent of the households in Niger according to data from the 2013 census. The fertility rate is high: 6.4 children per woman and 9.1 children per 100 women in regions such as Maradi and Dosso, which are among the poorest in the country. Women aged between 15-19 years old contribute significantly to the very high rate of population growth at 3.9 percent. The maternal mortality ratio is also very high: 824 deaths per 100,000 live births and the under-five child mortality rate is 192.0 births per 1,000 women ages 15-19. Preference is given to male children, which stems from cultural practices and social norms that perpetuate gender discrimination. High fertility rates have negative impacts on the time women have to devote to basic family care, leading to impaired health (e.g., better education, health and nutritional status), and higher infant mortality.


---
**Page 179**
---

70. Gender inequality is high in Niger: the country ranked 151 out of 160 countries on the gender inequality index in 2017, with a GII value of 0.494. Its ranking on the OECD Development Centre's Social Indicators and Gender Index (SIGI), which is a cross-country measure of women’s empowerment based on indicators of laws, policies, cultural norms, social norms, and practices) across 180 countries, is similar: Niger ranks 153rd out of 160 with a SIGI value of 0.4415 which is very high. Niger is on a list of 13 Sub-Saharan African countries with a high level of gender discrimination based on the SIGI.

Development and poverty

71. Niger was ranked 189 out of 189 countries on the 2017 Human Development Index (HDI), with an HDI value of 0.456, corresponding to a GNI per capita of $350. The HDI increased from 0.354, corresponding to an increase of 68.5 percent. As for the Gender Development Index (GDI) which measures gender equality in terms of health, education and income, Niger leads therefore to a GDI value of 0.812 (UNDP, 2018 Statistical Update: Niger). These disparities between men and women are reflected by the high levels of poverty, unemployment, mortality, access to assets, sexual violence and early marriage. Women, especially in rural areas, have limited access to productive resources such as land, water, seeds, tools etc., as well as collecting water and firewood, agricultural work, preparing meals or caring for children.

72. Gender inequalities contribute to poverty which disproportionately affects women, girls and children. Niger is one of the poorest countries in the world with a poverty rate of 44.1 percent (UNDP, 2018 Statistical Update: Niger). The regions with highest MPI are Maradi, Dosso and Zinder are the most affected ones. This is also reflected by the Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) which measures the proportion of people in a country who live on less than $1.90 per day. In 2017, Niger’s MPI was 58 percent, meaning that 58 percent of the population is considered poor[1]. The MPI results indicate strong disparities between men and women: women are more likely to be poor (63% compared with 48%) than men. Women also have a higher poverty rate in rural areas (0.72) than urban areas (0.276). Regions with highest MPI are Maradi (0.664), Tahoua (0.591) and Dosso (0.538). The results of the MPI, combined with other factors that have an impact on people’s vulnerability confirm the need to create an enabling environment for financing climate resilient agriculture.

73. In relation to political participation, 28 out of 117 seats in government are held by women; which represents a decrease from 1.2 percent in 1999 to 0.6 percent following the 2016 elections. Only 18 percent of cabinet ministers are women (5 out of 44), which is far below the regional average of 37%. In addition, only one out of every five appointed officials are women, corresponding to a participation rate of 16 percent; the rate is much lower in Maradi and Maroua.

Women and agriculture

74. Women make up more than 70 percent of the Niger’s workforce in the agricultural sector. Generally speaking, women have less access to land and other productive resources than plots managed by men: women in Niger produce 19 percent less per hectare than plots managed by men. The main determinants contributing to gender productivity gap in Niger are the lack of access to land and other productive resources, as well as differences in skills and characteristics, with men owning more land and enjoying higher returns to ownership than women. Women’s limited access to credit, information on agricultural practices, as measured by group membership and public speaking, is also considered as a key element that contributes to the gender productivity gap. The lack of access to credit makes it less likely to impact their resilience. A study on resilience in Niger found that female-headed households are more vulnerable than male-headed ones: 58 percent of female-headed households (one out of three women (36.2 percent) owns land compared to more than half of men (55.3 percent). Due to inequalities in access to productive resources, such as land and financing,

75\. Inheritance of agricultural land in Niger is subject to three categories of law: Islamic, modern and customary law. Not all categories consider women equally. Islamic law recognizes women as heirs but only gives them one-third share while customary laws are against two shares to male heirs. As a consequence, only 3,041 (4 percent) out of the 72,345 of irrigated land identified in Niger in 2004 are owned by women (Niger and the Gambia, 2006).

In Niger, women, especially widows, are the main victims of agricultural related property-grabbing including land, farm implements and inputs. discriminatory attitudes and practices reinforce unequal land tenure system and reinforce existing gender disparities (UN Women, 2018).


### Education

The literacy rate for women aged 15 years old and higher is very low, particularly in rural areas where it was 11 percent in 2014\. Long-standing traditions attach less value to educating girls than boys, as demonstrated by a high proportion of illiterate women compared with men. In 2014, the literacy rate for men aged 15 years and older was 67.8 percent while that for women was only 39.1 percent. These gender disparities are also reflected in inequalities in enrolment and completion of education: the primary Gross Enrolment Ratio (GER) for boys is 102.4 percent compared with 71.5 percent for girls; the secondary GER for boys is 68.1 percent while that for girls is only 71.3 percent for girls. The percentage for girls has increased over the last four years, from 64.8 percent in 2010 to 71.3 percent in 2014\. However, the GER for secondary completion remains low: 56.3 percent for boys and 72.1 percent for girls; the national average is 60.3 percent (UNICEF, 2018). The gender gap in education is particularly pronounced in the regions of Zinder and Tahoua\. This is compounded by the custom of marrying young girls to older men, which results in a high proportion of married women (and therefore older) men from a different climatic zone. Only 4.3 percent of adult women have reached at least secondary education compared with 10.9 percent for their male counterparts. Currently, the low level of education among women is an obstacle to understanding of the importance of climate information in integrated climate risk management.


### Employment

76\. 82.5 percent of women participate in the labour market, compared to 90.7 percent of men. On average active women are employed for fewer hours compared to men (28 hr vs women and 34 hr per week). Women earn less than their male counterparts: the median monthly earnings for women, while men earn more than double the amount: US$1,292\. Women who are paid by piece rate earn even lower wages. The proportion of women in self-employment and the proportion of companies led by women is very low (0.14 percent). The unemployment rate is estimated at 8.6 percent, with 8.2 percent for men against 8.1 percent for women.


79\. There is only a limited amount of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) owned by women. When they do own businesses, they have unmet financial needs and face various barriers to accessing finance. In addition, the majority of women-owned SMEs in rural communities rarely benefit rural women. At the same time, financial institutions also face considerable challenges when it comes to lending to women. The lack of a general understanding of the rural and agricultural sector, the gender dynamics in urban areas, high transaction costs, poor infrastructure, limited small loans, and unclear and unfavourable land tenure and property rights (GCF, Niger).


### Energy

80\. The gendered division of labour within households generates different energy needs. In rural areas, women and girls traditionally bear the main burden of collecting biomass such


---
**Page 181**
---

as charcoal, wood and agriculture waste for fuel. Fuelwood is the main energy source for about 97 percent of households, which has negative effects on women's health and well-being. In 2016, 65.4 percent of urban population has access to energy, while only 4.7 percent of rural population has access to energy. The government plans to increase the number of solar systems will contribute significantly to lower the gap between urban and rural, while helping women and girls by reducing the time spent on collecting heavy fuelwood.

### Policy framework

85. The constitution of Niger guarantees all citizens equal rights regardless of gender and prohibits sex-based discrimination. Despite these guarantees, the reality in rural areas is quite different. Women's access to education, health services, credit facilities and land is governed by a combination of customary and Islamic law (or local interpretations thereof), which often conflicts with socio-cultural, economic/legal and educational barriers affecting women’s access to services.

82. Niger ratified the CEDAW in 1999 and signed the Optional Protocol on Violence against Women in 2003. The country has also adopted a National Action Plan (NAP) for the participation to the management of public affairs. The law 2004-50, the commercial code and the civil code also provide provisions that should contribute to more gender equality.

83. A National Gender Policy was adopted in 2008, along with a decade-long plan (2009–2018). Since then, several sectoral policies have also integrated the promotion of gender equality into their orientations.

### Senegal

#### Demographics

86. Senegal has a population of about 14 million, of which 51 percent is female. More than half of households live in rural areas. The heads of households are mostly men; only 15 percent of households are headed by women. Children, women and the elderly are the most vulnerable groups to food insecurity. In addition to poverty, droughts, floods, climate shocks affect all Senegalese, who are vulnerable to the exhaustion of their food reserves and the loss of livestock. The lack of access to credit, among other factors, has negative effects on their lives and livelihoods; poor households struggle to satisfy their food, education and health needs and expenses.

#### Development and challenges

87. Although Senegal enjoys relative political stability, it faces several development challenges. The country's HDI value for 2016 is 0.534, which places it in the low human development category. In comparison to other countries in West Africa, Senegal’s HDI increased from 0.377 to 0.514, an increase of 36.5 percent. During


---
**Page 182**
---

this period, life expectancy at birth increased by 10.5 years, mean years of schooling increased by 0.9 years and expected years of schooling increased by 4.6 years. Senegal's GNI per capita increased by about 43.9 percent between 1990 and 2018 (Human Development Report, 2019). However, despite these gains in economic indicators, the population lives below the poverty line and 75 percent of households suffer from chronic poverty.

### 88. In 2018, Senegal had a Gender Inequality Index score of 0.523, slightly above the average for sub-Saharan Africa. It ranked 122 out of 162 countries. In Senegal, 41.8 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women, and 11.1 percent of adult women have reached at least secondary education compared to 70.5 percent of men and 39.5 percent of their counterparts. For every 100,000 live births, 315.0 infants die from pregnancy related causes, compared with 248.6 in the sub-Saharan average and 15.0 in high-income countries. Female participation in the labour market is 35.2 percent compared to 58.6 for men (Human Development Report, 2019).

### 89. Chronic malnutrition affects 17 percent of the population, with much higher rates in some regions. Among women of childbearing-age, 31.6 percent are malnourished and 22 percent suffer from chronic energy deficiency. Micronutrient deficiencies (iron, iodine, vitamin A) are also common among children under five years old (40.5 percent), pregnant women (68 percent), and women of reproductive age (73.9 percent). Among children under five (WFP, Senegal country strategic plan 2019-2023).

### Women and agriculture
* Women in rural areas constitute most of the labour force; 70 percent are active in subsistence agriculture, compared with 30 percent of men. Most men farmers produce cash crops on large farms (54 percent), while women grow food crops for their own use or sell them to local markets. In some areas where land is scarce and women have difficulty accessing land titles, they often cultivate small plots of land in the vicinity of their homes.
* At the level of the crop production sub-sector, in 2015, the majority of women own small areas of about 0.4 ha, while men own areas of about 1.3 ha. Men own 93.6 percent of the cultivated land for cash crops and 87.5 percent of the cultivated land for food crops.
* Women are more present in traditional poultry and small ruminant farming. They are also involved in watering and feeding livestock, and are responsible for the collection, processing, and sale of animal products (milk, eggs). In addition to their role as primary generators of income and jobs, women are mainly involved as tanners. Women are also involved in the production of honey.

### 92. With regard to livestock farming, men mainly look after cattle, sheep, and goats, while women are more present in traditional poultry and small ruminant farming.
* Women are legally entitled to acquire and own land independently of their husband or male relatives (Article 308 of the Civil Code). However, in practice, women have limited access to land. Yet, as they are legally the head of the household, they have greater access to agricultural inputs and more control over the proceeds from sales.
* In Senegal, there is no system of marital property; any property assets given to the woman upon marriage must be handed over to her husband who is tasked with managing them during marriage. More generally, land is allocated through local administrative processes in Senegal that do not


---
**Page 183**
---

officially recognize land sales or bequests. Instead, land belongs to the national domain, and local land committees grant land use rights. Until the 2001 Constitution, women were not allowed to own land in Senegal, nor is it part of the land committees, and as such rarely have access to this allocation.

94. Monogamy, despite its limitations, including land inheritance—are common, and ownership is primarily obtained through patrilineal lineage. Several inheritance laws coexist in Senegal. While the French-inspired system of inheritance grants greater equality in the division of property among children, the Islamic-influenced system, which prevails under the customary system, the latter prevails and very few women (approximately 4 percent) have any land to transfer or inherit. The 2015 Constitution has introduced a new law that modifies some practices relating to land ownership that discriminate against women are specifically banning inheritance laws that favor male heirs over female heirs. However, in some rural areas, village chiefs assign land under customary law, and women rarely benefit from this allocation. In addition, the lack of formal documentation for women’s possession of land for their own use, but in polygamous marriages, this may result in each wife receiving only a small portion of her husband's property. Studies by DAPSA found that only 10 percent of the total ownership titles belong to women.

95. In many cases, women are beneficiaries of only a small portion of land. Institutions such as rural councils for land attribution grant women a very small portion of exploitable land—usually less than one hectare. The lack of access to and control of exploitable land means that women resort to strategies and alternatives to secure their livelihoods. For example, in the Casamance region, land is reserved to small-scale women farmers such as rice cultivation, vegetable gardens and only rarely for cash crops. Women who are not married or widowed and who are associated with little financial knowledge and difficulty in building credit exclude women from accessing credit (WFP, GCF).

Education

96. The percentages of women with some secondary education and employed women are below the regional average. Literacy rates are 66 percent for men and 40 percent for women. In addition to the lack of access to education, other factors such as traditional and religious practices such as early and forced marriage prevail, leading to girls dropping out of school. This is compounded by gender-based violence and gender inequality, which contribute to widening the hunger gap (WFP). Senegal country strategic plan 2016-2025.

97. Although gender parity has been achieved in terms of girls in primary education (for every 100 boys enrolled, there are approximately 104 girls), as the level of education increases girls drop out at a higher rate than boys. This is due to several factors, including delays in the transition from primary to secondary, but also within secondary education. In 2009, girls' enrollment in secondary education was 27 percent and gender parity was still far from being achieved (UNESCO Global Partnership for Girls’ and Women’s Education – One Year On; Senegal).

Employment

98. The share of female labour force in total labour force has been increasing over the past two decades at a faster pace in Senegal than in the rest of sub-Saharan Africa. The growth was particularly strong between 2013 and 2015, when it increased by almost one percentage point. Female labor force participation rate jumped from 35 percent to 38 percent, and the female-to-male employment ratio rose from 47 percent in 2013 to 56 percent in 2015. However, women's participation rates in the Senegalese job market remain below sub-Saharan Africa’s average (Mata, Martinez and Tavares, MP WP/19/241, 2019).


---
**Page 184**
---

Energy

99. While Senegal has one of the highest national electricity rates in the region, access in rural areas is still limited to around 40 percent of households. This means that though access to alternative energy sources such as solar power and biogas is increasing, many people continue to rely on energy sources such as wood-burning fires for cooking, lighting, warmth and other needs. The burden of gathering wood for fuel traditionally falls on women and girls.

Policy framework

100. Senegal ratified CEDAW in 1985, and the Optional Protocol on violence against women in 2000. Yet, the country has not reported to the CEDAW committee since 1994. Senegal is a signatory of the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995) and the Convention on the Rights of Women in Africa in 2005. The government also adopted a National Gender Equity and Equality Strategy (SNEGEE) in 2016, which was updated to SNEGEE 2 in 2018 (SNEGEE 2 is the 4th reference framework for the period of 2017-2023). The SNEGEE 2 also supports the institutionalisation of gender (setting up of gender units, audits and gender action plans).

101. The Ministry of Women, Family and Gender, through its Gender Equity and Equality Directorate, is to support the ministries in charge of vegetable, livestock, fish and forest production by providing capacity-building and cooperatives' participation in the development of policies and programmes. The SNEGEE 2 also supports the institutionalisation of gender (setting up of gender units, audits and gender action plans).

102. In terms of results, there have been improvements at the institutional level on macro and sectoral indicators and the Government of Senegal has made advances in mainstreaming gender into its policies.

The Gambia

Demographics

104. The population of The Gambia is estimated at 1.9 million, with an annual growth rate of 3.3 percent (Globoc) and an average household size of about 8.3. About 50 percent of the population lives in urban areas. In rural areas, women are more likely to be heads of households than men; this is because the majority of the population is headed by a female, mainly because of the migration of males to urban areas and overseas. Women's poverty is closely linked to their high literacy level (73 percent), the absence of land ownership, lack of access to credit and financial services, lack of access to microfinance credit, land ownership, skills and support services. About 67 percent of the population is aged between 15-24 years old. The youth are more vulnerable than other age groups because they are more vulnerable, such as poverty, power relations, lack of economic power, low levels of education, and lack of limited technological knowledge, are also key issues affecting youths.

Development and power

105. In 2018, Gambia's value on the Human Development Index was 0.466, improving at a rate of 174 out of 189 countries in the index for 2018. The country has improved its ranking by 150* out of 192 countries in the 2018 index. In Gambia, 10.3 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women. For every 100,000 live births, 760 women die from pregnancy related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 70.2 births per 1,000 women of ages 15-


---
**Page 185**
---

19\. Female participation in the labour market is 51.7 percent compared to 67.7 for men (Human Development Report, 2019).

### Women and agriculture

- **Women** comprise 78 percent of the economically active population who work in agricultural production, compared with 34 percent of men.
- The majority of women farmers are unskilled agrarian wage earners and are responsible for about 40 percent of the total agricultural production in the country. Even though there are more women in agricultural production than men, they have limited access to land, inputs, credit and technology as well as other resources that would improve their social status.
- Most are involved in the production of non-cash crops such as rice, millet, sorghum, yams, cassava, groundnuts, etc. They also engage in livestock rearing and ownership of productive resources such as land, inputs, credit and technology, as well as access to markets for their produce.
- Although women farmers are able to generate relatively good revenues, this income is often invested in sustaining the household. Their limited access to financial services, lack of education and training, poor infrastructure, lack of ownership and control over resources such as land and modern agricultural equipment, among others, continue to limit them from generating enough revenue that would graduate into the mainstream livelihood economy (FAO, National gender profile of agriculture and rural livelihoods – The Gambia).

### In The Gambia

- Crops are grown according to gendered division of labour: men primarily grow sorghum, millet and groundnuts in the highlands using mechanized methods. Women primarily grow lowland rice, the main staple food in the country and one of the most important crops for women farmers.
- In the livestock sector, women are responsible for keeping poultry and small ruminants such as goats and sheep. They also deal with cattle for reasons of prestige. In the fisheries sector, men are responsible for the actual fishing while women process fish into various forms including smoked, salted, fresh and smoked or dried. This applies to both the artisanal and the industrial sector.
- This gender division is slowly breaking down with women moving into groundnut production and rice farming in increasing numbers. Women have also taken over the cultivation of rice varieties to a point of dominance in upland farming systems. However, the bulk of certified rice seed continues to be produced by men while most of the land for rice production and managed by women.
- Women also provide labour on male-controlled irrigated rice (FAO, National Gender Profile).

### A study commissioned by Action Aid found that in all the communities studied, land is controlled by men with women having limited access in most cases, except for land that women have inherited. In some areas of The Gambia, particularly in the North Bank Region and though some do inherit land others are beginning to purchase it outright in West Coast Region. Their landownership is very reduced: only 1 percent own land alone, 15 percent own land jointly with a male relative and 84 percent have no land at all.
- Women also have fewer livestock and a greater overall workload that includes fetching water and fuel wood, cooking, cleaning, childcare and other domestic chores. They are also responsible for providing services, technology and financial services. Access to market, storage and processing technologies remain poor and hamper value chain development (FAO, National Gender Profile).

### Conflicts sometimes arise when men have attempted to reclaim the secondary right after women have inherited land or purchased it outright in West Coast Region. It is therefore vital for the programme to take measures to ensure that women keep control of assets and production through consistent and sustainable capacity development support.


---
**Page 186**
---

One way of doing this is through Functional Literacy Programmes, Farmer Field Schools and tailored entrepreneurship training.

110. It has also been observed when the economic value of women-dominated agricultural commodities increases; men tend to be attracted in producing them and eventually compete with women for access to markets and other resources. This trend is noticeable in the cultivation of rice and horticultural products in The Gambia as men are gradually expanding into their production (FAO, National Gender Profile).

Education

110. Gambian young women lag behind the young men with an illiteracy rate that hovers around 20-30 percent. According to the Human Development Report 2019, 30.7 percent of all adults in The Gambia are literate compared to 45.8 percent for males and only 16.5 percent of their male counterparts. Access to climate information to orient the choices about adopting techniques and technologies is very limited. The country still lacks reliable CIEWS.

Employment

112. Women account for around 50 percent of the total labour force in the country and 70 percent of unskilled labours. 42 percent of female employment is in agriculture against 22 percent of male employment. 84.6 percent of women are considered in vulnerable employment compared to only 39.1 percent for men. Women also work as domestic workers against 29 percent of male. However, The Gambia has achieved important progress on gender parity in education and employment. In 2015, the female labour force participation rate was at 48.6 percent, up from 37.5 percent in 2005 to 73 percent in 2016, higher than 80 percent males in 1999 dropping to 68 percent in 2016 (FAO, National Gender Profile).

Energy

113. In 2016, 76 percent of the urban population had access to electricity, whereas only 34.4 percent of rural population had such access. This means that rural households rely on other energy sources such as fuel wood. Rural women still spend a significant amount of time gathering fodder.

Policy framework

114. The Government has taken concrete policy decisions and actions related to gender and women in the past two decades. In order to better manage the affairs of women and gender issues, the Government established the Gender Working Group (GWG) which replaced the Ministry of Women's Affairs in 1996 and created the Federation of Gambian Women (FGW). The GWG was responsible for formulating policies on gender equality and developed and implemented a number of national policies to address gender issues in the country. These include the 2008-2017 National Gender Equality Strategy (NGES) for women, which is the 1999-2009 National Women's Policy (NAGW). Currently, the 2010-2020 Gender and Women Empowerment Policy (GWEP) is being implemented together with the National Action Plan on Violence against Women and Children (NAPVAC) (2015).

The GWEP was developed through a consultative process informed by a series of consultations held in 2009. The GWEP was approved by the Cabinet, the Ministry of Women's Bureau. Furthermore, to put gender equality and women empowerment on a sound legal footing, the Government has enacted several laws such as the Domestic Violence Act 2013 and Women's Amendment Act 2012. These laws have domesticated international best practices instruments such as the CEDAW (FAO, National Gender Profile).

115. A Ministry for Women's Affairs responsible for providing policy guidance to the Government and stakeholders on gender issues and women was created in 1996 under the OVG who is the Minister. Gender focal points (GFPs) have been established in all line


---
**Page 187**
---

116. Even though the 2009 Women Act stipulates that every woman should have the “right to acquire, own, manage and dispose of the property freely without restrictions”, land ownership in The Gambia still traditionally favours men. Customary biases often mean that women do not exercise their land rights. This is compounded by a lack of awareness about the law, limited access to formal social norms. Management systems are weak; resources to address gender bias are extremely limited. There is a need for more research and data collection on land tenure for women. A shift is needed in the thinking, attitudes and understanding of men and women, as well as government officials.

117. The Gambia National Agricultural Investment Plan (GNAIP), the Gambian Sustainable Land Management Investment Framework 2016-2020 (GSAMIF) and the National Rice Development Project (NRDP) are all underpinned by the IFAD-funded project, which is underway, exemplified by higher levels of poverty among female-headed households. It is estimated that such households represent 18 percent of those in rural areas; 63 percent of these women are landless or have no access to land. Women’s rights to land are often ignored and they risk losing borrowed land. This is a particular problem for multi-year investments.

Stakeholder consultations

118. It should be noted that stakeholder consultations for this programme were conducted in the field missions of IFAD twelve projects between 2016 and 2019 in the 7 countries targeted locations for this programme (see annex list). The design of these programmes is an important element to ensure their success, sustainability and implementation. All stakeholders including women met during the meetings with sectors ministries and field visits (surveys, focus groups). The information obtained through these consultations was used as a basis for the IFAD targeting approach and for identifying priorities for interventions and for the design of programme activities. While some of this information is included in the annex list, it should be noted that the analysis was complimented by stakeholder consultations from in the identified project regions. These consultations were conducted during the same field missions as part of the development of the Gender Action Plan. Consultations will be conducted through the same IFAD targeting approach and for identifying priorities for interventions and for the design of programme activities, including the different roles, interests and priorities of women and men in the implementation areas, and later on in the project regions. This analysis will include the names of the people including women consulted. It will be submitted to the GCF no later than 6 months after the startup of the program.

117. For each country, a detailed gender analyses informed by stakeholder consultations and engagement will be conducted through the same IFAD targeting approach and for identifying priorities for interventions and for the design of programme activities, including the different roles, interests and priorities of women and other traditionally marginalized groups in relation to the objectives of the project. The analysis will include the names of the people including women consulted. It will be completed no later than 6 months after the startup of the program and submitted to the GCF no later than 12 months from the start. This analysis will include the names of the people consulted including women.

118. The gender analyses informed by stakeholder consultations and engagement to identify the needs and priorities of women and other traditionally marginalized groups will be a preliminary action that will be carried out in all of the program's intervention areas. These analyses will be submitted to the GCF for approval 6 months before implementation. This evaluation will also serve as an input into the selection process for the sites where the program’s start-up, subject to the identification of the sites. The Gender Focal Points (GFPs) and UN Women will be called upon to help implement the actions that will be proposed.

This includes investment gender plan and budget to complement and address any potential gap.
