117. Some of the main issues and recommendations collected and reported by the IFAD team and that need to be addressed in the design of this GCF programme are:

- All countries: Rural communities including women recognised the lack of quantitative data on climate change vulnerability, adaptation strategies and improving adaptation practices and mitigation measures on forestry, land use and energy. The IFAD team identified several examples of innovative approaches (EFS) as examples of innovative disaster risk finance mechanisms. Currently, women participate in the production of bioenergy through collection of firewood for combustion and adoption the most appropriate adaptation and mitigation practices/technologies to respond to climate change impacts (e.g., improved technologies for milling and processing; choice of the most suitable agroforestry practices and energy strategies; better access). Hence, their understanding of climate risks to better manage climate shocks and improve resilience. The IFAD team recommended that GCF should leverage climate equipment available in the countries and providing training to climate and meteorological services on gender analysis.

- Burkina Faso, Mali, Chad, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal and The Gambia: The IFAD team identified women’s need to produce their own compost for market gardening, which is a key activity in these countries. Women are also involved in activities including women as the main beneficiaries of the promotion of bioenergy, as it will help reduce the time they devote to the collection of wood, help improve sanitary conditions and create opportunities for them to participate in various training sessions (functional literacy, nutritional education).


---
**Page 190**
---

# Mauritania and Niger: observations from the consultations including the discussion with women and women groups highlighted the lack of funding for infrastructure for access to drinking water. Improving their access by providing villages with boreholes will contribute significantly to improving their livelihoods, which should be addressed as a priority. Component 2 of this GCF programme addresses this issue by providing activities that focus on increasing irrigation coverage and use through climate-resilient irrigation schemes, drip technologies powered by renewable energy sources, together with the consequences of drought and extreme heat events". Furthermore, 175 water points will be installed in villages where women are present. The project will also provide training according to the issues raised during the stakeholder consultations. Other important recommendations include: increasing the number of women involved in decision-making on herd rehousing to contribute to improve soil fertility through organic manure and hajj, improving access to credit for women, providing technical assistance to women engaged in fishing activities around ponds and supporting producers with improved seeds for produce.

# The Gambia: observations from the consultations with women associations and women, led by women with current farming experience in the country and its field missions led us recommend on a range of issues related to gender equality and support women's involvement, including:

The project should include women with proven capacity in working with women, including the use of female facilitators, if required.

During the implementation phase of the project and in subsequent village meetings, separate sessions should be held with women to ascertain their opinions on the project.

On a demand-driven basis, women should be given preferential access to appropriate credit facilities.

Gender mainstreaming responsibilities should be integrated into the terms of reference for all project staff.

The programme's M&E and knowledge management systems should be gender- and age-disaggregated to facilitate the identification of lessons learnt on how to support women in their livelihoods.

118. These recommendations were all taken in full consideration while developing the concept of this GCF programme and will be addressed in its activities throughout all components as outlined in the Gender Action Plan below.

121 Special attention will be given to women led MSMEs Farmers organizations with women membership defined as Women led MSMEs are:
- A formally registered agribusiness enterprise of which more than 51% of the firm's assets or shares are owned by women. This will be assessed and validated based on firm management structure and ownership.
- Women represent at least 30% of the Board of Directors or in senior management position
- Minimum firm size of three employees, of which more than 60% are women
- Activities that are mainly carried out along the agricultural value chain Track record of loan repayment
- Operating for a minimum period of more than two years Records and bookkeeping up to date
- Agriculture land holding of between two and ten ha Any other criteria deemed necessary by the Financial Institutions

Farmer Organizations (FOs)
These organizations shall have a (a) defined membership; (b) purpose for assembling; and (c) an organizational structure established to support members in pursuing individual and collective interests.
They may differ in their: (i) scale characteristics of memberships (e.g. national federations to local associations); (ii) function policy advocacy (e.g. policy, advocacy, economic, technical services); (iii) membership type (e.g. voluntary, paid, elected, unpaid, registered and clustered groups, associations, among others). In the Host Country and the target countries, FOs are often seen as key forces for improving bargaining and negotiation power particularly price achieving greater market scale in operations, improving quality control and better access to finance and market.

Definition of cooperative
An association or association of people who come together voluntarily to meet common economic, social and cultural aspirations and needs within a collective enterprise where power is exercised democratically. For this project, it is an enterprise that provides services to its members and/or the community in order to meet their needs and interests of its members and their communities. Agricultural cooperatives allow their members to create new economic opportunities by offering them a whole range of services in the agricultural sector


---
**Page 192**
---

The criteria for selecting farmer-based organizations, MSMEs, cooperative will be mainly based on the number of women into these groups and memberships as well as their participation in the board of directors.

Key barriers common to all target countries identified during field missions:

122 The country assessments above indicate a series of common factors that must be taken into account in the programme's design and implementation to ensure that it is effective in building resilience. These are grouped under five main headings, which represent barriers to women’s access to micro insurance and financial services:
- Gender-based roles and responsibilities: Women bear most of the daily life in the rural areas. Women are expected to assume full responsibility for household work and care for the family. This leaves little time for income-generating activities or other productive economic activities outside the home. Only a small percentage of women have paying jobs or that can generate enough income to support their families. Women also bear most of the child-rearing, family labour, technologies and crops that bring higher returns.
- Property rights: Women are often excluded from owning land, livestock, tools and practices; only a small minority of women own land and few have adequate and stable access to land and agricultural inputs. The lack of assets in their names is an important impediment to developing creditworthiness and securing loans. In many countries, women’s property rights are collateral. This remains a major challenge and prevents financial institutions from providing them with microfinance.
- Lack or awareness of financing opportunities and new instruments like agricultural insurance: Women have limited access to information on the financial options available to them with the financial institutions and men control resources such as land, livestock, tools and practices. Women are also constrained by cultural norms. Women have no understanding of climate risk transfer (agricultural insurance) that can strengthen their resilience to climate shocks depending on its intensity and magnitude.
- Lack of access to knowledge particularly reliable climate information: when dealing with climate change, women need to be provided with a wide range of relevant, science-based information to inform decision making, enabling EWS and creating opportunities for adaptation. Women also need to have access to reliable weather information for decision making processes in agriculture.
- Lack of education and literacy: Women are generally less educated, lower educational, literacy levels than men, and natality rates are high. They also lack access to knowledge and information on financial services.
- Biased perception of the financial sector: banks and MFIs consider smallholder agriculture as a high-risk sector and women are not attractive to banking. In situations where women have no collateral or assets in their names, they cannot borrow money.
- Many women are subjected to various forms of gender-based violence: domestic violence, female genital mutilation, child marriage and force-feeding.

123 IFAD is recognized for addressing gender inequalities in rural areas. A recent report by our Independent Office of Evaluation showed that IFAD has been successful in addressing gender inequality through its strategy on the ground in 40 countries, including in the countries where this initiative will be implemented. The programme will seek to address gender equality and women’s empowerment within a socio-economic context on which the gender strategy is based. The strategy to address these barriers includes, amongst others, multiple and complementary activities promoting women’s access to financial services, reducing the burden of women's workloads, support to women's organizations, working with men as critical


---
**Page 193**
---

they are often the gatekeepers of customary practices that limit women’s access to resources or public spaces, highly participatory approaches and cultural sensitivity.

### 124. In terms of political and insecurity risks, countries facing conflicts are Mali, Niger, Burkina and coup (Mali). The sites selected for the programme are secured sites where there is no risk of conflict or insecurity. Security measures will be put in place to ensure the safety of the intervention sites that will be taken into account during the evaluation mission. Additionally in IFAD projects, gender is systematically addressed. Community-based organizations and women’s groups will be involved in addressing other issues in the program. The program will build on existing frameworks, drawing on the approaches used by other IFAD projects to address gender equality and empower women in the area and those of other UN agencies, such as UN Women. This approach will be coupled with a focus on strengthening social cohesion and reducing tensions between men and women and avoid opposing them. In each of the program countries, experiences already exist and will serve as a model for programme interventions

Programme Interventions to address the key barriers

125. The programme’s main overall objective is to increase resilience and enhance the livelihoods and food and water security of poor smallholder farmers and rural communities particularly women and youth (15 percent) through integrated climate change adaptation, mitigation and sustainable land management approaches in seven countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW). It also seeks to enable these countries and local communities to adopt a more balanced approach towards agriculture by promoting agricultural development by promoting, upgrading and scaling up management measures that reduce greenhouse gas emissions while increasing mitigation co-benefits including the avoidance of 21 466 499 tCO2eq GHG emissions.

126. The main targeted products are key staple crops (maize, millet, maize, sorghum and groundnuts), livestock (dairy and beef, sheep, goats and chickens) and non-timber forest products (NTPs). The programme will benefit 350,784 smallholder farmers, 262 households and indirectly over 5,322,754 beneficiaries (see table for 64 disaggregated numbers). The programme is expected to have a significant impact on women, especially particularly on women and youth, as they are the most vulnerable people to climate change.

127. The programme will contribute to address these harms by: (i) strengthening women’s economic empowerment by improving their access to resources and opportunities for income generation; (ii) increasing women’s participation in agricultural training and income generating activities); (iii) strengthening women’s voice and influence (increasing their representation and voice in producers’ organisations and local governance structures); and (iv) promoting gender equality through more balanced workloads (promoting labour and time-saving technologies and practices, infrastructure work to improve access to markets and water). These are

128. For generation of employment, various activities were proposed will alternatively scale up income generating activities models though solar energy. Women typically carry the brunt of unpaid work which involves water and fuel collection, cooking, childcare and eldercare as well as agricultural production and caregiving. This is particularly true in rural context with limited basic services and labour saving technologies. Small-scale affordable power supplies, using renewable energy sources could benefit women and girls in many ways. It would decrease their

**Page 194**

### 129.

To ensure the adoption of the proposed innovations some key considerations will be taken: (1) the technology promoted needs to be effective; that is being reliable and generating measurable benefits of value to the women and their households; (2) the technology needs to be affordable for the women; (3) the technology needs to be appropriate for daily life and should be designed in collaboration with the women themselves, taking into account their specific needs and preferences; (4) linking the technology to potential income-generating activities could help increase its adoption rate. This would also reduce the time burden of fuelwood collection which decreases for women and girls, leading to positive outcomes on their education, health, nutrition, and overall economic security and well-being. Women can reallocate their time to leisure or economic opportunities by developing micro and small enterprises, diversifying their incomes. The same benefits in time and money could be achieved through the use of solar cookers for their education.

### 130.

In addition to targeting unpaid domestic work, the programme will also target local women's agricultural groups adopting labour-saving and cost-competitive technologies for developing vegetable gardens. This can make critical differences in a context where women are often excluded from accessing land and water resources as they struggle to access land with good soil quality and close to water sources. Within these groups, women will be encouraged to use solar cookers and other energy-efficient facilities, as well as childcare facilities powered through the solar system. Therefore empowering women by providing them with increasing access to productive assets is essential to help improve crops yields, increase vegetable intake and nutritional outcomes of households, and freeing up women's time in other productive and economic activities. This will also contribute to improving their resilience against climate change while being improved. Facilitating women's participation – in design, planning, implementation and evaluation stages of the programme – and ensuring that, as far as is locally acceptable – is essential to ensure that they have real choices and that their needs are met. IFAD targeted regions are characterized by these factors, the programme will be implemented in such regions and therefore the proposed activities will also address GBV. The use of household methodology to address the underlying causes of discrimination and violence against women is a key element of the project at household and community level. Consultation involving both men and women during the design phase of the project has been carried out, with the result that the project intervention is increasing women's economic independence and their bargaining power as well. The various form of violence are : Domestic violence, Sexual violence, Forced marriage, Female Genital Mutilation/Cutting (FGMC), Child, early and forced marriage, Domestic violence, Violence against children, Gender-based violence, Human Trafficking, Violence against women and girls. It lies on the Cultural beliefs, norms and traditions.

Furthermore, within women groups involved in vegetable gardens and sustainable forest management, one key activity is the launching of a domestic violence awareness campaign. To ensure that this training has a greater impact, this training will be combined with sensitization programs at the community level (through conferences, TV panel discussion, film projections, etc.) involving men and women from different age groups. The programme will engage such programs. Partnership with NGOs and other stockholders to address these sensitization activities.

134\. Female lead households may be subject to less access resources such as land, credit, to information on technologies and reduced engagement within women's group. To ensure that the project has a greater impact in terms of gender equality it will be important to strengthen the understanding of issues related to female headed households and their specific needs. The programme will address this situation and needs related to renewable energy and climate resilient adaptation measures. Based on the best way the programme could involve female led households in the activities.

135\. With regard to property rights and control over assets, the interventions will focused on the establishment of modern communal poultry farms for youth and women, with a focus on the development of a Community-based Agri-Pastoral/Farmer Field Schools (APFSS) and nurseries proposed under component 2 which will be implemented in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture. The programme will promote the dialogue and empower women to control over these resources.

136\. Concerning the lack of awareness of financing opportunities and new instruments like agricultural insurance schemes, the programme has a series of activities presented under the gender action plan which aims at improving awareness raising on financial services for women and men, including access to climate informations; and indirectly better access to credit. Activities planned are organized around three main areas: (i) Awareness raising on climate risk management of which 40% women. Increased access to micro climate insurance schemes, (ii) Access to credit facilities for smallholder, targets women and include Gender dimension in the formation of national agricultural insurance schemes, (iii) Establishment of local contingencies plans for the macro insurance. To facilitate this work, the programme will support the inclusion of women as part of each of the country RIFCs and the RCU.

137\. To address the access to knowledge particularly reliable climate information’s various training will be organized under this programme. To overcome the lack of education and literacy, information and awareness-raising activities will be carried out through extension agents and other means such as radio, television, mobile information and services. Additionally extension agents will provide training and technical assistance in financial literacy and business management to women groups address through the proposed approach. The programme foreseees will develop and deliver modules on financial literacy, which will especially target women. Though a better understanding of financial products is expected to reduce the perception of risk associated with financial products and links to credit products, the biased perception of women seen as risky will be tackled.

Based on the programme performance, necessary adjustment will be provided during the regular IFAD supervisions mission and during the mid term review.

# Gender Action Plan

In the seven (7) Sahelian countries in this programme, women play a fundamental role in agriculture and therefore any effort to strengthen the climate resilience in this sector must take measures to ensure that the support and benefits offered are gender-sensitive.

The gender action plan for the Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme: Building the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change impacts in 7 Sahelian Countries, is designed to address these issues. The programme has set a quota of 40% of the activities under each of the component to be allocated to women. As women play a major role in environmental and climate change issues such as household solid waste management, water harvesting and use of renewable energy sources, they are often affected by the lack of access to basic services. The programme's interventions will help reduce the time spent on these activities and allow more time for income-generating activities.

The gender action plan summarizes the programme interventions identified to address the gender gap in each of the selected country.

Outcome: Women smallholder farmers' resilience and adaptive capacity to climate change is strengthened.

Annex 9 – Baseline Stakeholder Consultations conducted in all countries between 2018 and 2019

Burkina Faso

Among the IFAD program team, the mission was composed of : Mr. Abdoul Barry, IFAD Portfolio Officer for Côte d'Ivoire and Burkina Faso, Head of Mission; Mr. Samir Bejaoui, Program Officer, IFAD / WCA; Mr. Ludovic Condérande, Program Support Officer, IFAD / WCA; Ms. Karine Désilets, Gender Specialist, IFAD / WCA; Mr. Jean-Pierre Faye, Agricultural Engineer, IFAD / WCA; Mr. Jean-François Gueye, Agricultural Engineer, IFAD / WCA; Ms. Sonia Andrianirina, Targeting, Gender and Monitoring & Evaluation Specialist, FAO / TCA; Mr. Mathieu Fajoux, Expert in value chains, FAO / TCA consultant; Ms. Garance Kalondo, Agromonitor, specialist in environmental and social assessment, FAO / TCA consultant; Mr. Jean-Pierre Kaba, Agricultural Engineer, IFAD / WCA; Mr. Jean-Pascal Kabore, Institutional Specialist, FIDA / WCA; Mr. Maladjo Barry, Rural Infrastructure Expert, FAO / TCA Consultant; Ms. Marie-Claire Bédié, Financial and Economic Analysis Economist, IFAD / WCA; Ms. Christelle Ketting, Public-Private Partnerships Specialist, IFAD / PPA; Ms. Mylene Kherallah, Lead advisor, FIDA / PTA. The Government was represented by Mr. Tourié Adama, Director of Policy Formulation at the


---
**Page 209**
---

General Directorate of Sector Studies and Statistics (DGESS) of the MAAL assisted by his close collaborators and Mr. Dav Compaoré Agnès, Program Officer at the General Directorate of Cooperation (DGCOOP), Ministry of the Economy, Finance and Development. The consultations which spent 3 weeks in the field raised the issues that the incidence of poverty in Burkina Faso is high. Indeed, in 2014, the proportion of the poor in the population at the national level was estimated at 40.1 percent with a depth of poverty of 97 percent and a severity of poverty of 3.3 percent. The disparities between regions are considerable: the incidence of poverty is 58.6 percent against 25.5 percent for the severity of poverty of 47.5 percent against 13.7 percent in urban areas. On the other hand, the disparities between the thirteen regions are considerable (Boucle du Mouhoun and Kourweogo regions have an incidence of a population living below the poverty line at 59.7 percent while the provinces of Yatenga and Ziro are extremely poor with incidences of poverty of 34 percent and 22 percent respectively). These populations are also exposed to climatic hazards, drought, rainfall deficit, bush fires) and agricultural risks (damage caused by animals or birds, livestock mortality, plant diseases). Local communities including community leaders in the targeted regions around focus groups and meetings to discuss needs. The consultations were held at the same time as the meetings of sector ministries and field visits (surveys, focus group). The information obtained on gender issues and equalities through these consultations in such country and the targeted sites provided important input for identifying priorities for interventions and for the design of programme activities.

The multidisciplinary national team was composed of: Mr. Koke Wadjet from the MPEFA, Mr. Kampele Ntoum from the MEF, Mr. Abubakar Tchoukoua from the MEF, Mr. Alain Aboukroum from CNPCRT and Ms. Mariam Titimbaye from CELIAF. The consultations with local communities particularly women raised the issues that Chad is a fragile country marked by poverty, food insecurity and high levels of malnutrition. These are exacerbated by climate change, which is linked with environmental and climatic risks following recent droughts, combined with inefficient traditional agricultural practices and less resilient to climate change. Women in particular have limited access to land and resources for agriculture, knowledge and do not participate to decision-making processes. The information obtained on gender issues and equalities through these consultations in the targeted sites provided important input for identifying priorities for interventions and for the design of programme activities.

February 4-8 M. Jonathan Apes, Lead Regional Technical Specialist for Rural Finance, Markets, Enterprises and Value Chains, IFAD/WCA, joined the mission from 27 January - February. A national project preparation team comprised M. Moumoudou L. Gassama, Coordinator of NEMA; M. Abdoulie Touray, M&E Officer, CPCU; M. Bakary Jamneh, Knowledge Management Officer, NEMA; M. Keita Manke, Water Management Specialist, NEMA; M. Saikou Sanyang, Director General, Diak. The mission met local communities including community leaders in the region of Kankan and Boke. The team visited several villages to assess the impact of climate change on agriculture in one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change because of its geographical location, characterised by high dependence on rain-fed agriculture and severe sub-seasonal variability and frequent floods. Consultations were organized with women organizations and key gender issues were compiled and used to inform the gender analysis component plan.
